One partner for the whole end of line, from product to pallet
The end of the line starts at the product. The product goes into the case, and the case goes onto the pallet, so
TRF
plans the whole journey as one system.
End-of-line problems often come from the gaps between machines, not from a single machine: a case packer that outruns the conveyor, an accumulation zone that cannot absorb a pallet change, or three suppliers who each point to another when the line stops.
TRF
addresses those gaps by delivering
case packing
,
conveying
and
robotic palletizing
as one engineered system: one project, one engineering team and one partner for the scope
TRF
delivers.
TRF
manages engineering, manufacturing, controls, assembly, integration and commissioning within one coordinated delivery model. The plant can also start with a single platform, such as a
TRF
Cobot
replacing manual palletizing on one line, and add a case packer and conveyors later without starting over with a new supplier or a new controls philosophy.

What problems does end-of-line automation solve?
End-of-line automation takes the most repetitive and hardest-to-staff work off the end of the line: manual palletizing, manual case packing and cases moved by hand between machines. The goal is a steady rate on every shift, with less strain on your people.
Hard-to-fill positions.
Robotic palletizing and case packing take repetitive lifting off the schedule, so the line depends less on staffing the toughest jobs on the floor.
Ergonomics.
Removing repeated lifting, bending and twisting at the end of the line takes that motion off your operators.
Line stops and backups.
Conveying, accumulation and routing keep cases moving between machines.
Throughput.
Each platform is matched to the rate, payload and pallet pattern of your line.
Floor space.
Compact cells and engineered layouts make the most of the area you already have.
Changeovers and product mix.
The real SKU mix is part of the application review, so the system fits the plant you run.
Technology partners and memberships
TRF
builds its systems with robots, controls and conveying components from brands that U.S. plants already specify, so your maintenance team works with equipment it knows.

About / Compliance
Integrity and compliance: Our policy and how
to report a concern.
The published policy and a confidential channel to report concerns, with or without identification.
What does the integrity and compliance policy cover?
The policy is organized in the commitments below. The full text is in the published document.
Code of conduct.
Ethical and transparent behavior; respect, dignity and fairness for everyone; no harassment or coercion; compliance with internal rules, laws and regulations; protection of confidential information, the brand and personal data; respect for the environment; and the duty to report any indication of a violation through the official channels.
Information security and industrial property.
Everyone who interacts with the company is responsible for protecting internal information and the materials shared through its systems.
Privacy and data protection.
Personal information of customers, employees, suppliers, service providers and partners is treated with care and in accordance with the applicable data protection law.
Respect for people and equal opportunities.
No form of prejudice is tolerated; internationally recognized human rights are respected; child labor, forced labor and slavery are not tolerated anywhere in the chain of operations, suppliers, partners and customers.
Fighting corruption and bribery.
No bribes, kickbacks or facilitation payments, directly or indirectly, in line with the Brazilian anti-corruption law, the U.S. Foreign Corrupt Practices Act (FCPA) and the UK Bribery Act.
Conflict of interest, gifts and presents.
Employees act in harmony with the company's interests; gifts, presents or hospitality in exchange for personal benefit or favoritism are prohibited.
Relationship with third parties.
Partners and suppliers are expected to act with ethics, social and environmental responsibility and in compliance with anti-corruption laws.
Sustainable development.
Environmental, social and governance (ESG) practices, compliance with environmental legislation and priority to products and services with low environmental impact, aligned with the United Nations Sustainable Development Goals.
Read the Integrity and Compliance Policy (PDF)
→

Use the ombudsman channel below. Any employee, customer, supplier, partner or other stakeholder can report a situation that indicates a violation or potential violation of ethical principles, policies, laws and regulations, discrimination, health and safety issues, working conditions or any other improper conduct. Under the policy, reports are confidential and may be anonymous, and the Integrity and Ethics Committee handles issues related to integrity, ethics and compliance.

page. This channel is only for compliance concerns.

**title:** Case Packing Equipment | TRF Industrial Automation
**description:** Case packing equipment for food and CPG lines: compare TRFRapidpack, TRFOnepack and TRFDroppack, from vision-guided robotic to gravity packing.

```
Case Packing
Case packing equipment that adapts to high-mix lines, difficult
products and repetitive flexible packages.
Robotic, one-machine and gravity case packing, so the architecture follows your product, your speed and your changeovers.
Case packing is the automated process of loading products into shipping cases, and of forming and sealing those cases, before palletizing, using robotic, gravity or integrated machines built for food and CPG lines.

What does a case packer do on a production line?
A case packer groups primary packages into the pattern the case requires and loads them into a shipping case, the secondary packaging step before palletizing. In a top-load machine, the case is presented open and the product enters from above, placed by a robot or released through a controlled drop.
The sequence is similar on most lines. Products arrive from primary packaging, often unevenly spaced, and the machine collates them by rows, layers or counted batches. The case is loaded, closed and sealed, then conveyed toward palletizing; in an integrated machine, it is erected from a flat blank right before loading.
Two variables decide how hard that sequence is. The first is product behavior: a rigid carton, a flexible pouch that changes shape when picked and a thermoformed pack that must not be pressed are different problems. The second is the mix: two SKUs and one pattern is a different project from many SKUs, several case formats and frequent changeovers.
Three case packing platforms: Flexibility, integration and efficiency
The
TRF
case packing family is organized by application rather than by machine size. Robotic handling brings flexibility and gentle product control, integration brings fewer machines and interfaces, and gravity brings mechanical simplicity.
TRF
Rapidpack
: Flexible Robotic Case Packing
TRF
Rapidpack
is the flexible robotic platform for high-mix lines and difficult products. Vision-guided or indexed robotic picking handles pouches, pillow bags, flow packs, thermoformed packages, trays, cartons, cups, tubs and other small packaged products that arrive in variable positions. Recipe-based changeovers reduce mechanical adjustments, controlled handling protects delicate or irregular products, and the cell can grow from one robotic module to several.
TRF
Onepack
: Erecting, Packing and Sealing in One Machine
TRF
Onepack
combines case erecting, robotic product loading and case sealing in one integrated platform. That means fewer machines, fewer control systems, fewer integration points and a smaller footprint, while keeping the flexibility of robotic loading. It is built for small and mid-sized food and CPG manufacturers replacing manual or semi-automatic secondary packaging.
TRF
Droppack
: Gravity Case Packing for Flexible Packages
TRF
Droppack
collates flexible packages and loads them into cases through a controlled drop, for lines that need medium to low throughput without robotic complexity. With no robotic end-of-arm tool and fewer complex components, it suits pillow bags, pouches, VFFS packages, IQF and frozen food bags, grated cheese, rice and grains, pet food and similar free-flowing packaged products.
How do you choose between
TRF
Rapidpack
,
TRF
Onepack
and
TRF
Droppack
?
Choose by product behavior first, then by speed and integration scope. Use the table to shortlist a platform before a detailed application review.
Platform
Application range
Packing principle
Best fit
TRF
Rapidpack
For applications of about 100 to 200 products per minute
Robotic picking, vision-guided or indexed
Many SKUs, variable product positioning, complex pack patterns, delicate or irregular products
TRF
Onepack
For applications of about 20 to 50 products per minute
Case erecting, robotic packing and sealing in one machine
Manual or semi-automatic secondary packaging today, limited floor space, low to medium throughput
TRF
Droppack
For applications of about 20 to 500 products per minute
Controlled gravity loading
Repetitive flexible packages, medium to low throughput, low mechanical complexity
Application ranges, not nominal machine speeds.
TRF
engineering confirms final capacity for each application (product, pack pattern, tooling, case format and process).
The decision rules are direct. Position
TRF
Rapidpack
when the application has high SKU variability, complex pack patterns, variable product positioning or needs vision-guided handling. Position
TRF
Onepack
when the priority is combining erecting, packing and sealing while reducing equipment interfaces and floor space. Position
TRF
Droppack
when the product is a repetitive flexible package that tolerates controlled gravity loading.
Some lines qualify for more than one platform. Then the tiebreakers are product integrity, the SKUs the machine must cover as your catalog grows, the floor space available and how much of the line you want to buy from one supplier.

Once the case is packed and sealed, it becomes the unit the palletizer handles. The
case palletizer page
continues the same line to the finished pallet.
What is the difference between a case packer and a case erector?
A case packer loads product into the shipping case; a case erector forms the case from a flat blank. At
TRF
, erecting is part of the packing system rather than a separate machine:
TRF
Onepack
erects, packs and seals in one platform, and case forming and closing are included in
end-of-line integration
projects.
TRF
does not supply stand-alone case erectors.
The practical benefit is fewer interfaces. Every separate machine in secondary packaging adds a handoff, a control system and a supplier relationship, so consolidating erecting, packing and sealing reduces the places where the flow can break.
Information to prepare before choosing a case packer
With the inputs below, a
TRF
Sales Engineer can recommend a platform, a tooling approach and a configuration:
Product:
package type, dimensions, weight, rigidity and how it behaves when picked or dropped.
Rate:
products per minute per line, peak versus average, and how many lines feed the case packer.
Pack pattern and case:
count per case, orientation, layers, case dimensions, erected cases or flat blanks, and sealing method.
Mix and changeover:
number of SKUs, changeover frequency and expected catalog growth.
Layout and integration:
floor space between primary packaging and palletizing, upstream machines, conveyors and the palletizer.

and prepared foods,

and

plants. Each

and

Questions
Frequently asked questions
What Is Top-Load Case Packing, and When Is It the Right Choice?
Top-load case packing loads product into the case from above, by robotic pick-and-place or by a controlled gravity drop. It suits pack patterns built by rows or layers, mixed SKU production and delicate products. All three
TRF
platforms are top-load machines, and the
top-load case packer page
compares them by application.
What Is a Drop Packer and How Does Gravity Packing Work?
A drop packer groups packages above the case and releases them through a controlled drop instead of picking them with a robot.
TRF
Droppack
uses this principle for repetitive flexible packages such as pillow bags, pouches, VFFS and IQF bags. With no robotic end-of-arm tool and fewer complex components, the machine stays simple to operate and maintain.
Can a Robotic Case Packer Handle Pouches, Trays and Thermoformed Packages in the Same Line?
Yes, within the validated application envelope.
TRF
Rapidpack
is built for high-mix production and handles pouches, pillow bags, flow packs, thermoformed packages, trays, cartons, cups and tubs with recipe-based changeovers. The limits are the tooling and the pack patterns, which
TRF
validates against your complete SKU list before the machine is specified.
How Many Products per Minute Can a Robotic Case Packer Run?
TRF
Rapidpack
is positioned for applications of about 100 to 200 products per minute,
TRF
Onepack
for about 20 to 50 and
TRF
Droppack
for about 20 to 500. These are application ranges used for commercial screening, not nominal machine speeds. Final capacity is validated for your product, pack pattern, tooling and case format.
Can a Case Packer Handle Frozen or IQF Products?
Yes. IQF product bags and frozen food bags are among the products
TRF
Droppack
handles with controlled gravity loading, and frozen food is a focus industry for
TRF
Rapidpack
. On frozen lines, palletizing below 32°F is covered by
TRF
Subzero
, so case packing and palletizing can be designed together for the same plant.
How Does a Case Packer Protect Delicate or Irregularly Shaped Products?
With controlled handling.
TRF
Rapidpack
picks thermoformed packs, trays, filled pouches and flow-wrapped items robotically, with tooling selected for each product. Vision-guided picking also handles products that arrive in variable positions, protecting product integrity while the line keeps its rate. For repetitive flexible packages that tolerate a controlled drop,
TRF
Droppack
avoids robotic handling altogether.
Can Case Packing Equipment Integrate With My Existing Palletizer and Conveyors?
Yes. Each
TRF
case packer can run as a standalone machine or as part of a
TRF
end-of-line system.
TRF
Intraflow
conveys, accumulates and routes sealed cases to the palletizer, and the
conveyor systems page
explains how the flow is engineered. Interfaces with equipment already installed are defined during project engineering.
What Is the Typical ROI of Switching From Manual to Robotic Case Packing?
There is no single figure. The return comes from the operator hours the case packer removes, multiplied by your loaded labor cost and compared with the installed cost of the system. Throughput that no longer depends on manual speed and controlled handling that protects the product add value beyond labor. The
palletizing ROI calculator
applies the same labor logic to the palletizing side.

```
Case Packing / Bag Case Packer
Bag case packers matched to
your line speed.
Pack VFFS, IQF and flexible bags into cases with the architecture that fits your line speed and setup.
A bag case packer is a top-load machine that collates filled flexible bags, such as VFFS, IQF, pet food and grated cheese bags, and loads them into shipping cases in a repeatable count and pattern.
TRF
Industrial Automation packs these bags with
TRF
Droppack
, its gravity drop packer. Pouches and pillow bags can also be packed with
TRF
Rapidpack
, and pillow bags with
TRF
Onepack
, when the line calls for robotic handling.
Talk with our Sales Engineer
→
Custom engineering
Solutions designed around your line.
Turnkey integration
From concept to full production.
High reliability
Built for continuous operation.
U.S. market focus
Support across the United States.
Lifecycle support
Backed by
TRF
Care
.
What is a bag case packer?
A bag case packer is a secondary packaging machine that takes filled and sealed flexible bags from the bagger, groups them into the count the case requires and loads them into the case from above. It sits between the bagging line and case sealing, conveying and palletizing.
TRF
uses three mechanisms for bags. Controlled gravity loading collates bags above the case and drops them along a guided path, which suits repetitive formats. Robotic pick-and-place lifts each bag or group and sets it in position, which suits variable positions, many SKUs and patterns where orientation matters. An integrated machine erects the case, loads it robotically and seals it, which suits lower volumes that want fewer machines.
TRF
supplies all three as top-load systems; the
top load case packer
page explains the format.
Bags are hard to pack because no two are exactly the same shape:
Slack fill and settling.
Product settles after bagging, so the same bag can present a different profile at the packer.
Trapped air.
Air in the bag changes its height and stability in the case and how many bags fit the pattern.
Seal geometry and film.
Fin seals and gussets stack differently, and some films stick to each other or to guides.
Temperature.
Frozen bags are stiff and may carry frost, while warm product can make film tacky.
That is why
TRF
validates every application with real bags at production condition.
How does gravity drop packing work for flexible bags?
Gravity drop packing collates bags above the open case and releases them through a controlled drop, so the bags land in the pattern the case requires without a robot picking each one.
TRF
Droppack
is built on this controlled drop process, which keeps operation simple.
The advantages come from the design. Fewer complex components mean less to maintain, and with no robotic end-of-arm tooling there is no gripper to change when the bag changes. Vertical product movement can also give a compact packing architecture, which helps when space between the bagger and the case sealer is limited.
The limits are equally clear. The product has to tolerate a controlled drop, and the pack pattern has to repeat so collation can build it. Bags that must lie in a specific orientation, overlap precisely or be handled with extra care are usually a robotic application.
TRF
evaluates the drop with your actual bag, because fill level, seal quality and product density change how a bag behaves on the way down.
The pack pattern ties the bag, the case and the pallet together: bags per case, layers, orientation and how the case closes. A case that is easy to pack but unstable on the pallet moves the problem downstream, so
TRF
validates the pattern with the case and the palletizing step together.
Which bags can a bag case packer handle?
TRF
Droppack
packs VFFS and other flexible bags, IQF and frozen food bags, pet food, rice and grains, and grated cheese bags, plus pillow bags and pouches.
TRF
Rapidpack
also packs pouches and pillow bags, and
TRF
Onepack
packs pillow bags. Each bag type below has its own engineering questions.
IQF and Frozen Food Bags
IQF product bags and frozen food bags fit gravity packing with
TRF
Droppack
when formats repeat, contents flow freely and the pattern is standardized. Cold packing areas change how film behaves and add frost, so
TRF
validates film behavior and pattern stability with real frozen product. Downstream,
TRF
Subzero
palletizes in areas below 32°F. See
frozen food and ice cream automation
.
Pet Food Bags
TRF
Droppack
packs pet food bags. Retail pet food bags packed into shipping cases are a case packing application, and the case format is defined together with the palletizing pattern because bag size and case weight change both. See
pet food packaging automation
.
Rice, Grains and Free-Flowing Products
TRF
Droppack
packs rice, grains and similar free-flowing packaged products. These bags settle into a stable shape, which is what gravity packing needs, so the engineering questions are about stability in the case: bags per case, layers, flat or on edge, and how the case closes. See
food processing automation
.
Grated Cheese Bags
TRF
Droppack
packs grated cheese bags. The bag is soft, the contents shift easily and the product is usually handled cold, so the pattern has to hold without crushing the bags below. Repetitive formats favor
TRF
Droppack
. See
dairy and cheese automation
.

pouches
and

Questions
Frequently asked questions
What Is the Throughput Range of a Gravity Case Packer?
TRF
Droppack
is designed for applications of about 20 to 500 products per minute, for lines that need gravity loading without robotic complexity.
TRF
publishes application ranges, not machine speeds: actual capacity depends on the bag, the collation, the drop path, the pack pattern and the case, and
TRF
engineering validates it with your real bags before the design is closed.
Does a Case Packer Need Vision-Guided Robotics for High-Mix Production?
Only when bag position varies or the line runs many formats.
TRF
Rapidpack
picks with vision-guided delta robots when bags reach the packer in a random flow, and with indexed picking when they arrive in fixed lanes. Repetitive bags in standardized patterns usually need neither, which is where
TRF
Droppack
and its controlled gravity loading fit.
Do All Bags Need to Be Packed in Cases Before Palletizing?
No. Many large bags and sacks ship directly on pallets without a shipping case, which makes them a palletizing application rather than a case packing one.
TRF
covers both: case packing with
TRF
Droppack
,
TRF
Rapidpack
or
TRF
Onepack
, and direct palletizing on the
bag palletizer
page, with the gripper and pallet pattern engineered for each bag.
What Is the Difference Between a Case Packer and a Case Erector?
A case erector forms an open case from a flat blank, and a case packer loads products into that case.
TRF
supplies case erecting only as part of an integrated solution:
TRF
Onepack
erects, packs and seals in one machine, and end-of-line projects include case forming and closing, so one partner answers for the flow from flat case to sealed case.
How Fast Can a Case Packer Change Over Between SKUs?
It depends on how different the bags, patterns and cases are. On
TRF
Rapidpack
, product and pattern changes are recipe-based with reduced mechanical adjustments. On
TRF
Droppack
, there is no robotic tooling to change, and the changeover plan for the formats the line runs is set during engineering. Count how many changeovers you run per week, because that number shapes the choice.
Can Case Packing Equipment Integrate With My Existing Palletizer and Conveyors?
Yes. Each
TRF
case packing platform can run standalone or integrate with conveyors, case handling, palletizing and intralogistics as one end-of-line system.
TRF
Intraflow
moves and accumulates packed cases to a
TRF
Cobot
,
TRF
Compact
or
TRF
Speed
palletizer, and interfaces with your bagger and existing equipment are defined during project engineering. See
end-of-line integration
.
What Is the Typical ROI of Switching From Manual to Robotic Case Packing?
There is no typical figure, because payback depends on your plant. The estimate compares the installed cost of the system with the annual savings: packers per shift, shifts per day, local labor cost, the output the bagger gains when the packing table stops limiting it, damaged bags and rejected cases avoided, and time saved at changeovers. A
TRF
Sales Engineer builds it with your data.

What is a carton packing machine?
A carton packing machine is the equipment that turns a stream of individual retail cartons into shipping cases ready for the pallet. It collects cartons as they leave the cartoner and the checks after it, groups them into the count and arrangement each customer requires, and lowers that group into an open case so it can be sealed, coded and palletized.
Folding cartons carry the brand at retail: frozen entrees, cereal and snack cartons, candy boxes and household products. They share useful properties for automation. They are rigid and rectangular, they present flat faces a tool can hold, and they stack predictably into layers. What varies from plant to plant is the mix of sizes, the orientation retailers expect and how fast the cartoner discharges.
Because
TRF
Rapidpack
and
TRF
Onepack
load through the open top of the case, cartons are placed rather than pushed, which keeps control of orientation and protects the printed faces. The
top load case packer
page explains the format across the family. Carton lines are common in
frozen food and ice cream
,
confectionery
,
CPG
and
household and cleaning products
plants.
What is the difference between a carton packing machine and a cartoner?
A cartoner forms the folding carton and puts product into it. A carton packing machine takes cartons that are already formed, filled and closed and packs them into shipping cases for distribution. The two machines sit one after the other: the cartoner serves retail presentation, the case packer serves secondary packaging.
TRF
supplies the second step. The case packer has to keep pace with whatever the cartoner discharges, so the interface between them, including line signals, SKU changes and an accumulation buffer that keeps the cartoner running through a case change, is defined during project engineering. Rejects from the checkweigher or code verification should leave the line before the packing area.
How are cartons packed into cases?
Cartons are packed in a pattern that sets the count, orientation and layers in each case, defined per SKU and stored as a recipe. The details that decide a carton project are orientation, collation and surface protection.
Orientation.
Cartons can enter the case flat, on edge or standing. Retailers may ask for a specific face toward the opening of a shelf-ready case, and some cartons go on edge so the case holds more units.
Collation and counts.
Cartons usually reach the case in groups, built by the robot picking several cartons per cycle, by an infeed that gathers a row or both. A plant often ships the same carton in more than one count.
Graphics and squareness.
Tooling and motion are set to avoid marking the print. Cartons that are slightly out of square or bowed after filling behave differently in a tight pattern, so
TRF
asks for samples from normal production.
One carton packing machine can run several sizes and patterns within the validated application.
TRF
Rapidpack
changes products and patterns by recipe, with reduced mechanical adjustments, and
TRF
Onepack
runs multiple products, recipes and packing patterns through robotic loading. Sharing planned carton formats early keeps a new SKU from becoming a mechanical rebuild after startup.
Which carton case packer fits your line setup and speed?

Questions
Frequently asked questions
Does a Carton Packing Machine Need a Vision System?
Only when carton position varies.
TRF
Rapidpack
packs with vision-guided delta robots when cartons arrive in a variable flow, or with indexed picking when they arrive in a controlled position, which is common when the cartoner discharges in a fixed index. The infeed design and the number of SKUs decide which approach fits, and
TRF
defines it in the application review.
Should Cartons Go Into a Shipping Case or Straight Onto the Pallet?
It depends on the channel. Cartons that ship in cases need a carton packing machine, while display-ready cartons that go directly onto the pallet need a
carton palletizer
. The two paths use different tooling, patterns and conveying, so decide early. Plants that do both can have the case packer, the conveying and the palletizer engineered by
TRF
as one project.
What Is the Difference Between an Integrated Case Packer and Separate Erecting, Packing and Sealing Machines?
An integrated case packer erects the case, loads it and seals it in one coordinated machine, with one controls platform. Separate machines each need their own interfaces, controls and floor space.
TRF
Onepack
is the integrated option, for applications of about 20 to 50 products per minute, and it can start standalone and later join a complete
TRF
end-of-line system.
How Many Products per Minute Can a Robotic Case Packer Run?
TRF
Rapidpack
is designed for applications of about 100 to 200 products per minute.
TRF
publishes application ranges, not machine speeds: actual capacity depends on the carton, the collation, the pick tool, the pack pattern and how cartons leave the cartoner, and
TRF
engineering validates it for each line before the design is closed.
Can Case Packing Equipment Integrate With My Existing Palletizer and Conveyors?
Yes. Each
TRF
case packing platform can run standalone or integrate with conveyors, case handling, palletizing and intralogistics as one end-of-line system.

What Is the Difference Between a Case Packer and a Case Erector?
A case erector forms an open case from a flat blank, and a case packer loads products into that case.
TRF
supplies case erecting only as part of an integrated solution:
TRF
Onepack
erects, packs and seals in one machine, and end-of-line projects with
TRF
Rapidpack
include case forming and closing, so one partner answers for the flow from flat case to sealed case.
What Is the Typical ROI of Switching From Manual to Robotic Case Packing?
There is no typical figure, because payback depends on your plant. The estimate compares the installed cost of the system with the annual savings: operators packing cases per shift, shifts per day, local labor cost, output the cartoner gains when packing stops limiting it, cartons scuffed or crushed by handling and time saved at changeovers. ==============================================================================
**title:** Cup and Tub Case Packer: Top-Load Case Packing | TRF
**description:** TRF cup and tub case packers load sealed cups and tubs into shipping cases with TRFRapidpack or TRFOnepack, chosen by your line setup and speed.

```
Case Packing / Cup and Tub Case Packer
Cup and tub case packer matched
to your line.
Pack cups and tubs into cases with the architecture that fits your product mix and line speed.
A cup and tub case packer is a top-load machine that picks filled, lidded cups and tubs from the line and places them upright into shipping cases in a set pack pattern.
TRF
Industrial Automation packs cups and tubs with
TRF
Rapidpack
and cups with
TRF

. The right one is defined mainly by your line setup and speed, not by the container alone.
Talk with our Sales Engineer

What is a cup and tub case packer?
A cup and tub case packer is a secondary packaging machine that collates filled, sealed cups or tubs and loads them into a shipping case from above, in the count and pattern the case requires. It sits after the filler and sealer and before case sealing, conveying and palletizing.
The machine does three things in sequence. It receives the containers, usually in lanes from the filling and sealing line. It groups them into the layout the case needs, in one or more layers. Then it places the group into an open case, by robotic pick-and-place or inside an integrated machine that also erects and seals the case. All
TRF
case packers load from the top; the
top load case packer
page explains the format across the family.
Cups and tubs arrive at the end of the line in high counts and frequent format changes: single cups, multipacks, promotional packs and private label versions of the same product. Manual packing works until volume, SKU count or labor availability makes it the slowest step on the line. Cup and tub lines are common in
dairy and cheese
,
frozen food and ice cream
and
food processing
plants, including prepared foods.
How does a case packer protect cups, tubs and lid seals?
A case packer protects cups and tubs by picking them on surfaces that can take the load and placing them rather than dropping them. Cups and tubs are rigid enough to pick one by one or in groups, but a machine can only grip the lid, the rim and the sidewall.
The lid is the seal.
Picking on a foil or film lid has to hold the container without deforming the seal. Where the lid cannot take the load, the tool picks from the sides or supports the container underneath.
Tapered walls.
Most cups and tubs taper, so side gripping has to account for the angle and for how containers sit against each other in the case.
Cold and condensation.
Refrigerated and frozen containers can arrive wet or frosted, which changes how tooling grips them.
Multipacks and sleeves.
Banded, shrink-wrapped or sleeved multipacks behave differently from single cups and are treated as a separate format in the recipe.
Label orientation.
Retail-ready cases may need labels facing one way, which adds an orientation step to the pick and place.
TRF
Rapidpack
is designed for controlled handling of delicate, flexible or irregularly shaped products.
TRF
validates the tooling against your real containers, including cold product, before the machine is built.
How are cups and tubs packed into cases?
Cups and tubs are packed upright in a pattern defined by container size, case size, count per case and the number of layers. Retail-ready cases, club-store formats and e-commerce cases can use the same container with different patterns, and that is where changeover time becomes a real cost.
TRF
Rapidpack
changes products and patterns by recipe, with reduced mechanical adjustments, so switching from one cup format to another is mostly a recipe selection plus any tooling change the containers require.
TRF
Onepack
runs multiple products, recipes and packing patterns through robotic loading within the validated application. Each format is validated during project engineering.
Settle a few questions before the pattern is fixed: which counts per case you actually ship, whether pads or dividers go between layers, how the case is closed and whether a specific face must be visible on the shelf.

Does a Cup and Tub Case Packer Need a Vision System?
Only when product position varies. When containers arrive in controlled lanes from the sealer, indexed picking is enough. When the flow is variable or several SKUs share the infeed,

What Is the Difference Between an Integrated Case Packer and Separate Erecting, Packing and Sealing Machines?
An integrated case packer erects the case, loads it and seals it in one coordinated machine, with one controls platform. Separate machines each need their own interfaces, controls and floor space.

Why do manufacturers choose a drop packer?
Food manufacturers choose
TRF
Droppack
when the package is flexible, the pattern repeats and simplicity matters more than robotic flexibility. These are the six reasons.
Simplicity
A gravity-based architecture avoids robotic handling when the application does not require it, so the machine stays simple to run.
Reliability
Reduced mechanical complexity supports consistent industrial operation.
Labor Reduction
The machine automates repetitive product grouping and manual case loading, so operators stop counting and loading by hand.
Small Footprint
Vertical product movement can provide a compact packaging architecture where floor space at the packing step is tight.
Low Maintenance
Fewer complex components and no robotic end-of-arm tooling to specify, change and service.
Efficiency
Continuous, controlled case loading combines labor savings and throughput improvement with a less complex automation architecture.
What is a drop packer, and how is it different from a robotic case packer?
A drop packer is a case packing machine that groups packaged products above an open case and releases them into it through a controlled drop, using gravity instead of a robot to place the product. It is also called a gravity case packer, and it suits flexible packages, such as bags and pouches, that settle into the case on their own.
A robotic case packer picks each product and places it in the case. That suits variable products, orientations and patterns, but it adds robots and end-of-arm tooling to specify and maintain. When a product tolerates a controlled drop and the pack pattern repeats every cycle, a flexible bag case packer built on gravity removes that complexity. When patterns change often or packages must be oriented, robotic packing is the better tool.
TRF
Droppack
brings that logic to lines where the product and the pack pattern do not require the complexity of robotic handling. Simple by design, it runs as a standalone case packer or as one part of a complete
TRF
end of line.
Which applications fit a drop packer?
Drop packing fits mid-sized and large food manufacturers running repetitive flexible packages, where simplicity and low operational complexity matter more than maximum robotic flexibility.
TRF
Droppack
packs:
Pouches
and
pillow bags

pet food
. The

goes deeper into VFFS, IQF, pet food, rice and grains and grated cheese bags.

What Is the Throughput Range of a Gravity Case Packer?

What Is a Vertical Case Packer and When Is It the Right Fit?
A vertical case packer moves the product downward into the case instead of placing it with a robot arm, which is how a gravity drop packer works. It fits when the package is flexible, the pack pattern repeats and the priority is efficiency with low mechanical complexity.
TRF
Droppack
uses that vertical movement, which can also keep the packing station compact on a crowded floor.
Can a Case Packer Handle Frozen or IQF Products?
Yes.

Droppack
packs IQF product bags and frozen food bags, along with other flexible bags and pouches.

Subzero

What Is the Difference Between a Case Packer and a Case Erector?
A case erector forms a flat corrugated blank into an open case; a case packer loads product into it.
TRF
does not sell stand-alone case erectors.

Onepack
erects, packs and seals inside one machine.

## /case-packing/flow-wrap-case-packer/
==============================================================================
**title:** Flow Wrap Case Packer | TRF Industrial Automation
**description:** TRF flow wrap case packers load flow packs into cases with TRFRapidpack robotic top-load packing, plus TRFOnepack or TRFDroppack when your line fits.

```
Case Packing / Flow Wrap Case Packer
Flow wrap case packer for fast
flow pack lines.
Pack flow wraps into cases without hand loading, with the platform chosen for your line speed and setup.
A flow wrap case packer is a top-load machine that loads flow packs, the horizontally wrapped packages produced by flow wrappers, into shipping cases in a set count, pattern and orientation.
TRF
Industrial Automation packs flow packs with
TRF
Rapidpack
, the robotic case packer built for high-mix lines.
Talk with our Sales Engineer
→
Custom engineering
Solutions designed around your line.
Turnkey integration
From concept to full production.
High reliability
Built for continuous operation.
U.S. market focus
Support across the United States.
Lifecycle support
Backed by
TRF
Care
.
What is a flow wrap case packer?
A flow wrap case packer is secondary packaging equipment that collects flow packs from one or more flow wrappers and loads them into a shipping case through its open top. It receives packages at wrapper speed, arranges them in the count and orientation the case requires and places them in a way that protects the wrap and the product inside.
Every
TRF
case packer loads from above, and the full case moves on to closing, conveying and palletizing. The
top load case packer
page explains the format for the whole family.
Flow wrapped products reach the end of the line in a fast, continuous stream, and they behave differently from rigid packages:
Soft, not rigid.
The film follows the shape of the product, so packages flex when handled and settle when stacked.
Seal geometry.
The fin seal along one face and the crimped ends add thickness at specific points, which affects how packages nest.
Brittle contents.
Biscuits, wafers, crackers and thin bars break if they are squeezed, dropped or loaded under too many layers.
Position at the discharge.
Spacing and orientation can vary when the wrapper accelerates, stops or restarts.
Flow wrap lines are common in
bakery and snacks
,
confectionery
and
food processing
plants, often with many SKUs on the same wrapper.
Does a flow wrap case packer need vision-guided robotics?
Only when package positions vary. When flow packs leave the wrapper with consistent spacing and orientation, indexed picking is simple: the robot knows where each package will be. When packages arrive rotated, shingled, with variable gaps or merged from more than one wrapper, vision guidance lets the delta robots find each package before picking it.
TRF
Rapidpack
supports both approaches.
During the application review,
TRF
looks at how packages leave each wrapper, how they are conveyed to the packer and what happens to the flow when a wrapper stops. Accumulation between wrapper and packer absorbs short stops, so the case packer keeps a stable rhythm.
The end-of-arm tool is chosen for the package, not for the category. Film, fin seal position, weight, headspace and the orientation required in the case decide how the package is held and whether the robot picks one package or a group at a time.
TRF
validates the tool with your real flow packs, including packages at the size and weight limits of each SKU.
How are flow packs packed into cases?
Flow packs are packed in a pattern that sets the count, rows, layers and orientation in each case. The pattern defines the cycle the packer must perform and the case that will be used, so it is decided early in the project.
On edge, in rows.
Packages stand on their long edge, side by side, common for bars and cookie packs and easy to unload at the store.
Flat, in layers.
Packages lie face up or face down in stacked layers, common for thin or wide flow packs that need support.
Shingled.
Packages overlap like roof shingles, which can stabilize soft packages.
Alternating fin seals.
Rows rotate so fin seals and crimped ends do not stack on each other, which keeps the layer flat.
Because flow packs compress, the case must let the flaps close without squeezing the layer while still holding the load stable in transport. The number of layers also sets how much weight rests on the bottom row, so fragile products may need fewer layers or a different orientation.
Flow wrapping lines often carry several flavors, seasonal formats and customer-specific cases.
TRF
Rapidpack
changes products and patterns by recipe, with reduced mechanical adjustments, so many changeovers become software selections. The project documents which SKUs share a recipe and which, if any, need a format part.

TRF

is the starting point for flow wrap lines, because flow wrapping usually means speed and SKU variety. It fits medium- to high-speed lines in food and CPG plants, handles delicate and irregularly shaped products, and can scale from one robotic module to several as production grows.
TRF

Each platform can run standalone or as part of a complete

Questions
Frequently asked questions
Is a Flow Pack the Same as a Flow Wrap?
Yes. Flow wrap, flow pack and horizontal form-fill-seal describe the same package: a tube of film formed around the product, sealed along its length and crimped at both ends. Buyers in the US usually say flow wrap or flow wrapped product, and
TRF
uses flow pack for the same soft, roughly rectangular package. On this page, all of these terms mean one package.
Can Several Flow Wrappers Feed One Case Packer?
It can, when the combined rate and the layout allow it.
TRF
reviews how many wrappers feed the packer, how packages arrive, how much accumulation exists and how the flow behaves when one wrapper stops. The infeed, the picking method and the collation are then defined for the combined flow, often with vision guidance when lanes merge.
How Does a Case Packer Protect Delicate or Irregularly Shaped Products?
By controlling the pick, the placement and the load in the case. The tool holds the package securely without pressing on the product and releases it in position rather than dropping it.
TRF
Rapidpack
is designed for controlled handling of delicate, flexible or irregularly shaped products, and
TRF
checks broken product, seal condition and pattern stability with real flow packs.
How Many Products per Minute Can a Robotic Case Packer Run?
TRF
Rapidpack
is designed for applications of about 100 to 200 products per minute,
TRF
Onepack
for about 20 to 50 and
TRF
Droppack
for about 20 to 500.
TRF
publishes application ranges, not machine speeds: actual capacity depends on the flow pack, the pick tool, the pack pattern and the infeed, and
TRF
engineering validates it for each line.
Can Case Packing Equipment Integrate With My Existing Palletizer and Conveyors?
Yes. Each
TRF
case packing platform can run standalone or integrate with conveyors, case handling, palletizing and intralogistics as one end-of-line system.
TRF
Intraflow
carries sealed cases to a
TRF
Cobot
,
TRF
Compact
or
TRF
Speed
palletizer, and interfaces with your wrappers and existing equipment are defined during project engineering. See
end-of-line integration
.
What Is the Typical ROI of Switching From Manual to Robotic Case Packing?
There is no typical figure, because payback depends on your plant. The estimate compares the installed cost of the system with the annual savings: operators casing packages by hand per shift, shifts per day, local labor cost, wrapper output lost to packing backlogs, broken product and time saved at flavor and format changes. Why do manufacturers choose an integrated case packer?
Small and mid-sized food and CPG manufacturers choose
TRF
Onepack
to automate secondary packaging in one step. These are the six reasons.
Integration
Case erecting, robotic packing and sealing are managed as one coordinated system, not as three machines to connect.
Labor Reduction
Manual work across several secondary packaging operations moves into one machine, freeing operators for other tasks.
Simplicity
Fewer equipment interfaces, controls and integration points make the project easier to specify, install and run.
Small Footprint
One machine architecture takes the place of separate erecting, packing and sealing stations, so the case step needs less floor space.
Flexibility
Robotic loading handles multiple products, recipes and packing patterns within the validated application envelope.
Reliability
An integrated architecture reduces handoffs between machines and is designed around continuous production flow.
What is an integrated case packing machine, and how is it different from separate erecting, packing and sealing machines?
An integrated case packing machine erects the case from a flat blank, loads the products into it and seals it inside one coordinated system. It covers the whole secondary packaging step, from flat case to sealed case, ready for conveying and palletizing. Form, pack, seal: three processes, one solution.
The alternative is a line of separate equipment: a case erector, then a case packer machine, then a case sealer, each with its own frame, controls and interfaces. That arrangement works, but it multiplies the interfaces to specify, install, connect and support, and it takes more floor space.
What you compare
Three separate machines
TRF
Onepack
Equipment interfaces
Erector to packer to sealer, each connection designed and commissioned
One machine, fewer interfaces and integration points
Controls
One control system per machine, plus line coordination
The three processes managed as one coordinated system
Floor space
Three frames plus the conveyors between them
Several packaging functions in one machine architecture
Operation
Operators follow three machines and their handoffs
Operators follow one machine
Responsibility
Several suppliers, schedules and acceptance steps
One capital project with one responsible partner
Inside the machine, product loading is robotic, so integration does not lock the line to a single format. The trade-off is scope and speed: lines that need higher rates, vision-guided picking or a very wide range of formats belong with a dedicated robotic cell such as
TRF
Rapidpack
.
Which applications fit an integrated case packer?
TRF
Onepack
fits small and mid-sized food and CPG manufacturers that want to replace manual or semi-automatic secondary packaging with one machine that erects, packs and seals, at low to medium throughput. It packs these formats from the top:
Pillow bags
Thermoformed packages
Trays
Cartons
Cups
Small packaged products, validated per application
Typical plants are in
food processing
, including prepared foods, and in
consumer packaged goods
, where the case step still depends on manual or semi-automatic work.

TRF

when most of these statements are true:

TRF

application range.

Floor space for three separate machines is limited.
You want fewer equipment interfaces and one partner for the whole case step.
The line runs more than one product or packing pattern.

Questions
Frequently asked questions
What Floor Space Does an Integrated Case Packing Machine Require?
The space depends on the case formats, the product infeed and how the machine sits in your line. The principle stays the same:
TRF
Onepack
consolidates case erecting, packing and sealing into one machine architecture, so it replaces three separate frames and the conveyors between them.
TRF
confirms the footprint for your plant with a layout study during the application review.
How Much Does a Case Packing Machine Cost?
The cost depends on your products, case formats, rates and the integration scope around the machine.
TRF
Onepack
combines erecting, packing and sealing in one capital project, so the scope is defined once instead of three times. Payback comes from the manual labor removed across those three steps, the shifts the line runs and the separate machines you no longer need to buy and connect.
How Fast Can a Case Packer Change Over Between SKUs?
It depends on what changes between the SKUs. On
TRF
Onepack
, robotic loading supports multiple products, recipes and packing patterns within the validated application envelope, so a product or pattern change starts with a stored recipe. A new case size also involves the erecting and sealing steps, so
TRF
defines the case format range and the changeover plan before the design is confirmed.
What Is the Difference Between a Case Packer and a Case Erector?
A case erector forms a flat corrugated blank into an open case; a case packer loads products into that case.
TRF
Onepack
does both and seals the case as well, in one machine.
TRF
does not sell stand-alone case erectors: case forming exists inside
TRF
Onepack
and inside
end-of-line integration
projects that
TRF
engineers around its case packing platforms.
Can Case Packing Equipment Integrate With My Existing Palletizer and Conveyors?
Yes. Because
TRF
Onepack
seals the case, what leaves the machine is a finished shipping unit ready for conveying.
TRF
engineers the interfaces to conveyors and palletizers already in your plant, and
TRF
Intraflow
covers the integration layer with sensors, PLC and HMI, machine communication and palletizer interfaces. Existing equipment and controls are reviewed with your team before the scope is defined.
Who Supplies Case Packing Equipment for Food and CPG Manufacturers in the US?
TRF
supplies top-load case packing equipment to food and CPG manufacturers across the United States:
TRF
Onepack
for integrated erecting, packing and sealing,
TRF
Rapidpack
for high-mix robotic packing and
TRF
Droppack
for gravity packing of flexible packages.
TRF
also supplies the conveying and palletizing around them, so one partner answers for the whole end of line.

**description:** TRF pillow bag case packers load pillow bags into cases with TRFRapidpack, TRFOnepack or TRFDroppack, chosen by your line setup and speed.

```
Case Packing / Pillow Bag Case Packer
Pillow bag case packer sized
to your line.
Pack pillow bags into cases with the platform that fits your line speed and setup.
A pillow bag case packer is a top-load machine that loads pillow-shaped flexible bags into shipping cases in a set count and pattern, by robotic placement, by an integrated erect, pack and seal machine or by controlled gravity drop. Pillow bags are the one product that all three case packing platforms from
TRF
Industrial Automation pack:
TRF
Rapidpack
,
TRF
Onepack
and
TRF
Droppack
. The choice comes down to your line setup and speed.

→

Turnkey integration

High reliability

U.S. market focus

Backed by
TRF
Care
.
What is a pillow bag case packer?
A pillow bag case packer is secondary packaging equipment that receives filled and sealed pillow bags from the bagger and loads them into an open case in a set count and pattern. The bags enter from above, and the full case moves on to sealing, conveying and palletizing. All
TRF
case packers work this way; the
top load case packer
page explains the format across the family.
Snack bags, frozen vegetable bags, candy bags and many other flexible packages share the pillow shape: a tube of film sealed at the top and bottom, with a seal along the back. The shape is simple, but the bag is not:
Headspace.
Many pillow bags carry air or gas around the product, which cushions contents but makes the bag springy.
Fragile contents.
Chips, crackers and similar products break if the bag is squeezed, dropped too far or compressed in the case.
Shifting contents.
Frozen pieces, grains or small items settle to one end, changing the bag's shape and balance.
Seal fins and film.
Seals add stiff edges, and printed films can be slippery on conveyors and under a pick tool.
That is why automated pillow bag case packing is validated with real bags, not only with bag dimensions. Pillow bag lines are common in
bakery and snacks
,
confectionery
and
frozen food and ice cream
plants.
How are pillow bags packed into cases?
Pillow bags are packed in a pattern that sets how many bags fit in each case and how they sit. Because the bags compress and rebound, the pattern and the case dimensions are defined together.
On edge, in rows.
Bags stand on their side or bottom seal across the case, common for snack bags.
Flat, in layers.
Bags lie face down in one or more layers, common for frozen and heavier bags.
Alternating or interlocked.
Bags rotate from row to row so seal fins and filled ends nest, which can improve case fill.
Robotic placement builds these patterns bag by bag and switches between them by recipe. Gravity loading builds the pattern through collation before the drop, which suits patterns that repeat case after case.
Headspace sets the case size. A case that is too tight compresses the bags when the flaps close and stresses the seals; one that is too loose lets bags move and rub in transport.
TRF
validates the pattern with real bags at the fill and headspace your bagger produces, so the case closes cleanly and the load stays stable.
How does a pillow bag case packer protect fragile contents?
A pillow bag case packer protects fragile contents by limiting squeeze, impact and compression at every step.
TRF
Rapidpack
is designed for controlled handling of delicate, flexible or irregularly shaped products, and
TRF
Droppack
uses a controlled drop, not a free fall, for bags that tolerate gravity loading.
In robotic loading, the end-of-arm tool is selected for the bag: film, headspace, weight and pattern decide how it is held, and
TRF
validates the tool with your bags during project engineering. In gravity loading, collation, drop path and drop height are engineered to bring bags into the case in pattern while protecting seals and contents.
During validation,
TRF
checks breakage after loading and case closing, seal condition, bag position and label visibility, and pattern stability when the case is sealed and conveyed.
Which pillow bag case packer fits your line setup and speed?
Choose by line setup and speed. The three
TRF
case packing platforms form one family, and because all three pack pillow bags, the right model is defined by your SKU mix, the products per minute you run and whether the case should be erected and sealed in the same machine.

Questions
Frequently asked questions
Does a Pillow Bag Case Packer Need Vision-Guided Robotics?
Only when bag positions vary.
TRF
Rapidpack
picks with vision-guided delta robots when bags leave the bagger in a random flow, and with indexed picking when they arrive in a controlled position.
TRF
Droppack
collates bags before the drop and does not use robotic picking.
TRF
defines the approach in the application review, based on your infeed, SKU count and pattern.
What Is the Difference Between a Drop Packer and a Robotic Case Packer?
A robotic case packer places each bag or group of bags in a precise position, which suits high-mix lines and fragile contents. A drop packer collates bags and loads them by controlled gravity, which suits repetitive formats with less mechanical complexity and no robotic tooling to maintain.
TRF
offers
TRF
Rapidpack
as the robotic option and
TRF
Droppack
as the drop packer.
What Is the Difference Between an Integrated Case Packer and Separate Erecting, Packing and Sealing Machines?
An integrated case packer erects the case, loads it and seals it in one coordinated machine, with one controls platform. Separate machines need their own interfaces, controls and floor space.
TRF
Onepack
is the integrated option, for applications of about 20 to 50 products per minute, and it can start standalone and later join a complete
TRF
end-of-line system.
Can a Case Packer Handle Frozen or IQF Products?
Yes, within the validated application.
TRF
Droppack
packs IQF product bags and frozen food bags, and frozen food is one of the focus industries for
TRF
Rapidpack
.
TRF
validates the bag, the pattern and the operating conditions with real product before closing the design, and the same project can extend to palletizing below 32°F with
TRF
Subzero
.
How Fast Can a Case Packer Change Over Between SKUs?
It depends on how different the bags, patterns and cases are.
TRF
Rapidpack
changes products and patterns by recipe, with reduced mechanical adjustments, and
TRF
Onepack
runs multiple recipes through robotic loading.
TRF
Droppack
is designed for standardized patterns. During engineering,
TRF
defines which changes are made in software and which, if any, require a format part.
Can Case Packing Equipment Integrate With My Existing Palletizer and Conveyors?
Yes. Each
TRF
case packing platform can run standalone or integrate with conveyors, case handling, palletizing and intralogistics as one end-of-line system.
TRF
Intraflow
carries sealed cases to a
TRF
Cobot
,
TRF
Compact
or
TRF
Speed
palletizer, and interfaces with your bagger and existing equipment are defined during project engineering. See
end-of-line integration
.
What Is the Typical ROI of Switching From Manual to Robotic Case Packing?
There is no typical figure, because payback depends on your plant. The estimate compares the installed cost of the system with the annual savings: people packing bags by hand per shift, shifts per day, local labor cost, the extra output the bagger gains when packing stops limiting it, broken product avoided and time saved at changeovers. **description:** TRF pouch case packers load pouches into cases with TRFRapidpack robotic packing or TRFDroppack gravity loading, chosen by line setup and speed.

```
Case Packing / Pouch Case Packer
Pouch case packer for robotic
or gravity loading.
Pack pouches into cases with vision-guided robotic loading or controlled gravity loading.
A pouch case packer is a top-load machine that groups filled pouches and places them into shipping cases, by robotic pick-and-place for high-mix lines or by controlled gravity loading for repetitive formats.
TRF
Industrial Automation packs pouches with
TRF
Rapidpack
, its robotic case packer, and
TRF
Droppack
, its gravity drop packer. Your line setup and speed decide which one fits.

What is a pouch case packer?
A pouch case packer is secondary packaging equipment that receives pouches from the filling and sealing machine and loads them, in a defined count and pattern, into a shipping case. It has to keep pace with the filler, arrange pouches in the orientation the case requires and place them without stressing the seals or the film.
Every
TRF
case packer loads from above. The case is presented open at the top, the pouches enter vertically, and the full case moves on to sealing, conveying and palletizing. What separates the options is how pouches reach the case: picked by robots, or collated and released through a controlled drop. The
top load case packer
page explains the format across the whole family.
Pouches are harder to automate than rigid packages. Contents shift and move the center of gravity, two pouches of the same SKU can lie differently on the conveyor, seals and zippers must not be pulled or crushed, and stand-up pouches carry their weight low on a gusseted base. That is why
TRF
validates each application with real pouches before recommending a platform.
Pouch lines are common in
food processing
,
frozen food and ice cream
,
pet food
and
confectionery
plants, where hand packing limits the filler.
How are pouches packed into cases?
Pouches are packed in a pattern that sets the count per case, the rows and layers and the position of each pouch. For pouches, orientation is often what decides between robotic and gravity loading.
Flat, in layers.
Pouches lie on their face, one layer over another, common when cases go to distribution rather than straight to the shelf.
On edge, in rows.
Pouches stand side by side, which uses case volume well and suits cases opened in the store.
Standing upright.
Stand-up pouches rest on their base, often with the front panel facing a defined side of the case.
Shingled.
Pouches overlap partially, which can stabilize flexible packages in the case.
Robotic placement holds a specific orientation pouch by pouch. Gravity loading sets the pattern through collation and the drop path, which works when the pattern repeats and the pouch settles predictably. Case dimensions, pattern and fill are defined together, to keep pouches from shifting in transport and seals from being stressed when the flaps close. When fill levels vary between pouches,
TRF
engineering accounts for that variation in the pattern and in the loading method.
Does a pouch case packer need vision-guided robotics?
Only when pouch position varies.
TRF
Rapidpack
picks with vision-guided delta robots when pouches arrive in a random flow, and with indexed picking when they arrive in a controlled position. The choice follows how pouches leave the filler, how many SKUs the line runs and the orientation the case requires.
Gravity packing with
TRF
Droppack
needs no vision and no robotic tooling, which suits repetitive pouches that tolerate a controlled drop. Seal strength, fill and product type are reviewed together before a pouch is assigned to gravity packing.
Either way, protection comes from controlling every contact with the pouch.
TRF
Rapidpack
is designed for controlled handling of delicate, flexible or irregularly shaped products, with an end-of-arm tool selected for your film, fill and weight. During validation,
TRF
checks seal and zipper condition after loading, pouch position in the case and pattern stability when the case is closed and conveyed.

A case erector forms an open case from a flat blank, and a case packer loads products into that case.

Why do manufacturers choose a robotic case packer?
Food and CPG plants choose
TRF
Rapidpack
when SKU complexity, product orientation and labor decide the case packing project. These are the six reasons.
Speed
High-performance robotic picking and packing serves medium- to high-speed lines, and more robots can be added as the rate grows.
Flexibility
One platform handles multiple products, SKUs, pack patterns and packaging formats, from pouches and pillow bags to trays, cartons and cups.
Fast Changeover
Product and pattern changes are recipe-based, with reduced mechanical adjustments.
Product Integrity
Controlled handling protects delicate, flexible and irregularly shaped products on their way into the case.
Labor Reduction
Robots take over repetitive manual picking, grouping and case loading, so operators move to supervision and quality tasks.
Scalability
Start with one robotic module and expand to multiple robots as your production and your SKU count grow.
What is a robotic case packer, and how is it different from gravity packing?
A robotic case packer uses robots to pick packaged products from a conveyor, arrange them in a pack pattern and place them into shipping cases, replacing repetitive manual picking, grouping and case loading. It sits between primary packaging, where the product is bagged, wrapped, sealed in a tray or filled into a cup, and the end of the line, where cases are closed, conveyed and palletized.
A case packing robot changes what it picks and how it places it through recipes, so a line that runs several SKUs, formats or patterns in the same week does not need a machine rebuilt around each one.
TRF
Rapidpack
uses delta robots, parallel-arm robots built for fast, light pick-and-place work from above, which suits top-load case packing.
A gravity drop packer works differently: it collates products above the case and releases them in a controlled drop, with no robot placing each item. Gravity suits repetitive flexible packages in standard patterns. Robotic packing suits lines where products, orientations and patterns vary, which is the job
TRF
Rapidpack
was designed for.
Which applications fit robotic case packing?
Robotic case packing fits medium- to high-speed lines with repetitive picking and top-load case packing, where labor dependency, SKU complexity, product orientation or throughput limit production.

Rapidpack
packs these formats:

food processing
and prepared foods,
frozen food
,
protein processing
,
bakery and snacks
,
confectionery
,
dairy
and
consumer packaged goods
.
When one line runs several of these formats, each product gets its own recipe for pick logic and pack pattern, and operators switch recipes when the SKU changes instead of rebuilding the process.

Operators spend each shift on repetitive picking, grouping and case loading.
You expect volumes or SKU count to grow.
Before quoting,

When case forming, case closing, conveying or palletizing are part of the project, they are scoped at the same time.

Questions
Frequently asked questions
How Many Products per Minute Can a Robotic Case Packer Run?
TRF
Rapidpack
is positioned for applications of about 100 to 200 products per minute. That figure is an application range, not a nominal machine speed. The rate a specific line reaches depends on product weight and shape, pack pattern, case size and robot count, and
TRF
engineering confirms it for each application.
Can a Robotic Case Packer Handle Pouches, Trays and Thermoformed Packages in the Same Line?
Yes, within the validated application envelope.
TRF
Rapidpack
handles multiple products, SKUs, pack patterns and packaging formats, including pouches, trays and thermoformed packages, with recipe-based product and pattern changes. Some formats need a different end-of-arm tool, so
TRF
validates every product in your mix before confirming the design. See the
pouch case packer
and
tray packer
pages.
Does a Case Packer Need Vision-Guided Robotics for High-Mix Production?
Not always. Vision-guided picking is needed when products arrive in random positions or orientations; when they arrive spaced and oriented, indexed picking is simpler and runs on a fixed cycle.
TRF
Rapidpack
uses delta robots with either strategy, and high SKU variability or variable product positioning are the usual triggers for vision.
TRF
reviews your product flow before recommending one.
How Fast Can a Case Packer Change Over Between SKUs?
It depends on how different the two SKUs are. On
TRF
Rapidpack
, product and pattern changes are recipe-based with reduced mechanical adjustments, so a change within the same format starts with selecting a stored recipe. A change that needs another end-of-arm tool or case size takes longer.
TRF
maps these changes before the design is confirmed, so the changeover plan is part of the project.
How Does a Case Packer Protect Delicate or Irregularly Shaped Products?
A robotic case packer protects products through controlled handling.
TRF
Rapidpack
picks each package with an end-of-arm tool selected for that product and places it into the case from above, instead of pushing or dropping it. Controlled handling of delicate, flexible or irregularly shaped products is one of the design drivers of the platform, and it keeps the pack pattern consistent case after case.
What Is the Typical ROI of Switching From Manual to Robotic Case Packing?
Return depends on the labor hours replaced, shifts per day, local labor cost, throughput gained and the installed cost of the system.
TRF
Rapidpack
combines labor savings, higher throughput and flexible production in one scalable platform. Because each system is configured for your products, robot count and integration scope, the cost and the payback estimate come from a scoped proposal built on your line data.
What Is the Difference Between a Case Packer and a Case Erector?
A case erector forms a flat corrugated blank into an open case; a case packer loads products into that case.
TRF
offers case erecting only inside integrated solutions: in
TRF
Onepack
, which erects, packs and seals in one machine, or within an end-of-line project around
TRF
Rapidpack
.
TRF
does not sell stand-alone case erectors.
Can Case Packing Equipment Integrate With My Existing Palletizer and Conveyors?
Yes.
TRF
engineers the interfaces between the case packer and the equipment around it, including conveyors and palletizers already installed in your plant.
TRF
Intraflow
covers the integration layer with sensors, PLC and HMI, barcode and vision interfaces, machine communication and palletizer interfaces. Your existing equipment and controls are reviewed with your team before the scope is defined.

What is a thermoformed package case packer?
A thermoformed package case packer is the machine at the end of a thermoform-fill-seal line that collects finished, sealed packages and loads them into shipping cases, ready for case sealing, coding and palletizing. It takes over the work of operators who pick packs off the discharge conveyor, count them, turn them to the right orientation and set them into an open case by hand.
Thermoformed packages are made inside the packaging machine: a web is formed into cavities, the cavities are filled, a lidding film is sealed over them and the web is cut into individual packs. The result is a package with a rigid or semi-rigid body, a flat sealing flange and a lid that is usually the most sensitive surface of the pack. Sliced meats, cheese portions, snack kits and prepared meals are common examples.
Every
TRF
case packer loads the case from above, and
TRF
Rapidpack
and
TRF
Onepack
place each pack into position instead of pushing it. The
top load case packer
page explains the format across the whole family. Thermoformed lines are common in
protein processing
,
dairy and cheese
and
food processing
plants, including prepared foods.
How does a case packer protect thermoformed packages?
A case packer protects thermoformed packages by controlling where the tool touches the pack, how fast the pack moves and how it enters the case.
TRF
Rapidpack
is designed for controlled handling of delicate, flexible or irregularly shaped products, and top loading sets each pack down in place.
Lidding film and seal area.
The lid often carries the print the shopper sees. During the application review,
TRF
defines where the end-of-arm tool may contact the package and where it must not, so the pack reaches the store looking the way it left the thermoformer.
Geometry and flange.
Cavity depth, flange width and rigidity change how a package can be held and how it sits in the case. Shallow packs of sliced product behave differently from deep-draw portions or ready meals.
Product movement.
Loose contents can shift when a package accelerates and stops, so the motion profile, pick point and placement height are defined around the product, not only around the package.
Chilled surfaces.
Moisture on chilled packs affects how any tool grips them, which is why
TRF
asks for samples taken under production conditions.
Does a thermoformed package case packer need vision-guided robotics?
Only when package position varies.
TRF
Rapidpack
packs with vision-guided delta robots for variable product flow, or with indexed picking when packages reach the pick area in a controlled position.
Thermoformers usually release packages in rows, one index at a time, across several lanes. If the discharge conveyor keeps those rows spaced and aligned, indexed picking is the simpler solution. If packages spread, rotate or bunch on the way to the packer, or if lanes merge, vision lets the robot find each pack and pick it where it actually is. Before the review, it helps to document how many lanes leave the thermoformer, the gap between packs at normal speed, what happens when the thermoformer stops or runs a partial index, and where rejected packs leave the line.
How are thermoformed packages packed into cases?
Thermoformed packages are packed in a pattern that sets the count, orientation and layers in each case, defined per SKU and stored as a recipe. Some packs stack lid to base in layers, others stand on edge, and some patterns alternate orientation so the packs nest efficiently. Retail customers often expect packs to face a specific way for display or scanning.
Most thermoformed lines run more than one pattern: the same package may ship in different counts to different customers, or several package sizes may run on the same thermoformer.
TRF
Rapidpack
changes products and patterns by recipe, with reduced mechanical adjustments, and
TRF
Onepack
runs multiple products, recipes and packing patterns through robotic loading within the validated application.
Share planned package formats at the start. A case packer that handles today's SKUs but needs long mechanical changes for every new package will limit the plant later, so
TRF
validates current and planned packages, patterns and cases during project engineering.
Which thermoformed package case packer fits your line setup and speed?
Choose by line setup and speed. The three
TRF
case packing platforms form one family, and the right model is defined mainly by how packages leave the thermoformer, how many SKUs you run, whether the case must be erected and sealed in the same machine and the products per minute you run.

A top load case packer is a machine that places products into an open shipping case from above, by robotic pick-and-place or controlled gravity drop, before the case is sealed. Every case packing platform from

What is a top load case packer?
It is secondary packaging equipment that fills a shipping case through its open top. The case stands with its flaps open, products are grouped into the required pack pattern, and the machine lowers or drops them into the case in a controlled way. The full case then moves on to closing, sealing, conveying and palletizing.
The format is named for the direction of loading. Products travel vertically into the case, so the machine can place them in rows, in layers, flat or on edge. That is why one format works for very different packages: a robot can set a delicate thermoformed tray down gently, while a gravity system can collate a group of flexible bags and release it in one controlled motion.
Most top-load lines follow the same sequence, whatever the loading technology: an empty case is formed from a flat blank and presented open; packages arrive from primary packaging and are spaced, oriented or grouped; the group is placed into the case from above; the flaps are closed and sealed; and the sealed case leaves toward coding, conveying and palletizing.
For most plants, the practical question is not whether to top load but how. The answer depends on how many SKUs the line runs, how products arrive, whether case erecting and sealing already exist, the floor space available and the products per minute the line needs.
TRF
organizes its
case packing equipment
around exactly those variables, for food and CPG plants in
food processing
,
frozen food and ice cream
,
bakery and snacks
,
dairy and cheese
and
pet food
.
Which products can a top load case packer handle?
A
TRF
top load case packer handles pouches, pillow bags, flow packs, thermoformed packages, trays, cartons, cups, tubs and flexible bags, including VFFS, IQF and frozen food bags. The table shows which platforms pack each product and links to the page that explains how that product is packed.

How do pack patterns and changeovers work on a top load case packer?
The pack pattern is the arrangement of products inside the case: how many units, in how many rows and layers, and in which orientation. It is a key input for a top load case packer, because it sets the number of picks or drops per case, the collation the infeed must perform and the case dimensions.
Count and layers.
Multi-layer patterns add a placement step per layer, which affects cycle time on robotic systems.
Orientation.
Products can lie flat, stand on edge or sit at an angle, depending on the package and on how the case is opened later.
Fill and stability.
The pattern must fill the case enough to protect the product in transport without overfilling it.
Retail requirements.
Some customers specify the pattern and the case, and the packer must deliver it consistently across shifts.
Changeover capability differs by platform.
TRF
Rapidpack
changes products and patterns by recipe, with reduced mechanical adjustments, which makes it the platform for lines with frequent SKU changes.
TRF
Onepack
runs multiple products, recipes and packing patterns through robotic loading within the validated application.
TRF
Droppack
is designed for standardized pack patterns, where changeovers are less frequent and a simpler machine has fewer parts to maintain.
