The decision rule is short:
TRF
Rapidpack
when the application has high SKU variability, complex pack patterns, variable product positioning or needs vision-guided robotic handling. It can scale from one robotic module to several as production grows.
TRF
Onepack
when you want to combine case erecting, robotic packing and case sealing while reducing equipment interfaces and floor space, at lower volume.
TRF
Droppack
when the product is a repetitive flexible package that tolerates controlled gravity loading and the line needs medium to low throughput without robotic complexity.
When two options stay open, a few questions usually settle it: how often you change SKU or pattern, whether you already own case erecting and sealing equipment, whether the product tolerates a controlled drop and how much floor space is available at the end of the line.
Questions
Frequently asked questions
What Is the Difference Between a Case Packer and a Case Erector?
A case erector forms an open case from a flat blank, and a case packer loads products into that case.
TRF
supplies case erecting only as part of an integrated solution:
TRF
Onepack
erects, packs and seals in one machine, and end-of-line projects include case forming and closing, so one partner answers for the flow from flat case to sealed case.
What Is a Drop Packer, and How Does Gravity Packing Work?
A drop packer collates products above an open case and loads them through a controlled drop instead of picking each one with a robot.
TRF
Droppack
uses this principle for pillow bags, pouches, VFFS and other flexible packages. It fits when the product tolerates gravity loading and the pattern repeats, which keeps mechanical complexity low and removes robotic end-of-arm tooling from maintenance.
How Many Products per Minute Can a Robotic Case Packer Run?
TRF
Rapidpack
, the robotic platform, is designed for applications of about 100 to 200 products per minute.
TRF
publishes application ranges, not machine speeds: actual capacity depends on the product, the pick tool, the pack pattern, the case and the infeed, and
TRF
engineering validates it for each line before the design is closed.
Does a Case Packer Need Vision-Guided Robotics for High-Mix Production?
Only when product position varies.
TRF
Rapidpack
picks with vision-guided delta robots when products arrive in a random flow, and with indexed picking when they arrive in a controlled position. High-mix lines often benefit from vision because it removes mechanical re-orientation at every SKU change, while repetitive formats can run on indexed picking or gravity loading.
Can Case Packing Equipment Integrate With My Existing Palletizer and Conveyors?
Yes. Each
TRF
case packing platform can run standalone or integrate with conveyors, case handling, palletizing and intralogistics.
TRF
Intraflow
carries sealed cases to a
TRF

,
TRF
Compact
or
TRF
Speed
palletizer, or to a
custom palletizer
, and interfaces with existing equipment are defined during project engineering.
What Is the Typical ROI of Switching From Manual to Robotic Case Packing?
There is no typical figure, because payback depends on your plant. The estimate compares the installed cost of the system with the annual savings: people packing cases by hand on each shift, shifts per day, local labor cost, the output the line gains when packing stops being the constraint and time saved at changeovers. A
TRF
Sales Engineer builds it with your data.

and

, and can supply case packing, conveying and palletizing as one
end-of-line system

Case Packing / Tray Packer
Tray packer for robotic or
integrated case packing.
Pack trays into cases with high-mix robotic packing, or with one machine that erects, packs and seals.
A tray packer is a top-load case packer that places trays of packaged product into shipping cases in a set pattern, as a robotic cell or as an integrated erect, pack and seal machine.
TRF
Industrial Automation packs trays with
TRF
Rapidpack
robotic case packing and with

, which also erects and seals the case . Your line setup and speed decide which one fits.

What is a tray packer?
A tray packer, in secondary packaging, is the machine that takes filled trays from the packaging line and loads them into shipping cases so they can be sealed, palletized and shipped. It does the job operators do by hand today: collect trays as they leave the sealer or wrapper, count them, orient them and lower them into an open case without disturbing the product.
In the US market, "tray packer" also names machines that build a corrugated tray from a flat blank and place product inside it. That is tray forming, a different category outside what
TRF
supplies in the United States. On this page, a tray packer loads finished trays into shipping cases.
Trays reach the case packer in many forms: trays with a sealed film lid, overwrapped trays and rigid trays for bakery items or ready meals, some with a shelf-ready label on top. Each is a separate application, validated with your real trays before a platform and tooling are confirmed. Because
TRF
Rapidpack
and
TRF
Onepack
load from above, a tray is lowered into the case rather than pushed, which matters for film lids and delicate contents. The
top load case packer
page explains the format across the family.
Tray lines run in
bakery and snacks
,
dairy and cheese
,
protein processing
and
food processing
plants, including prepared foods.
Does a tray packer need a vision system?
Only when tray position varies.
TRF
Rapidpack
packs with vision-guided delta robots for variable product flow, or with indexed picking when trays arrive in a controlled position. The choice depends on how trays reach the packer and how many SKUs the line runs.
A tray sealer that discharges in a fixed index, single file or in neat rows presents trays the robot can pick by position alone. A line that merges lanes, rejects trays at an inspection station, runs an accumulation table or lets trays rotate on transfers presents a variable flow, and vision guidance removes the need to re-orient every tray mechanically. Before the review, document whether trays arrive single file or in lanes, whether they can rotate between sealer and packer, how many tray sizes run and whether any tray must be turned for label position.
How are trays packed into cases?
Trays are packed in a pattern that sets the count, orientation and number of layers in each case, defined per SKU and stored as a recipe. One tray packer can run different tray sizes and pack patterns within the validated application:
TRF
Rapidpack
changes products and patterns by recipe with reduced mechanical adjustments, and
TRF
Onepack
runs multiple products, recipes and packing patterns through robotic loading.
Tray patterns raise their own questions. Trays are usually wider than they are tall, so cases often hold few layers rather than a deep stack. Some trays nest when stacked, while others need the case wall or a divider so they do not bear on the product below. Shelf-ready cases may require a specific tray face toward the opening, and retailers may set different counts for the same SKU.
Each tray size and pattern is validated during project engineering. Sharing planned tray formats early is what lets the machine absorb the next format without a rebuild.
Which tray packer fits your line setup and speed?
Choose by line setup and speed. The three
TRF
case packing platforms form one family, and the right model is defined mainly by your SKU mix, how trays arrive, whether the case must be erected and sealed in the same machine and the products per minute you run.

industrial conveyor systems

Cleanflow
lines

What does an industrial conveyor system do on a production line?
An industrial conveyor system does four jobs at once: transport, flow management, routing and integration with the machines at both ends. The difference between a line that flows and a line that stops is usually in the last three.
Transport carries product between machines, across levels and through work areas. Flow management accumulates product when a downstream machine pauses, meters it so a robot picks at a steady rhythm and creates gaps where a scanner or diverter needs them. Routing merges lines, diverts to a second lane, sorts by SKU or destination and rejects product that fails inspection, while integration links the conveyor to the machines through sensors, PLC and HMI, barcode and vision interfaces.
A conveyor that only transports leaves the line exposed. If a palletizer pauses for a pallet change and there is no accumulation, the case packer upstream has to stop. Those are design questions, not catalog questions, and they are why
TRF
engineers conveying as a system.
Two conveyor platforms, chosen by product and environment
Moving a sealed case through a packaging hall and moving exposed food between processing and packaging are different engineering problems. One is about flow and scalability, the other about hygiene, cleanability and uptime between sanitation cycles, so
TRF
answers them with two complementary platforms.
TRF
Intraflow
: Custom Conveyor Systems for Case Intralogistics
TRF
Intraflow
is the packaged-goods material-flow platform. It connects individual machines into one coordinated production flow using standardized modules, and it scales from one line to complete multi-line case intralogistics systems:
Straight conveying
: powered roller, belt and modular belt conveying.
Curves and transfers
: transfers, curves, turntables and pop-up transfers.
Accumulation and flow control
: accumulation, zero-pressure accumulation, metering and gapping.
Merge, divert and sorting
: merging, diverting, sorting and reject stations.
Elevation
: incline and decline, elevators and lowerators, vertical transfers.
System integration:
sensors, PLC and HMI, barcode and vision interfaces, machine communication, palletizer interfaces and warehouse interfaces where applicable.
Typical users are mid-sized and large food, beverage, CPG, protein, frozen food, pet food, personal care and industrial manufacturers that need reliable case movement, routing, accumulation or line balancing.
TRF
Cleanflow
: Sanitary Conveyor Systems for Exposed Food
TRF
Cleanflow
extends
TRF
automation upstream, providing hygienic product handling between processing, inspection and packaging. It is designed around sanitation, accessibility, product integrity and uptime rather than transportation alone. The platform has a Dry line and a Washdown line, and its priority industries are beef, pork, poultry, seafood, frozen foods, ready-to-eat products and dairy and cheese.

Can a single supplier engineer, build and install your entire conveyor system?
Yes.
TRF
manages engineering, manufacturing, controls, assembly, integration and commissioning within one coordinated delivery model, from concept and layout to installation and startup. Conveyors are never sold as loose sections: every project is an engineered system designed around your production flow and layout.
The reason is practical. A conveyor bought as a component solves a distance problem and leaves the flow problem to you: where accumulation goes, how a merge is metered, how the palletizer knows a case is ready and who answers when a transfer jams. When one partner owns the system, those questions are answered inside one scope of supply.
The same logic extends to the whole end of the line.
End-of-line integration
puts
case packing
, conveying and
robotic palletizing
under one engineering scope, with the interfaces to stretch wrapping, labeling and warehouse systems defined in the same project.
What is the difference between accumulation, merging, diverting and sorting?
Each function solves a different flow problem, and the wrong one in the wrong place creates the jam it was meant to prevent.
Accumulation
builds a buffer so upstream equipment keeps running while a downstream machine pauses.
Zero-pressure accumulation
does the same in zones that stop and release individually, so units do not push against each other and release stays predictable.
Metering
releases units at a controlled rate and
gapping
creates space between them, which matters wherever a scanner, a diverter or a robot picks.
Merging
brings two or more flows into one lane and depends on metering to avoid collisions at the junction.
Diverting
transfers units out of the main lane, for a second packing station or a reject line, and
sorting
routes units by destination, usually from barcode or vision data. A
reject station
removes flagged units without stopping the flow.

TRF

where product enters and leaves the system, and which machines sit at each end.
Rates and shifts:
average rates, peak rates and planned growth.
Environment and sanitation:
dry, low-moisture or washdown, and how the area is cleaned today.
Layout and existing equipment:
drawings with floor space, levels, columns and aisles, plus the case packer, palletizer, scanner or warehouse system the conveyor connects to.

## /conveyor-systems/accumulation-and-flow-control/
==============================================================================
**title:** Accumulation Conveyor Systems | TRF Industrial Automation
**description:** Accumulation conveyor systems from TRF buffer, meter and space cases between packaging, case packing and palletizing. Talk with our Sales Engineer.

```
Conveyor Systems / Accumulation and Flow Control
Accumulation conveyor systems for
balanced lines.
Flow management modules that buffer, meter and space cases between packaging, case packing and palletizing.
Accumulation conveyor systems are the flow management part of a conveyor line: they hold cases in a controlled buffer, meter them at a set rate and space them with gaps, so a pause at one machine does not stop packaging, case packing and palletizing.
TRF
Industrial Automation engineers these modules inside
TRF
Intraflow
, always as part of a complete line.
Talk with our Sales Engineer
→
Custom engineering

Turnkey integration
From concept to full production.
High reliability

Lifecycle support
Backed by
TRF
Care
.
Key facts about accumulation and flow control
Item
Accumulation and flow control
What it is
The Flow Management module family of
TRF
Intraflow
, engineered into a complete conveyor system
Standard modules
Accumulation, zero-pressure accumulation, metering and gapping
Items handled
Cases, cartons, trays, totes and other packaged units
Where it runs
Between secondary packaging, case packing, palletizing and warehouse interfaces
Main job
Balance the line, so a pause at one machine does not stop the machine before it
Buffer length and release rate
No catalog figure; defined per project from your line rates, case data and stop patterns
How it is supplied
Always inside an engineered system integrated into the line, never as loose components
What is an accumulation conveyor system?
An accumulation conveyor system is a section of conveyor that lets product wait, in a controlled way, when the equipment downstream cannot take it yet. Instead of stopping the whole line, cases queue in the accumulation zone until the downstream machine is ready, and then they are released in order.
In
TRF
Intraflow
, accumulation belongs to the Flow Management family, which has four standard modules, each solving a different flow problem. Accumulation creates buffer capacity. Zero-pressure accumulation keeps waiting cases from pressing against each other. Metering releases cases at a controlled rate. Gapping creates the spacing that scanners, diverts and palletizers need.
An engineered accumulating conveyor differs from an improvised buffer because it is sized for the actual rates of the connected machines, controlled by sensors and a PLC, and designed together with the modules around it. A buffer that is not designed with the case packer, the palletizer and the controls in mind rarely performs as expected.
Why do packaging and palletizing lines need accumulation?
Packaging and palletizing lines need accumulation because the machines on an end of line rarely pause at the same moment, and without a buffer every short stop spreads. The case packer stops to refill its case supply or to change over a product. The palletizer stops for a pallet change, a slip sheet or a full pallet leaving the cell. A checkweigher or a scanner rejects a case and the flow hesitates.
Without a buffer, each of these pauses travels backward through the line: a palletizer that pauses briefly can stop the case packer, which stops the machine that feeds it, and the restart can take longer than the original pause. With a buffer sized for the stops the line has to absorb, upstream machines keep running while the downstream machine recovers. The same logic works in reverse, when a full buffer keeps the palletizer building pallets while the case packer is stopped.
How does zero-pressure accumulation work on a case conveyor?
Zero-pressure accumulation divides the conveyor into zones controlled by sensors, and each zone stops independently, so every case waits in its own zone instead of pushing against the case ahead. Cases stop with a defined space between them and restart in the same order, with no build-up of force along the queue.
On a conventional accumulating conveyor, the queue builds pressure: the cases at the tail push on the cases at the head, and the pressure grows with the length of the queue. That can crush lightly built cartons, misalign trays, mark printed cases and cause jams at the release point. Zero-pressure accumulation suits light or thin-walled cartons, trays and printed cases, applications where scanners, labelers or diverts need cases separated, and buffers ahead of a palletizer with frequent stops.
Standard accumulation, without zone control, can be enough for sturdy cases on shorter buffers.
TRF
selects between them during engineering, based on the case, the length of the buffer and the way the downstream machine receives product, using the application data you share.
What is the difference between metering and gapping?
Metering releases cases at a controlled rate, and gapping creates a defined space between cases, so the machine or device that receives them can handle each case individually. They are separate functions that usually work together, often in this order on a
TRF
Intraflow
line: accumulation to hold, metering to release and gapping to space.
Metering
matters wherever a stream of cases reaches a point that can only accept so many at a time. At a merge, it keeps two lines from arriving at the same moment. At a palletizer infeed, it presents cases at the rate the palletizer can accept. After an accumulation zone releases, it stops the whole queue from rushing forward at once.
Gapping
turns a tight, irregular stream into one with consistent spacing. Scanners read a case more reliably when it is separated from its neighbors, a divert needs a gap to move one case without catching the next, and a palletizer works better when each case arrives at a predictable position. The release rate of each metering point is defined per project, from your line rates and case dimensions.
Where should accumulation go in an end-of-line system?
Accumulation goes wherever two machines with different stop patterns are connected, and the most common positions are the discharge of the case packer and the infeed of the palletizer. These are the positions a flow review looks at first:
After the case packer.
A buffer lets the case packer keep running while a downstream stop clears, and lets the line downstream keep working during a case packer changeover. Cases can come from the
TRF
Rapidpack
robotic case packer
, the
TRF
Onepack
integrated case packer
, the
TRF
Droppack
drop packer
or equipment already installed.
Ahead of the palletizer.
This buffer keeps the palletizing cell fed during a pallet change and absorbs the stop when a full pallet leaves. Each palletizer, from
TRF
Cobot
and
TRF
Compact
to
TRF
Speed
and the
custom palletizer
, has its own infeed requirements, defined through a palletizer interface.
Before and after a merge.
A short buffer before a merge lets metering release cases at the right moment, and a buffer after it smooths the combined stream. The
merge, divert and sorting modules
rely on this spacing.
At level changes.
Before an incline, decline or elevator, a buffer keeps the vertical transfer from becoming the bottleneck, as described on the
elevation conveyors page
.
Signals such as buffer full, buffer empty and downstream ready are engineered once, in one control concept that covers the sensors, PLC and HMI of the conveyor and the machines at both ends. The buffer can then report how full it is, which is often the first sign of a problem downstream.
Accumulation and flow control belong to
TRF
Intraflow
. For exposed food or hygienic zones, see
TRF
Cleanflow
; both platforms are compared on the
conveyor systems page
. Extended to case packing and palletizing, the same approach becomes
end-of-line integration
.
Questions
Frequently asked questions
How Do I Know if My Line Needs an Accumulation Conveyor?
Your line likely needs one if the case packer stops at every pallet change, if cases reach the palletizer in bunches, if operators push or straighten cases at transfers, or if several lines share one palletizing area. A short review of stop causes and durations shows where a buffer will recover the most output, and where the line already has enough.
How Long Should an Accumulation Buffer Be?
Long enough for the upstream machine to keep running through the longest stop the downstream machine is expected to have, at the rate of the line. There is no fixed length: the buffer is calculated from your line rates, case size and stop patterns during the application review and confirmed against the layout and the space available.
Can Accumulation Prevent the Case Packer From Stopping When the Palletizer Changes Pallets?
Yes, when the buffer is sized for the pallet change. Cases queue on the accumulation section ahead of the palletizer while the pallet is exchanged, and the case packer keeps discharging. When the palletizer resumes, the buffer releases at a metered rate.
TRF
engineers the buffer and the signals between the machines together, so the behavior is defined before startup.
How Does Accumulation Work When Several Lines Feed One Palletizer?
Accumulation and merging decide how the lines take turns. Each line has its own buffer, and metering releases cases onto a shared conveyor at a controlled rate, so no line starves or floods the others. The sharing logic is designed together with the merge modules and the palletizer interface, so a stop on one line does not have to stop the rest.
Can a Conveyor System Integrate With My Palletizer and Case Packer?
Yes.
TRF
Intraflow
includes machine communication and palletizer interfaces, so the accumulation zones exchange signals with the case packer and the palletizer at both ends. Interfaces with
TRF
case packers and palletizers, or with equipment already installed, are defined during engineering, and one partner is responsible for the complete flow from layout to startup.
Talk with our Sales Engineer about accumulation

TRF
Sales Engineer will outline the accumulation, metering and gapping your line needs, engineered as part of a complete
TRF

system. Start on the

.

What is a Custom conveyor system, and how is it different from buying conveyor sections?
A custom conveyor system is an engineered network of conveyors, transfers, accumulation zones, routing devices and controls that moves packaged goods between automated processes in one specific plant. "Custom" refers to the layout, the flow logic and the interfaces, not to one-off hardware:
TRF
Intraflow
uses standard, repeatable modules, and the engineering goes into arranging and controlling them around your production flow.
This is case intralogistics: once a product is packed, the case has to be transported, accumulated, merged, diverted, sorted, elevated and delivered to a palletizer or a warehouse interface, at the rate of the lines. A packaging conveyor system bought section by section tends to leave gaps: cases pushed by hand at transfers, lines that stop when the palletizer pauses, and controls that do not talk to each other.

engineers them into a complete material-flow system with controls and palletizer interfaces, from layout to startup, rather than supplying individual conveyor sections.
Which applications fit Custom conveyor systems?
TRF
Intraflow
fits plants where cases are bottlenecked or still moved by hand between automated processes. Typical applications:
Case packer to palletizer
, with accumulation that decouples the two machines.
Multi-line consolidation
, merging several packaging lines into one palletizing area or a centralized system such as
TRF
Speed
.
Line balancing
, with metering and buffers that keep upstream equipment running during short downstream stops.
Case routing and sorting
, sending each SKU to the right palletizing position or warehouse interface.
Reject handling
, removing cases that fail code reading or inspection before palletizing.
Level changes
, with inclines, elevators, lowerators and vertical transfers where floor space is limited.
Warehouse conveyor systems
, exchanging data with the warehouse management system where the project requires it.
The platform serves mid-sized and large manufacturers in
food processing
,
beverage
,
protein processing
,
frozen food
,
pet food
,
personal care
,
consumer packaged goods
and industrial manufacturing, wherever several packaging or end-of-line processes need reliable case movement.
Is your line ready for a Custom conveyor system?
Your plant is a strong candidate for
TRF
Intraflow
when most of these statements are true:
Operators push, lift or straighten cases between machines.
A short palletizer stop also stops the case packer upstream.
Several packaging lines need to reach one palletizing area.
Cases must be routed by SKU, code or destination.
Aisles, forklift paths or existing equipment block the shortest route.
Conveyors, case packers and palletizers run on controls that do not communicate.
Before quoting,
TRF
reviews five inputs with your team: the units conveyed (dimensions, weight range and bottom construction for each SKU), the line rates and number of lines, layout drawings with levels, columns, aisles and utilities, the points where the flow breaks today, and the case packers, palletizers, scanners and warehouse systems the conveyors must connect to.

Questions
Frequently asked questions
How Does Zero-Pressure Accumulation Work on a Case Conveyor?
Zero-pressure accumulation divides the conveyor into zones controlled by sensors. When a downstream machine pauses, each case stops in its own zone instead of pushing against the case ahead, so cases are not compressed and restart in order. In accumulating conveyor systems built with
TRF
Intraflow
, it is a standard flow management module, sized for the stops your line has to absorb.
What Is the Difference Between a Merge, a Divert and a Sorting Conveyor?
A merge combines cases from two or more conveyors onto one path. A divert sends cases from the main path to another lane, such as a second palletizer or a reject lane. A sorting system uses product data, such as a barcode, to send each case to its assigned destination.
TRF
Intraflow
includes merge, divert, sorting and reject station modules under one control system.
Can a Conveyor System Integrate With My Palletizer and Case Packer?
Yes.
TRF
Intraflow
includes palletizer interfaces, machine communication, sensors, PLC and HMI as standard integration modules.
TRF
connects the conveyors to case packers such as
TRF
Onepack
and
TRF
Droppack
, to palletizers such as
TRF
Cobot
and
TRF
Compact
, and to equipment already installed in your plant. Each interface is defined during project engineering.
Can a Single Supplier Engineer, Build and Install My Entire Conveyor System?
Yes.
TRF
takes ownership of
TRF
Intraflow
projects from concept and engineering to manufacturing, controls, assembly, installation and commissioning, within one coordinated delivery model. One partner answers for the complete intralogistics solution, which reduces the integration risk of combining sections, controls and interfaces from several suppliers. The same team can also integrate the case packers and palletizers at both ends.
How Much Does a Custom Conveyor System Cost?
The cost depends on the number of lines served, line rates, case size and weight, the layout and floor space, the number of accumulation, routing and elevation points, and the interfaces with case packers, palletizers and warehouse systems.
TRF
Intraflow
is always quoted as a complete engineered system.
Can Roller, Belt and Modular Belt Conveyors Be Combined in One System?
Yes.
TRF
Intraflow
combines powered roller, belt and modular belt conveying with transfers, curves, turntables, accumulation, merges, diverts and elevation modules in one coordinated system.
TRF
selects each module for the unit conveyed, the path it follows and the function of that segment, then connects it with sensors, PLC, HMI and machine communication, so the whole line runs under one control logic.

Conveyor Systems / Curves and Transfers
Conveyor curves and transfers, engineered
into the line.

Conveyor curves and transfers are the directional handling modules that change the path or orientation of a case without manual handling: 90° transfers, curves, turntables and pop-up transfers.
TRF
Industrial Automation engineers them as part of the
TRF
Intraflow
platform, together with the straight runs, buffers, routing and controls around them.

Key facts about conveyor curves and transfers
Item
Curves and transfers
What it is
Directional handling module family of the
TRF
Intraflow
platform, engineered into a complete conveyor system for each plant
Standard modules
90° transfers, curves, turntables, pop-up transfers
Items conveyed
Cases, cartons, trays, totes and other packaged units
What it does
Changes the direction or orientation of the product without manual handling
Works together with
Straight conveying, flow management, routing, elevation and system integration modules
Controls and interfaces

Throughput and spacing

How it is supplied

What are conveyor curves and transfers?
Curves and transfers are the parts of a conveyor system that make a case change direction or orientation. In
TRF
Intraflow
they form the Directional Handling module family, with four standard modules: 90° transfers, curves, turntables and pop-up transfers.
Straight conveying
carries product from one point to the next, and these modules decide where that path bends, splits or turns.
Directional handling is short in length and large in effect. A direction change is where a case is least stable and where spacing is most easily lost, so engineering those points with the right module and the right controls helps keep cases flowing.
Which directional handling module fits your layout?
The right module depends on the turn the layout requires, the unit being conveyed and what happens to the case immediately afterward.
TRF
confirms the choice for each position with your product, layout and line rates during the application review.
Module
What it does
Typical position in the line
Curve
Carries the case around a bend on a continuous path
Around columns, walls and equipment, or where a line changes bay
90° transfer
Moves the case from one conveyor to another at a right angle
Where two lines meet or a line branches, and at the entrance to a work station
Turntable
Rotates or redirects the case to a new orientation or direction
Where the orientation of the case matters or one input feeds several directions
Pop-up transfer
Lifts a transfer mechanism to move the case sideways, then lowers it
Where a case must leave a straight line without stopping the line behind it
90° Transfers
A 90° transfer is the standard way to build an L-shaped path, to feed a line from the side and to branch a flow into a work station or a second lane. Because the case leaves one section and enters another, the design has to control the moment of transfer: the speed of both sections, the gap between cases and the position of the case as it crosses.
Curves
Curves let a line follow the shape of the building, going around columns, walls, equipment and other lines without breaking the flow into separate sections. Their advantage is continuity: the case never changes conveyors, so there is no transfer point to manage. A long case behaves differently in a bend than a short one, so the curve is selected for the unit and the spacing the line must keep.
Turntables
A turntable is used where orientation matters, for example when a case must present a specific face to a scanner, a labeler or a palletizer, or where one input must feed several outputs at different angles in a compact space. Sensors and the PLC detect the case, start the rotation and release it at the right moment, so operators do not have to time it by eye.
Pop-Up Transfers
A pop-up transfer lets a single conveyor feed a second lane, a palletizing position or a station without stopping the flow behind it. It is common where several destinations share one line, and it works closely with the
merge, divert and sorting
modules: the pop-up is the physical action, and the routing logic that triggers it is engineered by the same team.
Why do cases jam at conveyor transfers?
Cases jam at transfers when speed, spacing and timing are not engineered together with the modules on both sides. A case that arrives too close to the one ahead can collide with it, a case that arrives off-center can catch a guide, and a module that has not returned to its ready state stops the next case.
An engineered transfer follows a controlled sequence:
Approach.
A straight section, usually powered roller or belt, carries the case toward the module at a controlled speed and spacing.
Detection.
A sensor identifies the case as it approaches, and the PLC prepares the module.
Direction change.
The curve carries the case around the bend, the transfer moves it onto the next conveyor, the turntable rotates it or the pop-up lifts and moves it sideways.
Release and recovery.
The case leaves in the position and spacing the next section needs, and the module returns to its ready state in time for the next case.
Where spacing must be restored, metering and gapping from
accumulation and flow control
work before and after the turn. That is why
TRF
designs directional modules with the system and not one by one.
Where do curves and transfers go between the case packer and the palletizer?
Curves and transfers go wherever the path of the case has to change, and between the packaging machine and the pallet that usually means these positions:
After the case packer.
The first module often turns the case from the discharge direction of the machine into the direction of the main conveyor. Cases can come from
TRF
Rapidpack
robotic case packing
,
TRF
Onepack
, which erects, packs and seals cases in one machine
,
TRF
Droppack
gravity case packing
or equipment already installed.
Around obstacles.
Curves guide the flow around columns, walls and other equipment, so the line follows the building. Combined with
elevation conveyors
, the path can also use the vertical space of the plant.
At line junctions and branch points.
90° transfers and pop-up transfers bring cases from several lines onto a common conveyor or send them toward more than one destination.
At inspection and labeling stations.
Turntables and transfers present the case to a scanner, checkweigher or labeler in the orientation the device needs.
Before the palletizer.
The last module sets the orientation of the case at the pick position, which affects how the
TRF
Cobot
,
TRF
Compact
,
TRF
Speed
or
custom palletizer
builds the pallet pattern. The right palletizer is defined by line setup and speed.
Sensors, PLC and HMI, barcode and vision interfaces, machine communication and palletizer interfaces tie every module to the machines around it, so each curve or transfer behaves as part of the line and not as a stand-alone device.
Curves and transfers belong to
TRF
Intraflow
. For exposed food or hygienic zones, see
TRF
Cleanflow
; both platforms are compared on the
conveyor systems page
. Extended to case packing and palletizing, the same approach becomes
end-of-line integration
.
Questions
Frequently asked questions
What Is the Difference Between a 90° Transfer and a Curve?
A curve carries the case around a bend while it stays on the same conveyor. A 90° transfer moves the case from one conveyor onto another at a right angle. Curves give continuity, with no transfer point to manage, while transfers make it possible to join, split or feed lines.
TRF
chooses between them based on the unit, the layout and the spacing needed.
Which Industries Use Conveyor Curves and Transfers?
Any plant that moves packed cases, cartons, trays or totes toward a palletizer or a warehouse, especially where several packaging areas share one palletizing zone. That includes
food processing
,
beverage
,
consumer packaged goods
,
protein processing
,
frozen food
,
pet food
and
personal care
manufacturers.

```

case flow.

Elevation conveyors move cases, cartons, trays and totes between floors and levels of a plant, using incline and decline conveyors, elevators, lowerators and vertical transfers.
TRF
Industrial Automation engineers them as modules of the
TRF
Intraflow
platform, integrated into the customer's line and controlled together with the machines around them.

Key facts about elevation conveyors
Item
Elevation modules
What they are
Standard modules of the
TRF
Intraflow
platform that move packaged units between levels
Module types
Incline and decline conveyors, elevator and lowerator, vertical transfer
Items conveyed
Cases, cartons, trays, totes and other packaged units
Where they run

Typical reasons to use them

How they are supplied

What are elevation conveyors, and when does a line need them?
Elevation conveyors are the part of a conveyor system that changes the height of the product path, so cases can climb to a higher level, descend to a lower one or cross a space a flat conveyor cannot use. In
TRF
Intraflow
, the elevation family has three standard modules: incline and decline conveyors, elevator and lowerator, and vertical transfer.
Plants usually need elevation because the building was laid out around other things first: the packaging hall is on a mezzanine and the palletizer sits at floor level, or a forklift aisle separates the end of the line from the pallet area. Without elevation, the alternative is people carrying cases, which consumes labor hours and puts repetitive lifting on your team.
A line is a good candidate for elevation modules when cases change level between machines, when floor space between packaging and palletizing is limited or blocked by aisles, utilities and existing equipment, or when cases are still carried by hand at a level change.
Which elevation module fits: Incline, elevator, lowerator or vertical transfer?
The right elevation module matches the unit, the height change, the footprint and the flow around it.
TRF
selects among them during layout engineering, not from a catalog.
Module
What it does
When it fits
Incline conveyor
Carries units continuously upward along a sloped path
The height change can be spread over a longer horizontal run
Decline conveyor
Carries units continuously downward along a sloped path
Cases come down from a mezzanine or raised packaging area to floor level
Elevator
Lifts units to a higher level in a compact vertical footprint
Little floor space is available for the height change
Lowerator
Lowers units to a lower level in a compact vertical footprint
Cases descend to a lower level and the horizontal run is short
Vertical transfer
Moves units vertically between two conveyor levels at a defined point
Two flows at different levels must exchange product at one location
Incline and Decline Conveyors
An incline or decline conveyor is a sloped section that moves cases continuously between two heights, with no stop at the level change. It is the simplest way to change level when the plant has enough horizontal distance. A sealed corrugated case, an overwrapped tray and a reusable tote behave differently on a slope, so the conveying surface is selected for the unit and the slope is confirmed against the case data. The section belongs to the same
straight conveying
architecture as the rest of the line.
Elevator and Lowerator
An elevator lifts cases to a higher level and a lowerator brings them down, both in a compact vertical footprint. They are the choice when floor space is the scarce resource, because they use the height of the building instead of a long horizontal run. The flow at an elevator is grouped rather than continuous, so the module is paced against the
accumulation and flow control
that feeds and follows it.
Vertical Transfer
A vertical transfer moves units between two conveyor levels at a defined point. It fits where two flows at different heights have to exchange product, and it usually connects with
merge, divert and sorting
modules so cases from different levels reach the right destination.
Where does an elevation conveyor go in the line?
An elevation conveyor goes wherever the product path has to change level, and its position matters as much as the module. The typical positions are these:
Right after the case packer.
Cases leave a packing cell on a raised platform or mezzanine and descend to floor level on a decline or lowerator.
Over an aisle or around equipment.
A path blocked at floor level by a forklift aisle, a utility corridor or an existing machine rises above it and comes back down on the other side, with an incline and a decline, or an elevator and a lowerator, as the pair.
Before the palletizer.
When the palletizer sits at a different height than the line that feeds it, the last modules adjust the level, then meter and orient the cases for the pick position.
At the warehouse boundary.
Where the plant hands product to a warehouse interface at a different height, elevation modules adjust the level and the controls exchange data with the warehouse system where the project requires it.
Around every level change, spacing is set before the module, curves and transfers re-orient cases after it, and a buffer absorbs pauses so the module keeps working when the palletizer stops for a pallet change. The elevation module does not run on its own timer: it knows when the zone ahead is full and when the zone behind has product waiting.
Can an elevation conveyor integrate with my palletizer and case packer?
Yes. Elevation modules connect to case packers and palletizers through the same system integration modules as the rest of
TRF
Intraflow
: sensors, PLC and HMI, machine communication and palletizer interfaces. Each interface is defined during project engineering, so the machines and the conveyors agree on spacing, orientation, signals and what happens when one side stops.
Upstream, case packing.
Cases can come from the
TRF
Rapidpack
robotic case packer
, from the
TRF
Onepack
integrated case packer
, which erects, packs and seals in one machine, from the
TRF
Droppack
drop packer
, or from equipment already installed.
Downstream, palletizing.
Cases are presented to the
TRF
Cobot
collaborative palletizer
, the
TRF
Compact
compact palletizer
, the
TRF
Speed
high-speed palletizer
or a
custom palletizer
. Which one fits depends on your line setup and speed.
Elevation touches these machines in three ways: spacing, because the level change must not disturb the palletizer cycle; timing, because the buffer around the module absorbs a pallet change so the case packer keeps running; and information, because barcode or vision identification stays attached to each case until the routing or palletizing decision. The
Avon case study
covers conveying, case handling and vertical transport.
Elevation for cases belongs to
TRF
Intraflow
. For exposed food or hygienic zones, see
TRF
Cleanflow
; both platforms are compared on the
conveyor systems page
. Extended to case packing and palletizing, the same approach becomes
end-of-line integration
.
Questions
Frequently asked questions
What Is the Difference Between an Incline Conveyor and an Elevator?
An incline conveyor carries cases continuously along a sloped path and needs enough horizontal distance to gain height. An elevator lifts cases in a compact vertical footprint and suits plants where floor space is the constraint.
TRF
selects between them during layout engineering, based on the unit, the height, the footprint and the rate of the line.
What Is a Lowerator Conveyor?
A lowerator is the module that brings cases down to a lower level in a compact vertical footprint, the counterpart of an elevator. It is typically used when cases leave a packing area on a raised platform or mezzanine and must reach floor level. In
TRF
Intraflow
, it is paired with accumulation and metering so the flow stays paced after the level change.
Can Elevation Conveyors Keep Barcode or Vision Data Attached to Each Case?
Yes, where the project requires it.
TRF
Intraflow
includes barcode and vision interfaces, and the controls keep the identification of each case as it moves through the modules, including a level change. That allows sorting, rejecting and routing decisions to be made after the elevation module, with the data each case must carry defined during project engineering.
Which Industries Use Elevation Conveyors?
Manufacturers that run packaging and end-of-line processes on more than one level, or in plants where floor space is limited.
TRF
Intraflow
serves mid-sized and large manufacturers in
food processing
, beverage,
CPG
, protein,
frozen food
,
pet food
, personal care and industrial sectors.

End-of-line automation: One partner,
one line.
One partner, one design and one point of accountability for case packing, palletizing and conveying.
End-of-line automation is the engineering of case packing, conveying and palletizing as one connected system, instead of separate machines bought from different suppliers and joined together during startup.
TRF
Industrial Automation designs, builds, installs and supports that complete line as one project, from the product to the finished pallet.

What is end-of-line automation?
End-of-line automation covers every step after primary packaging: case packing, case conveying and routing, palletizing, and the handoff to stretch wrapping, labeling or warehouse systems. Engineered as one system, these steps move product from the packaging line to the finished pallet under one coordinated design.
In practice, an end of line packaging system has three jobs:
Pack.
A top-load case packer groups packaged products, such as pouches, pillow bags, flow packs, thermoformed packages, trays, cartons, cups and tubs, and loads them into shipping cases.
Move.
Conveyors carry the cases, buffer them when the next machine pauses, merge lines, divert cases to the right destination and change level where the layout requires it.
Palletize.
A robotic palletizer builds the pallet pattern, with pallet handling and slip sheets where the project includes them, and hands the pallet to wrapping, labeling or the warehouse.
The real question is whether the three jobs are designed together. That decision shows up months later: whether the case packer keeps running during a pallet change, whether a second line can be added without rebuilding the conveyors, and who answers when the flow stops between two machines.
In hygienic food operations, the flow starts earlier.
TRF
Cleanflow
sanitary conveyors
carry exposed product between processing, inspection and primary packaging, and the packaged product then enters the sequence above. One project can cover both zones as a single coordinated material-flow system.
Can a single supplier engineer, build and install the entire end of line?
Yes. One partner for end of line automation solutions means one engineering scope, one control concept and one responsibility for the result.
TRF
supplies case packing, conveying and palletizing as one integrated line and manages engineering, manufacturing, controls, installation and commissioning within one coordinated delivery model.
For the buying committee, the practical differences are these:
Engineering.
Case rate, buffer size, pack pattern, pallet pattern and interfaces are calculated against each other, not machine by machine.
Controls.
One control concept covers the machines and the conveyors between them, instead of several platforms that have to be made to communicate during startup.
Schedule.
One supplier coordinates manufacturing, delivery and installation, with fewer handoffs between companies.
Responsibility.
One supplier answers for how the machines perform together, instead of each supplier answering for its own machine.
Support.
Technical support follows training and handover, so the partner that built the line stays involved after startup.
What is the difference between an integrated case packer and separate erecting, packing and sealing machines?
An integrated case packer erects, packs and seals the case in one machine, while separate machines split those three steps across different equipment, often from different suppliers.
TRF
Onepack
is the integrated option, and complete end-of-line projects can also include case forming and closing around
TRF
Rapidpack
robotic case packing
.
Case forming, loading and sealing share the same case, the same recipe and the same rate. Bought as separate machines, they bring separate change parts, separate control systems and several points of contact for one operation. In one machine, they run with one interface, fewer integration points and less floor space.
TRF
supplies case erecting only as part of an integrated solution, never as a stand-alone case erector, which keeps one partner responsible for the whole packaging flow.
Can new equipment integrate with the palletizer, conveyors and wrapping line you already have?
Yes, within the engineering scope of each project.
TRF
systems are designed for machine communication and for interfaces with conveyors, palletizers, stretch wrapping, labeling and warehouse systems. Each interface with existing equipment is defined during project engineering, including the signals exchanged at every boundary and who is responsible for each side of it.
This matters in brownfield plants, where the end of line grows in layers: a filler that stays, a conveyor run that has to be rebuilt, a palletizer that has reached its limit. A realistic integrated project starts by mapping what stays, what is replaced and where the boundaries are. From there, automated case packing and palletizing systems can be added to the line without forcing the plant to replace equipment that still does its job.

Which industries use end-of-line automation?
Plants that pack food, beverage, CPG, chemical, pharmaceutical and e-commerce products use end-of-line automation. The principles are the same everywhere, while the product, the pack pattern, the sanitation routine and the temperature change what the line looks like.
Protein processing
: sanitary conveying for exposed product, case conveying, case packing and palletizing, including frozen areas.
Food processing
and
frozen food and ice cream
: cold areas, high SKU counts and several lines meeting at one palletizing area.
Bakery and snacks
and
confectionery
: flexible packages and frequent changeovers.
Dairy and cheese
and
beverage
: case and tray flows from several lines consolidated before palletizing.
Pet food
,
chemicals
and
household and cleaning products
: heavier units and dense pallet patterns.
Pharmaceutical
and
personal care and cosmetics
: small products and short runs.
CPG
,
co-packers
,
paper and packaging
and
e-commerce and distribution
: frequent changes in product mix.

Can an End-of-Line Project Start With One Machine and Grow Later?
Yes. Each
TRF
palletizing and case packing platform can operate on its own and then integrate into a complete end-of-line system, with conveying added as an engineered system when volume grows. A plant can automate case packing first and complete the line with palletizing later. Planning the full flow at the start keeps each step compatible with the next one, including floor space, buffers and controls.
Does End-of-Line Automation Make Sense for a Lower-Speed Line?
Yes. Palletizing starts at about 1 to 10 cases per minute with
TRF
Cobot
, and case packing covers applications from about 20 products per minute with
TRF
Onepack
and
TRF
Droppack
. Lower-speed lines often begin by removing manual case packing or hand palletizing, then connect the steps as volume grows, with each machine validated for its own application.
How Much Does an End-of-Line Automation Project Cost?
Each integrated line is quoted for its full scope, because cost depends on the platforms selected, the number of lines, line speed, case weight, pallet pattern, conveyor length and routing, pallet handling and the interfaces with existing equipment. The

What Information Should I Prepare Before an End-of-Line Project Review?
Prepare product dimensions, weight and packaging format for each SKU family; products per minute on each line and the number of lines; case dimensions, weight and pack pattern; pallet type, pattern and height; layout drawings with floor space, ceiling height, columns and aisles; shifts, planned growth and operating temperature; and the equipment the line must connect to.

Why do manufacturers choose a hygienic food conveyor?
Food processors choose
TRF
Cleanflow
for the path between processing and packaging. These are the six reasons.
Hygiene
Sanitary design is focused on direct food-contact applications and demanding washdown environments.
Robustness
Heavy-duty stainless-steel construction is engineered for repeated washdown cycles in wet, cold and demanding plants.
Cleanability
Open, accessible construction simplifies sanitation and helps reduce cleaning time.
Uptime
A hygienic architecture supports faster sanitation cycles and a reliable return to production.
Product Integrity
Gentle, controlled handling protects exposed food products during transfer.
Turnkey Delivery
One partner is responsible for engineering, manufacturing, controls, installation and commissioning.
What is a sanitary conveyor, and how is it different from a standard industrial conveyor?
A sanitary conveyor, also called a hygienic conveyor, is designed so that every surface the product touches, and every surface that can collect soil, can be cleaned and inspected as part of the plant's sanitation routine. In a food plant the conveyor sits inside the hygienic zone, so it is judged by how fast and how completely it can be cleaned, not only by how much product it moves.
The difference from a standard industrial conveyor is the whole architecture, not the motor or the belt alone: open frames instead of closed boxes, sloped and self-draining surfaces instead of flat ledges, continuous welds instead of crevices, and belts and guides that come off for cleaning. A food grade conveyor system adds food-grade belting: on the Washdown line, the hygienic conveyor belts are modular plastic or monolithic, selected for the product.
TRF
Cleanflow
extends
TRF
automation upstream into food processing. It is designed around sanitation, accessibility, product integrity and uptime rather than transportation alone, and each stainless steel conveyor path is engineered as one hygienic system that conveys, inclines, transfers, distributes and merges product.
Which applications fit sanitary conveyor systems?
TRF
Cleanflow
fits applications where sanitation or product handling limits production. Typical applications:
Processing to packaging transfer
of exposed cuts, portions or prepared foods.
Inspection feeds
, carrying product from processing to inspection before packaging.
Distribution
of one product stream to several packing stations.
Merging
product from several processing points into one packaging line.
Inclines and declines
between processing and packaging equipment.
Wet and cold production areas
, where stainless construction has to withstand repeated washdown cycles.
The platform is built for mid-sized and large food processors in
protein processing
(beef, pork, poultry and seafood),
frozen food
,
dairy and cheese
and
food processing
, including ready-to-eat products, wherever sanitation, washdown, product integrity and uptime are critical.
Is your line ready for a sanitary conveyor system?
Your line is a strong candidate for
TRF
Cleanflow
when most of these statements are true:
Exposed food moves between processing, inspection and packaging.
Sanitation time limits how soon the line returns to production.
Product tips, sticks or breaks at transfer points.
Some areas are cleaned daily with water and chemicals, while others are cleaned dry.
Existing conveyors trap water or product, or are hard to reach for cleaning.
You want one partner from engineering to commissioning.
Before quoting,
TRF
reviews five inputs with your team: the product (dimensions, weight, temperature and condition), the process rate, layout drawings with ceiling heights and drain positions, your sanitation program and chemicals, and the equipment the conveyor feeds or receives from.

What is a dry conveyor system?
A dry conveyor system is a conveyor engineered for food production zones that are cleaned without water, using dry methods such as vacuuming, brushing and wipe-down, while still handling food or food-contact product. It is a sanitary conveyor, not a general-purpose one: the hygienic logic is the same as in a washdown line, but the cleaning method and the electrical protection are different.
The distinction matters because many food plants run both environments. A plant may wash down the area where raw product is processed and keep the area where dry or low-moisture product is handled free of water on purpose. Using a general industrial conveyor in the dry zone leaves cavities, ledges and fasteners that a crew cannot clean with a vacuum and a cloth.
TRF
Cleanflow
Dry sits between those two mistakes. It is designed for dry and low-moisture food-processing environments where cleanability, product protection and reliable operation are required, applying the same platform philosophy as the washdown line to a zone with a different sanitation routine.
What is the difference between dry-clean and washdown conveyor lines?
A dry-clean line is cleaned with vacuum, brushing and wipe-down, and a washdown line is cleaned with water, chemicals, high pressure and high temperature. Choose the dry conveyor system for industrialized foods with moderate, infrequent cleaning, and the washdown line for animal protein and similar products sanitized daily. The deciding factor is the sanitation program of that specific area, not the plant as a whole.
Question about the zone

If the answers are mixed, the zone needs a closer look. Areas that are dry most of the time but receive occasional wet cleaning, and areas next to a wet zone, are cases where
TRF
engineering reviews the sanitation procedure with your team before specifying the line. When both are needed, the project uses a
TRF
Cleanflow
Washdown line for high-pressure cleaning zones
where water is used and the Dry line where it is not.
What makes a conveyor easy to clean in a dry zone?
A conveyor is easy to dry-clean when a crew with a vacuum, a brush and a cloth can reach every surface that matters without dismantling it. These are the design targets of
TRF
Cleanflow
Dry:
304 stainless-steel construction
, for a surface that tolerates repeated cleaning.
Open-frame architecture
, so the structure can be seen, reached and vacuumed from more than one side.
Reduced horizontal surfaces and contamination points
, because in a dry zone flat ledges collect powder, crumbs and fines.
No unnecessary cavities or product accumulation points
, which removes the places where product builds up out of sight.
Easy-access belt and components
, so the belt underside and the area below it can be cleaned without dismantling the conveyor.
Food-grade belting
, selected for the product and for the way the line will be cleaned.
Sealed stainless fasteners where appropriate
, which keeps threads and gaps out of the product zone.
Food-grade lubricants where required
, for points that need lubrication inside a food handling area.
Easy vacuum, brushing and wipe-down access
, designed around the tools a dry-clean crew actually uses.
Dry cleaning has a different failure mode from wet cleaning. In a washdown zone, the risk is water and residue trapped where they cannot drain. In a dry zone, the risk is product that accumulates unnoticed, or a crew that reaches for a hose because dry methods cannot reach a hidden surface. Both come from construction that was never designed to be cleaned, and the features above address that directly.
What electrical protection does a dry conveyor need?
A dry conveyor needs electrical protection matched to a zone that is cleaned without hoses but still has to tolerate dust, wiping and incidental moisture. On
TRF
Cleanflow
Dry, the design target is IP65 electrical components as a baseline, with protected electrical and pneumatic routing.
Routing matters as much as the rating. Cables and pneumatic lines are run so they do not create ledges above the product zone, so they can be wiped or vacuumed like the rest of the conveyor, and so they stay protected from damage during production and cleaning.
When does an enclosed product path make sense?
An optional enclosed or covered product path makes sense when the product must be protected from the environment, or the environment from the product. It is a project decision, not a default.
The trade-off is access: any cover has to open or come off for cleaning and inspection.
TRF
engineering evaluates the covered path together with your sanitation procedure.
TRF
Cleanflow
dry or
TRF

?

conveyor systems page
compares both platforms.
Both lines belong to the
TRF
Cleanflow
sanitary conveyor system
. Once the product is packed,
TRF

TRF

for repetitive flexible packages or
TRF

robotic case packing

end-of-line automation engineered as one system
.

What changes between a washdown conveyor and a standard industrial conveyor?
A washdown conveyor changes every construction detail that decides whether water, chemicals and residue can be removed completely, while a standard industrial conveyor is designed mainly to move product from point to point. The difference is not the belt alone. It runs from the frame profile and the welds to the motors, the cable glands and the control enclosure.
Design point
Typical standard conveyor
TRF
Cleanflow
Washdown design target
Frame
Closed profiles chosen for load, not for drainage
304 stainless open-frame hygienic architecture, 316 optional
Joints
Bolted joints that leave crevices
Continuous weld construction
Surfaces
Flat horizontal ledges that hold water
Sloped, self-draining surfaces with minimized horizontal ledges
Hollow members
Open tube ends and cavities
No exposed hollow-frame openings
Mounting
Components bolted flat against the frame
Hygienic stand-offs and stainless-steel fasteners
Guides and wear strips
Fixed in place
Removable for cleaning
Drives
General-duty motors and gearboxes
Washdown-duty motors and gearboxes
Electrical
General industrial protection
IP66 / IP69K components in direct washdown zones, NEMA 4X control enclosure, sealed sensors and connectors
These differences show up where plant managers look every day. Sanitation time grows with every ledge, gap and tool-dependent part. Residue survives on surfaces that cannot be reached or drained. And motors, sensors and connectors that are not built for direct spray fail earlier and stop the line.
What features reduce cleaning time on a hygienic conveyor?
The features that reduce cleaning time are the ones that remove the places where water and residue collect and that let crews reach every surface, without tools where practical. On
TRF
Cleanflow
Washdown, the hygienic architecture is built from these design targets:
Open-frame hygienic architecture
, so spray and light reach the frame from more than one side.
Continuous weld construction
, which removes the crevices that bolted joints leave behind.
Sloped structural surfaces and minimized horizontal ledges
, so water and debris run off instead of pooling.
Self-draining surfaces and reduced water and product traps
, so the conveyor dries faster after the final rinse.
No exposed hollow-frame openings
, which keeps water and residue out of cavities that cannot be inspected.
Hygienic stand-offs
, which hold components away from the frame so the gap between them can be cleaned.
Quick-release belt tension and tool-less belt lifting or removal where practical
, so crews reach the belt underside and the frame below it.
Removable guides and wear strips
, which come off for cleaning instead of hiding residue behind them.
Full access for inspection and sanitation
, so a sanitation supervisor can verify the result before the line is released.
Which belt and electrical components does a washdown conveyor use?
A washdown conveyor uses a washdown-compatible, food-grade belt and electrical components protected for the position they occupy in the spray path. On
TRF
Cleanflow
Washdown, the belt is modular plastic or monolithic depending on the product, selected with how product is loaded, transferred and inclined, and with how the belt will be lifted or removed during sanitation.
Transfer points between belts, and between the conveyor and inspection or packaging equipment, are engineered in the same layout, because transfers are where exposed product is most likely to tip or be damaged. Gentle, controlled handling protects the product's appearance and integrity from processing to packaging.
The electrical design targets for direct washdown zones are washdown-duty motors and gearboxes, IP66 / IP69K electrical components, a NEMA 4X control enclosure, sealed sensors and connectors, washdown-rated cable glands and hygienic cable routing. Protection is specified by position: a motor under the belt in the direct spray path needs a different level than a sensor above the line or an enclosure placed outside the wash zone. Cables are routed so they drain and can be cleaned, instead of lying flat on the frame and becoming new harborage points.
How does conveyor design affect uptime in a meat or poultry plant?
Conveyor design decides how long sanitation takes and how reliably the line restarts afterward, In beef, pork, poultry and seafood plants, daily washdown is part of every production day, so a conveyor that is slow to clean or fails after cleaning costs production time.
TRF
Cleanflow
Washdown addresses that in four ways:
Uptime.
A hygienic architecture that supports faster sanitation cycles and a reliable return to production.
Reliability.
Robust stainless-steel construction designed for wet, cold and demanding food-processing environments.
Product integrity.
Gentle, controlled handling that protects exposed product during transfer.
Integration.
A defined connection between processing, inspection, primary packaging and the downstream automation.
A typical project for a protein or frozen food plant combines both zones of the plant in one coordinated
TRF
material-flow system:
TRF
Cleanflow
Washdown moves exposed product from processing through inspection to the primary packaging machines.
Packaged product is packed into cases, for example by
TRF
Rapidpack
robotic case packing
on high-mix lines.
TRF
Intraflow
moves, accumulates and routes the cases to palletizing.
A
TRF
palletizer builds the pallet, including
TRF
Subzero
for areas below 32°F
in cold-chain plants.
The same logic applies across the priority industries of the platform:
protein processing
,
frozen food
, ready-to-eat and
food processing
, and
dairy and cheese
. In each of them, the washdown conveyor sits where exposed product moves between processing and packaging.
What is the difference between
TRF
Intraflow
and
TRF
Cleanflow
?
TRF
Cleanflow
moves exposed food and
TRF
Intraflow
moves packaged goods. Use
TRF
Cleanflow
when the conveyed item is exposed food or the conveyor sits in a hygienic or washdown zone, and use
TRF
Intraflow
when the item is a case, carton, tray, tote or other packaged unit moving between automation processes. Inside
TRF
Cleanflow

Questions
Frequently asked questions
What Does IP69K Mean and Why Does It Matter for Washdown Conveyors?
IP69K is an ingress protection rating for equipment exposed to close-range, high-pressure, high-temperature water jets, which is what motors, sensors and connectors in the spray path face during every washdown. It matters because a component that lets water in fails early and stops the line. On
TRF
Cleanflow
Washdown, IP66 / IP69K components in direct washdown zones are a design target, confirmed per project.
What Is a NEMA 4X Enclosure and When Is It Required?
A NEMA 4X enclosure protects electrical equipment against hose-directed water, splashing and corrosion. A NEMA 4X control enclosure is a design target on
TRF
Cleanflow
Washdown, with the enclosure location defined in the project layout.
What Is the Difference Between 304 and 316 Stainless Steel in Conveyor Construction?
Both are stainless grades used in food equipment. Grade 304 is the common choice for sanitary construction, and 316 offers higher corrosion resistance where the product or the cleaning chemicals are harder on the steel.
TRF
Cleanflow
Washdown has 304 stainless-steel sanitary construction as its design target, with 316 as an option for aggressive environments, chosen per project.
What Is a Washdown-Rated Motor and Gearbox?
It is a drive built to withstand frequent spraying with water and cleaning chemicals, with sealed housings and surfaces that shed water instead of holding it. On a washdown conveyor, drives often sit in the direct spray path. Washdown-duty motors and gearboxes are a design target on
TRF
Cleanflow
Washdown, selected for the zone where each drive is installed.
How Often Does a Sanitary Conveyor Need to Be Washed Down?
The plant's sanitation program sets the frequency, not the conveyor. Zones that handle animal protein and similar exposed products are typically sanitized daily with chemicals, high pressure and high temperature, while dry zones for industrialized foods are cleaned moderately and infrequently.
TRF
Cleanflow
Washdown is designed for frequent washdown environments, with the cleaning routine set by your sanitation program.
Who Builds Sanitary Conveyor Systems for Meat and Poultry Plants?
TRF
Industrial Automation engineers
TRF
Cleanflow
Washdown for beef, pork, poultry and seafood plants in the United States, delivered turnkey, from engineering and manufacturing to controls, installation and commissioning. The same partner can continue the project through case packing, conveying and palletizing.
