## /conveyor-systems/merge-divert-and-sorting/
==============================================================================
**title:** Merge and Divert Conveyors and Sorting Systems | TRF
**description:** Merge and divert conveyors from TRF route cases from several lines to the right palletizer or lane, with sorting and reject. Talk with our Sales Engineer.

```
Conveyor Systems / Merge, Divert and Sorting
Merge and divert conveyors for
case routing.
Routing modules that combine lines, send each case to its destination and remove nonconforming units.
Merge and divert conveyors are the routing part of a conveyor line: a merge combines cases from several lines onto one path, a divert sends a case to another lane, sorting directs each case to its destination and a reject station removes nonconforming units.
TRF
Industrial Automation engineers these modules inside
TRF
Intraflow
, always as part of a complete line.
Talk with our Sales Engineer
→
Custom engineering
Solutions designed around your line.
Turnkey integration
From concept to full production.
High reliability
Built for continuous operation.
U.S. market focus
Support across the United States.
Lifecycle support
Backed by
TRF
Care
.
Key facts about merge, divert and sorting
Item
Routing modules
What it is
The Routing module family of
TRF
Intraflow
, engineered into a complete conveyor system
Standard modules
Merge, divert, sorting and reject station
Items handled
Cases, cartons, trays, totes and other packaged units
Where it runs
Between secondary packaging, case packing, palletizing and warehouse interfaces
Main job
Bring several lines together, send each case to the right destination and remove nonconforming units
Identification
Barcode and vision interfaces where the project requires them
Throughput
No catalog speed figure; routing rates are defined per project around your line rates
How it is supplied
Always inside an engineered system integrated into the line, never as loose components
What is the difference between a merge, a divert and a sorting conveyor?
A merge combines cases from two or more conveyors onto one path, a divert moves a case from the main path to another lane, and a sorting conveyor sends each case to an assigned destination based on product data, usually read from a barcode or by vision. A reject station, the fourth routing module, takes out cases that fail a check.
In
TRF
Intraflow
, these four functions form the Routing family of standard modules, and they are useful only when the other families support them: a merge needs spacing, a sort needs identification and a divert needs somewhere for the case to go.
A conveyor routing system therefore has two layers. The physical layer is the hardware that joins two streams, moves a case sideways or pulls it off the line. The information layer is the sensors, the PLC and the data that tell the hardware what to do with each case.
TRF
engineers both layers together, because a routing decision made without reliable information sends cases to the wrong place, and reliable information is of no use without hardware that acts on it at line rate.
How does a conveyor merge combine several lines into one?
A conveyor merge combines cases from two or more conveyors onto one path, so several packaging lines can share the equipment downstream, most often one palletizing area. It is the module that makes multi-line consolidation possible.
A good merge is not just a place where two conveyors meet. If cases from both lines arrive at the same moment, they collide or jam. If one line always wins priority, the other backs up and its case packer stops. The merge is engineered with the modules around it:
accumulation and flow control
holds cases from each line, metering releases them at a controlled rate and gapping creates the space the merge needs. Control logic decides how the lines take turns, for example alternating, weighted toward the busier line or based on which palletizer position needs product.
Merging matters most when a plant wants to centralize palletizing. A
TRF
Speed
high-speed palletizer
serving several lines needs one steady, well-spaced stream, and the merge, with its buffers and metering, is what produces it.
When does a conveyor line need a divert?
A conveyor line needs a divert wherever one stream must split into two or more. The reasons vary by plant and are defined with you during the application review:
A second palletizing position, so a line keeps running when one palletizer or pallet position is busy.
A manual station, for cases that need hand handling, sampling or repacking.
A separate destination by SKU, customer or shipping route.
A reject lane, where cases that fail a check leave the main flow.
Balancing between two palletizers or two packing cells, so neither waits while the other is overloaded.
A divert has to act on a single case without catching the next one, which is why it depends on gapping and on sensors that confirm the case position. The mechanism is selected for the case size, weight and packaging and the rate of the line during engineering. A lane that is full must be known to the control system, so it can choose another path or slow the flow.
How does a conveyor sorting system identify each case?
A conveyor sorting system identifies each case with a barcode reader or a vision interface and then directs it to its assigned destination. Where a divert follows a simple rule, sorting follows information carried by the case itself:
Spacing.
Gapping separates the cases so each one can be read individually.
Identification.
A barcode reader or vision interface reads the label or recognizes the case.
Decision.
The PLC compares the data with the routing rules of the plant, which may come from a warehouse management system where the project requires it.
Action.
Diverts send the case to its lane, and the count per lane is tracked.
Exception.
A case that cannot be read or does not match any rule goes to the reject station instead of being guessed at.
Sorting helps keep SKUs from mixing on the pallet. When several lines feed a shared palletizer, each pallet must hold the correct product, and sorting is the step that sends the right case to the right pallet position.
What does a reject station do on a conveyor line?
A reject station removes cases that fail a check before they reach the palletizer or the warehouse, such as a case with an unreadable or mismatched code, a case that does not meet the expected profile or a case flagged by an upstream inspection device. A nonconforming case on a pallet can force rework, so removing it before palletizing keeps the pallet clean.
The reject station is placed downstream of the reader or inspection device, with enough spacing to act on one case and a lane that leads to a location an operator can reach. It is connected to the control system, so the line can record rejected cases where the project requires it.
Merge, divert, sorting and reject modules belong to
TRF
Intraflow
. For exposed food or hygienic zones, see
TRF
Cleanflow
; both platforms are compared on the
conveyor systems page
. Extended to case packing and palletizing, the same approach becomes
end-of-line integration
.
Questions
Frequently asked questions
Can Several Packaging Lines Share One Palletizer?
Yes. Merge modules bring the lines onto one path, with buffers and metering that give each line its turn, so a stop on one line does not have to stop the others, and sorting helps keep SKUs from mixing on the pallet. The sharing logic is engineered together with the palletizer interface:
TRF
Speed
is built to consolidate the flow of several lines, and a
custom palletizer
covers layouts beyond the standard platforms.
Does Sorting Require a Warehouse Management System?
No. Sorting can run on the product data read from the case and on rules held in the conveyor PLC. Where the plant uses a warehouse management system,
TRF
Intraflow
includes WMS and warehouse interfaces, so the routing rules can come from the plant's logistics system. That interface is defined during project engineering with the plant's IT team.
Can a Conveyor System Integrate With My Palletizer and Case Packer?
Yes. Routing modules carry each case from the machine that packs it, such as
TRF
Rapidpack
,
TRF
Onepack
or
TRF
Droppack
, to the palletizer or position that receives it, through defined interfaces. Signals such as lane full, palletizer ready and case rejected are engineered once, in a single control concept.
Which Industries Use Merge and Divert Conveyors?
Plants with several packaging lines or many SKUs use them the most, including
food processing
,
protein processing
,
personal care
,
CPG
,
beverage
and
pet food
manufacturers.
What Information Is Needed to Design a Routing System?
Layout drawings, line rates, case dimensions and weights, the number of lines and destinations, the way cases are identified and the equipment the conveyors must connect to. A list of your SKUs and the routing rules you use today is often the most useful document to start with. The application review turns this into the module arrangement and the control logic.

Sales Engineer will outline the merge, divert, sorting and reject modules your line needs, engineered as part of a complete

system. Start on the
```

```
handling lines.
Powered roller, belt and modular belt conveying engineered into one integrated line, from case packer to palletizer.
Straight conveying systems are the powered roller, belt and modular belt sections that carry cases, cartons, trays and totes along a straight path between machines. In lines from
TRF
Industrial Automation, they form the backbone of the
TRF

platform, engineered together with transfers, accumulation, routing and controls as one system.

Item
Straight conveying
What it is

TRF
Intraflow
platform, engineered into a complete conveyor system for each plant

What is straight conveying in a case handling line?
Straight conveying is the family of standard modules that move product in a straight line from one point of the plant to the next. In
TRF
Intraflow
it includes three module types: powered roller, belt conveyor and modular belt, each chosen for the case, carton, tray or tote it will carry.
It is easy to underestimate this family, because a straight run looks like the simplest part of a conveyor system. In practice it defines the character of the whole line. The surface under the case decides how the case behaves at a transfer, how it is held during accumulation and how it presents itself to a sensor or a palletizer. The wrong surface causes trouble that is hard to correct downstream.
Straight sections also join the other
TRF
Intraflow
functions into one continuous path.
Curves and transfers
change direction.
Accumulation and flow control
buffers and spaces the product.
Merge, divert and sorting
decides where each case goes.
Elevation conveyors
move product between levels. Every run is part of a system whose layout, controls and interfaces are engineered together, never a component shipped alone.
Which straight conveyor fits your product: Powered roller, belt or modular belt?
The right straight conveyor depends on the unit being conveyed, the path it follows and the function of that segment, and a single line often uses more than one type.
TRF
selects the module for each segment and confirms the choice with you during engineering.
Powered Roller Conveyor
A powered roller conveyor carries product on driven rollers and suits rigid units with a firm, flat bottom, such as corrugated cases, trays and totes. Because driven roller sections can be divided into independent zones, they are a natural base for zero-pressure accumulation, where each case waits in its own zone instead of pushing against the case ahead. Inside a
TRF
Intraflow
line, a roller conveyor system suits case packer discharge, buffering runs and palletizer infeeds, where control over the position of each case matters.
Belt Conveyor
A belt conveyor carries product on a continuous surface and suits packs that benefit from support across the full underside, including packs whose bottom is less rigid than a corrugated case. The continuous surface holds the unit in place through a long run or into an incline. Its start, stop and speed follow the flow logic of the whole line rather than reacting on their own.
Modular Belt
A modular belt conveyor uses a belt built from interlocking modules, giving the line a surface that can follow the path the layout requires.
TRF
specifies modular belt where a run must adapt to direction changes or where the belt surface itself needs to match the packaged product being carried.
Can a conveyor system integrate with my palletizer and case packer?
Yes. Straight conveying connects a case packer to a palletizer through a defined interface at each end, so the two machines run at their own rates while the conveyor absorbs the difference. Both interfaces are engineered in the same project as the conveyors.
At the case packer.
Cases come from case packing platforms such as
TRF
Rapidpack
, the robotic case packer
,
TRF
Onepack
, which erects, packs and seals cases in one machine
, and
TRF
Droppack
, the gravity case packer
, or from equipment already installed. The first straight section accepts the case at the discharge height, speed and orientation of that machine.
Along the run.
Straight sections carry cases past scanners, checkweighers and labelers at a steady spacing, form buffer runs with zoned powered roller sections and bring the output of several lines toward a merge.
At the palletizer.
The last sections present cases at the spacing and orientation the palletizer expects, whether it is the
TRF
Cobot
collaborative palletizer
, the
TRF
Compact
compact palletizing cell
, the
TRF
Speed
high-speed palletizer
or a
custom palletizer
. The right palletizer is defined by line setup and speed.
The controls make this work. Sensors detect each case where the line needs to know it is there, the PLC decides when each zone starts, stops and releases a case, the HMI gives operators a view of the line, and machine communication links the conveyors to case packers and palletizers. Barcode, vision and WMS interfaces are added where the project requires them. A straight section therefore behaves as part of the line, not as a stand-alone conveyor.
Can a conveyor system be customized to my exact plant layout?
Yes.
TRF
Intraflow
is custom engineered around the actual production flow, layout and operational requirements of each plant, using standard modules. Straight runs are laid out by working through these questions with your team:
What exactly is on the conveyor?
Case, carton, tray or tote, with dimensions, weight range and bottom construction. The bottom of the unit is the first input to the choice between roller, belt and modular belt.
How many SKUs and sizes share the line?
A line with many sizes needs modules and guides that adapt without adjustment by hand.
What are the line rates?
Rates at the case packer and the palletizer define spacing, section speed and the buffer between them.
How much space is available?
Bay width, ceiling height, columns, aisles and existing equipment set the path and whether elevation modules are needed.
What has to happen along the run?
Scanning, weighing, labeling, inspection and rejection each add requirements to the section that serves them.
The answers become the basis of the layout and of the quote for the complete system.
Straight conveying belongs to
TRF
Intraflow
. For exposed food or hygienic zones, see
TRF
Cleanflow
; both platforms are compared on the
conveyor systems page
.
Questions
Frequently asked questions
Can Roller, Belt and Modular Belt Conveyors Be Combined in One Line?
Yes. Many lines use more than one type, because each segment has a different job.
TRF
selects the module for each position and connects all of them to one control concept, so speed, start and stop follow the state of the whole line. The plant receives a single engineered system rather than a mix of independent sections from different suppliers.
Can a Single Supplier Engineer, Build and Install My Entire Conveyor System?
Yes. With
TRF
Intraflow
, engineering, manufacturing, controls, assembly, integration and commissioning are managed within one coordinated
TRF
delivery model, from concept to startup. The same partner can also supply the case packers and palletizers at both ends of the conveyors, so the interfaces between them are defined once, in one project.
How Does Straight Conveying Reduce Manual Case Handling?
Straight conveying carries cases under control from the case packer to the palletizer, so people do not have to move, turn or clear cases by hand between machines. Sensors and controls keep spacing and speed matched to both ends of the line, which supports steady throughput and less repetitive lifting.
Which Industries Use Straight Conveying Systems?
Any plant that packs product into cases, cartons, trays or totes and moves them to a palletizer or a warehouse.
TRF
Intraflow
serves mid-sized and large manufacturers in
food processing
, beverage, CPG, protein,
frozen food
,
pet food
, personal care and industrial sectors. The full list is on the
industries page
.
```

```
How do you choose packaging automation for your industry?
Start from your line, not the sector: the industry sets the conditions and the line sets the platform. Screen four variables before you shortlist equipment.
Speed.
Cases per minute decides among palletizers, and products per minute among case packers, using the figures in the table above.
Load and product behavior.
Case weight sets the palletizing platform. Repetitive flexible bags point to gravity packing, while delicate, variable or mixed products point to robotic packing.
Space and layout.
Brownfield plants with tight packaging areas favor compact cells and machines that combine functions, such as
TRF
Onepack
. Multi-line plants favor a centralized palletizing area fed by
TRF
Intraflow
.
Environment.
Exposed food and washdown zones point to
TRF
Cleanflow
, palletizing areas below 32°F to
TRF
Subzero
, and packaged-goods zones to
TRF
Intraflow
.
When more than one platform could work, choose the one that matches your throughput, production intensity and growth plans, not simply the lowest initial investment.
Case studies
TRF
publishes three customer case studies:
Custom Foods
, palletizing below freezing in De Soto, Kansas;
Lodge Cast Iron
, ergonomic case packing plus robotic palletizing in Tennessee; and
Avon
, conveying, case handling and vertical transport. All three are in the
case studies section
.
Questions
Frequently asked questions
Which Industries Need Sanitary Conveyors?
Industries that move exposed food or run conveyors in hygienic and washdown zones.
TRF
Cleanflow
lists beef, pork, poultry, seafood, frozen foods, ready-to-eat products, and dairy and cheese among its priority industries. Where the item conveyed is a case, carton, tray or tote, the platform is
TRF
Intraflow
instead, and a project that crosses both zones combines the two platforms in one coordinated material-flow system.
Which Industries Palletize Below Freezing?
Frozen food, ice cream, frozen prepared foods and the frozen areas of meat processing plants. For palletizing areas below 32°F, the platform is
TRF
Subzero
, the
TRF
palletizer built for those areas.

How Does Automation Help With Labor Shortages in Food Plants?
Automation takes over the repetitive end-of-line tasks that are hardest to staff: grouping and loading products into cases, moving cases by hand and building pallets. Operators move to supervision, quality checks and changeovers, and the line no longer depends on how fast people can lift. Repetitive lifting, bending and twisting also leave the end of the line.
How Do I Estimate the Payback of Automation in My Plant?
For palletizing, use the
palletizing ROI calculator
. The estimate divides the system cost, including maintenance, by the annual net savings: the labor cost of manual palletizing minus the electricity the robot uses. The result is an initial planning estimate that a
TRF
Sales Engineer completes with the costs of your project.
```

```
How does a case packer protect delicate or irregularly shaped products?
A case packer protects delicate products by picking without crushing, orienting without tearing film and placing packages in a pattern that protects them in transit. Snack cakes, cookies, crackers and chips break easily, and air-filled pillow bags behave very differently from rigid cartons.
TRF
Rapidpack
is designed for controlled handling of delicate, flexible or irregularly shaped products, with vision-guided robotic handling where products arrive in variable positions. Each product is validated with its real package before the end-of-arm tool and pack pattern are fixed.
Protection also depends on flow. Wrappers and baggers rarely deliver product at a steady rate: stops, restarts and rejects create gaps and surges. Accumulation, metering and gapping on
TRF
Intraflow
conveyors between machines let each machine run at its own pace, so the case packer receives a steadier flow instead of catching up.

How fast can a case packer change over between SKUs?
When the change is recipe-only, it is a selection at the operator panel: the case packer and palletizer load the stored pick pattern, case count and pallet pattern for the new SKU.
TRF
Rapidpack
is designed for recipe-based product and pattern changes with reduced mechanical adjustments, and
TRF
Cobot
supports multiple recipes, pallet patterns and SKU changes.
Some changes still involve hardware. A new case size may need guide adjustments on the case conveyor, and a very different product may need another end-of-arm tool. During the application review, list every product, case and pallet combination you run or plan to run, and confirm which changes are recipe-only. A plant with many changeovers a week benefits from robotic handling; a plant that runs one product per shift may get more from a simpler gravity solution.
Questions
Frequently asked questions
Can a Robotic Case Packer Handle Flow-Wrapped Bakery Products and Pillow Bags?
Yes.
TRF
Rapidpack
handles flow packs, pillow bags, pouches, trays, cartons and other small packaged products, with controlled handling for delicate, flexible or irregularly shaped items. Recipe-based changeovers let the same packer run several bakery and snack SKUs, which suits bakery case packing on high-mix lines.
TRF
engineering validates each product and pack pattern before quoting.
How Many Products per Minute Can a Robotic Case Packer Run?
TRF

is designed for applications of about 100 to 200 products per minute on medium- to high-speed lines. It can expand from a single robotic module to multiple robots as production grows, so packing capacity can follow new SKUs and higher volumes. The final rate is validated for each product, pack pattern and case.
```

```
How does zero-pressure accumulation work on a case conveyor?
Zero-pressure accumulation holds units in separate zones so they wait without pushing against each other, then releases them at the rate the next machine wants. On a beverage line, it keeps a pallet change, a wrapper fault or a label roll change from backing up the line.
TRF
Intraflow
includes accumulation and zero-pressure accumulation modules, plus metering and gapping. Sizing the buffer is an engineering calculation made in the layout phase: how long the downstream machine can stop before the line backs up, at the line rate, sets the accumulation length. Adding buffer later is harder than designing it in, and
TRF
Intraflow
is always engineered as a complete system, never sold as loose sections.

A
custom palletizer
is engineered when the unit, rate, weight or building layout falls outside the standard platforms. With
end-of-line integration
, the questions that sit between machines (which machine holds product when the wrapper stops, how a reject leaves without stopping the flow, how the warehouse knows a pallet is ready) are designed once and tested at commissioning.
How do you select a beverage palletizer?
A beverage palletizer is selected on three factors: units per minute, unit weight and the pallet pattern the load needs to stay stable. Rate comes first, especially in plants that consolidate several filling lines into one palletizing area, which points to
TRF
Speed
. Weight then filters the shortlist, using the measured weight of every unit rather than an estimate.
Pallet stability is the factor most often left for later. Layer patterns, interlocking, slip sheets, load height and the handoff to the stretch wrapper decide whether the pallet arrives intact. Share the pallet specification your distribution centers require, including height and overhang limits, before the layout is fixed.
Questions
Frequently asked questions
What Is the Difference Between a Merge, a Divert and a Sorting Conveyor?
A merge combines units from two or more conveyors into one flow, for example several filling lines feeding one palletizing area. A divert sends selected units to another path, such as a reject station. A sorting conveyor separates units by SKU or destination, often after reading a barcode.
TRF
Intraflow
includes all three as standard modules, engineered into one system.
What Is the Difference Between a Dedicated Palletizing Cell and a Centralized Multi-Line System?
A dedicated cell palletizes one line and is simpler to control and expand line by line. A centralized system receives units from several lines and builds their pallets in one area, saving floor space but needing merging, sorting and buffering logic.
TRF
Speed
scales from a dedicated cell to a centralized multi-line system, and
TRF
Intraflow
provides the routing between them.
Can a Palletizer Be Integrated With My Existing Conveyor and Wrapping Line?
Yes.
TRF
defines the interfaces with the machines already installed, from the bundler or case packer discharge to the stretch wrapper, labeling and warehouse systems.
TRF
Intraflow
carries the integration layer: sensors, PLC and HMI, barcode and vision interfaces, machine communication, palletizer interfaces and warehouse interfaces where applicable. Who owns each signal is agreed during project engineering.
How Do You Choose Between
TRF
Cobot
,
TRF
Compact
and
TRF
Speed
?
Choose by line speed, unit weight and floor space:
TRF
Speed
fits high-rate beverage lines and several lines feeding one area, for units within its weight range.
TRF
Compact
fits secondary lines and brownfield rooms where floor space is limited.
TRF
Cobot
fits smaller producers replacing manual stacking at lower rates.
TRF
validates the choice with the real unit weight and pallet pattern.
Can One Automation Partner Supply Palletizing, Case Packing and Conveyors for a Beverage Plant?
Yes.
TRF
supplies conveying and palletizing as one integrated end-of-line solution, with
TRF
Intraflow
at the core and
TRF
Cobot
,
TRF
```

```
Industries / Chemicals
Chemical packaging automation: Case packing, conveying and palletizing engineered
as one end of line.
Take heavy manual handling out of the end of the line with the palletizing platform matched to case weight, rate and layout.
Chemical packaging automation at the end of the line is the case packing, conveying and palletizing equipment that moves filled and closed cases of chemical products from packaging to a shipping-ready pallet.

End-of-line challenges in chemical plants
The end of a chemical packaging line combines heavy cases and long production schedules. Three challenges stand out.
Heavy Cases and Manual Handling
Heavy cases are awkward and repetitive to handle. Manual palletizing at a chemical plant is hard to staff and repeats the same lifting, bending and twisting all shift. It is also where a plant discovers that its filling line can run faster than its people can stack.
Case Variety on the Same Site
The same product family can ship in several case sizes. Each one needs its own end-of-arm tool setting, its own pallet pattern and sometimes its own pallet size, so the first screening question is which cases can share tooling.
Long Runs and Continuous Production
Many chemical lines run two or three shifts, or continuously, with long runs of the same product. That profile rewards throughput over flexibility: the palletizer must not become the bottleneck, and a pallet change must not hold up the line.
What is packed and palletized at the end of a chemical line?
Chemical plants palletize sealed shipping cases and bundles, and case pack consumer-size products first. Each unit has its own detailed page.

What payload does a palletizing robot need for chemical cases?
The robot must carry the case weight plus the end-of-arm tool, and for multi-unit picks, the combined weight of the units. The three standard
TRF
platforms cover loads up to about 22 lb, about 44 lb and about 66 lb, subject to end-of-arm tool and reach validation. Loads outside those ranges are reviewed by
TRF
engineering, including as a custom palletizer.
Case size and surface decide the tooling. Pallet stability is the next decision: interlocking patterns, controlled placement and slip sheets between layers keep heavy loads square in transit, and
TRF
Speed
supports slip sheet handling, automatic pallet handling and pallet discharge, as defined in project engineering. The
case palletizer
page covers tooling and patterns for sealed cases.

Questions
Frequently asked questions
What Is the Difference Between a Dedicated Palletizing Cell and a Centralized Multi-Line System?
A dedicated cell palletizes one line, at the end of that line. A centralized system brings several lines to one palletizing area through conveying, merging and accumulation.
TRF
Speed
scales from a dedicated cell to a centralized multi-line system, which suits chemical plants with several packaging lines.
TRF
validates the combined rate and case mix before quoting.
What Is Slip Sheet Palletizing and When Is It Needed?
Slip sheet palletizing places a sheet of paperboard or plastic under the first layer or between layers to stabilize the load and protect the units. It is specified for loads that shift.
TRF
Speed
supports slip sheet handling, automatic pallet handling and pallet discharge, and your pallet specification defines when the sheets are used.
Can a Compact Palletizer Be Installed in a Brownfield Plant With Limited Space?
Yes.
TRF
Compact
puts an industrial robot in a compact standardized cell designed for brownfield plants where floor space is a major limitation, and it keeps multiple SKU recipes and pallet patterns.
TRF
lays out the cell, the infeed and the pallet exchange around your columns, aisles and forklift paths during the application review.
How Many Cases per Minute Can a Robotic Palletizer Handle?
It depends on the platform.
TRF
Cobot
covers about 1 to 10 cases per minute,
TRF
Compact
about 5 to 15 and
TRF
Speed
about 10 to 35. These are typical ranges for commercial screening:
TRF
confirms the final rate for your cases, end-of-arm tool, pallet pattern and reach, and loads outside the ranges are reviewed by
TRF
engineering.
How Is Payback Calculated for Chemical Palletizing?
Payback depends on the labor hours the palletizer replaces, shifts per day, local labor cost and the installed cost of the system. The estimate divides the system cost, including maintenance, by the annual net savings: the labor cost of manual palletizing minus the electricity the robot uses. Long continuous runs keep utilization high. The
payback calculator
returns a first estimate.
```

```
What should a co-packer look for in end-of-line automation?
A co-packer should look for flexibility first, because the product mix belongs to the customers and contracts have horizons. A co-packer sells capacity, while a brand owner optimizes a known portfolio, so both buy the same three steps, case packing, conveying and palletizing, but justify the equipment differently. Brand owners are covered on the
consumer packaged goods
page.
In contract packaging automation, a line that runs pouches this quarter may run cartons next, and every contract can bring its own case size, count and pallet pattern. The questions that decide a purchase are how much flexibility a machine buys, how quickly it can be set up for a new product, and whether the investment can be spread across several contracts.
End-of-line challenges in contract packaging
The end of the line is where a co-packer's own labor concentrates: erecting cases, loading them, sealing them and building pallets to each customer's specification. Three challenges stand out.
High SKU Mix and Customer-Owned Specifications
A contract packager may run many products from several customers in the same week, each with its own carton, case count and pallet pattern. Automated lines absorb that variety with recipes and tooling validated for the whole product list, so the application review asks for every SKU, not only the fastest one.
Short Runs and Frequent Changeovers
In contract packaging, a run can last a few hours, so changeover time is a capacity number. Minutes lost at every change add up faster than any gain in rated speed, and a stored recipe lets any trained operator run the change instead of one experienced person.
Labor Across Shifts
Co-packers often add a second or third shift to cover peaks, and end-of-line positions are hard to keep filled. Automation reduces dependence on repetitive manual palletizing and moves operators to line setup, quality checks and changeovers.
What is packed and palletized in a co-packing plant?

Questions
Frequently asked questions
What Is a Cobot?
A cobot is a collaborative robot, an arm designed for shared workspaces at moderate speeds. In palletizing, a cobot picks each case from the infeed conveyor and places it on the pallet in a stored pattern.
TRF
Cobot
puts one in a standardized cell built for co-packers and contract manufacturers with short runs and frequent SKU changes.
Can a Cobot Palletizer Handle Multiple SKUs and Pallet Patterns?
Yes. Built for high-mix palletizing,
TRF
Cobot
stores multiple recipes, pallet patterns and SKU changes for lines of about 1 to 10 cases per minute with cases up to about 22 lb, so each customer's pattern can be stored and recalled.
TRF
confirms that the end-of-arm tool and reach cover every case in your mix. Higher rates move to
TRF
Compact
or
TRF
Speed
.
Can a Robotic Case Packer Handle Pouches, Trays and Thermoformed Packages in the Same Line?
Yes, within its validated range.
TRF
Rapidpack
handles pouches, pillow bags, flow packs, thermoformed packages, trays, cartons, cups, tubs and small packaged products, and changes products and patterns through recipes with reduced mechanical adjustments. That breadth is what makes flexible case packing serve several customers on one line.
TRF
validates the full product list before quoting.
What Is the Difference Between an Integrated Case Packer and Separate Erecting, Packing and Sealing Machines?
An integrated case packer erects the case, loads it and seals it in one coordinated machine.
TRF
Onepack
works this way, with one controls platform, fewer interfaces and a smaller footprint than three separate machines. For a contract packager, that means one machine to learn and maintain when the line changes customers.
Can a Robotic Palletizer Operate Below Freezing?
Yes, with a palletizer built for it.
TRF
Subzero
palletizes in frozen areas below 32°F, which fits co-packers that run frozen food or ice cream programs. The Custom Foods case study documents robotic palletizing below freezing.
TRF
validates the layout and the equipment for each cold application before quoting.
How Should a Co-Packer Calculate Payback on Packaging Automation?
Start with the labor hours the equipment replaces, shifts per day, local labor cost and the installed cost, including electricity and maintenance. Then calculate against utilization across contracts, not one product, count changeover time recovered, and compare the payback with the length of the contracts behind the line. The
payback calculator
gives a first estimate.
```

```
Does a case packer need vision-guided robotics for high-mix production?
Not always. Vision-guided robotic handling pays off when products arrive in variable positions, pack patterns change often or the package is appearance-sensitive, which describes many candy and chocolate lines. Repetitive bags packed in the same pattern all shift may not need it.
TRF
Rapidpack
uses vision-guided or indexed handling, depending on the application, and is designed for controlled handling of delicate, flexible or irregularly shaped products. Glossy, printed wraps show handling marks, so the end-of-arm tool is matched to the wrap and each product is validated with its real package before the pack pattern is fixed. When a bag is repetitive and tolerates a controlled drop,
TRF
Droppack
loads it by gravity, without robotic end-of-arm tooling.

How should a confectionery plant size case packing and palletizing?
Size the case packing and palletizing for the peak rate the plant commits to, then choose platforms that scale. A line sized only for an average rate runs at its limit whenever volume rises.

Questions
Frequently asked questions
How Does a Case Packer Protect Delicate or Irregularly Shaped Products?
By controlling how the package is picked, placed and stacked. Robotic picking with an end-of-arm tool matched to the wrap limits pressure on the package, placing rather than dropping avoids scuffs, and a pack pattern matched to the case keeps packages from rubbing in transit.
TRF
Rapidpack
is built around controlled handling, and
TRF
validates each product with real samples.
How Fast Can a Case Packer Change Over Between SKUs?
When the change is recipe-only, it is a selection at the operator panel: product size, count per case, case format and pallet pattern are stored and recalled.
TRF
Rapidpack
changes products and patterns with reduced mechanical adjustments. Some changes still need tooling or guide adjustments, so
TRF
reviews the full SKU list during the application study and states which changes are recipe-only.
How Many Products per Minute Can a Robotic Case Packer Run?
TRF
Rapidpack
is designed for applications of about 100 to 200 products per minute on medium- to high-speed lines. Because light confectionery units are rarely limited by payload, the number of picks per minute matters most, and the platform can add robotic modules as volume grows. The final rate is validated for each product, pack pattern and case.

How Is Payback Estimated for Confectionery Automation?
Payback depends on the labor hours the system replaces, shifts per day, local labor cost and the installed cost of the system. The estimate divides the system cost, including maintenance, by the annual net savings: the labor cost of manual palletizing minus the electricity the robot uses. The
```

```
End-of-line challenges in CPG plants
A growing catalog, promotional packs and rising volumes reach the end of the line before they reach the budget for more people. Four challenges define the end of the line in consumer goods plants.
SKU Proliferation and Frequent Changeovers
Consumer goods multiply: sizes, scents, flavors, club packs, value packs and retailer-exclusive configurations. Each one can bring its own case, count and pallet pattern, and every promotion or launch adds a changeover. When runs are short, changeover time is a capacity number, so recipes and tooling validated for the full product list matter more than peak speed.
Labor Across Shifts
Manual case packing and palletizing are among the hardest positions to keep staffed, and turnover is expensive when every new operator has to learn several products. Automation reduces dependence on repetitive manual work and moves operators to supervision, quality checks and changeovers.
Floor Space Shared Between Lines
Many CPG plants grew by adding lines to buildings laid out for something else. Compact cells, and machines that consolidate case erecting, packing and sealing, save floor space where every square foot is already assigned.
Multi-Plant Rollout
Brand owners run several plants and lines at once. A standardized platform reduces engineering complexity and lead time compared with a fully custom cell, and a first successful installation becomes the template for the next plant.
What is packed and palletized on a CPG line?
CPG lines case pack flexible, rigid and formed packages, then palletize sealed cases and boxes. Each format has its own detailed page.

How much automation does a CPG plant really need?
Automate the repetitive work without over-automating the process. A plant with a stable portfolio does not always need the highest-rate machine; it needs the machine that changes formats quickly, handles the widest product list and keeps running on every shift.
When more than one platform could technically work,
TRF
recommends the one that matches your required throughput, production intensity and five-year growth path, not simply the lowest initial investment. For a brand owner, that five-year path is the product plan. A platform that can be extended when a launch succeeds, such as
TRF
Rapidpack
adding robots or
TRF
Speed
growing into a multi-line system, can spare you a second project.
Standardizing on one family of platforms also helps across plants: the same cell, the same routines and the same partner make training and maintenance easier from one site to the next. The
robotic palletizer cost
page explains what drives the investment.
Case studies

Avon case study
documents conveying, case handling and vertical transport for a beauty and personal care manufacturer. The
Custom Foods case study
documents palletizing below freezing in De Soto, Kansas.
```

```
What features reduce cleaning time on a hygienic conveyor?
Open construction, surfaces that drain and parts that come off without tools reduce cleaning time, because the crew can reach, wash and inspect everything in the window between runs. In dairy and cheese, cleaning is production time.

comes in two lines because dairy plants have two kinds of zones, and the design targets below are confirmed for each project.
```

**title:** Distribution Center Palletizing | TRF
**description:** Distribution center palletizing and case handling for e-commerce and distribution: robotic palletizing with engineered conveying, from one partner.

```
Industries / E-Commerce and Distribution
Distribution center palletizing and case handling for
e-commerce and distribution operations.
Take manual lifting out of pallet building in distribution centers and keep outbound flow moving, with a platform matched to your case mix.
Distribution center palletizing is the automated building of outbound pallets from cases and boxes that arrive by conveyor in a distribution or e-commerce operation, replacing repetitive manual stacking at the end of the flow.
TRF
Industrial Automation supplies that step and the conveying that feeds it in the United States.

What does distribution center palletizing cover?
Distribution center palletizing covers three things: a robot that places cases in a stable pattern, conveying that presents them in the right order and rhythm, and controls that route cases to the palletizing points.
TRF
supplies all three as one engineered system of case handling automation.
In a manufacturing plant, palletizing closes a production line. In a distribution center, it closes a flow: cases and boxes converge from picking, packing, repack or sortation areas, and someone has to build a stable pallet for an outbound trailer. That work is repetitive, it peaks unevenly through the day, and it is hard to keep staffed.
Operational challenges in e-commerce and distribution
Distribution operations combine swinging volume, a wide case mix and limited space at the dock. Four challenges stand out.
Labor Availability and Seasonal Peaks
Volume swings by season, promotion and day of week, and the labor market does not swing the same way. Manual palletizing is where that gap shows first: overtime, temporary staff and training all rise when volume rises.
Case and Box Variety
A production line palletizes a handful of case sizes; a distribution center can see many more, with different weights, materials and top surfaces. The end-of-arm tool has to grip the full range, and the pallet pattern has to stay stable across every case size the cell is validated for.
Flow Rhythm and Accumulation
Sortation and packing send cases in bursts, while a palletizer works in cycles. Without accumulation, metering and gapping, the two rhythms fight each other and the flow stops at its slowest moment.
Space and Dock Flow
Palletizing cells compete with staging space, forklift aisles and dock doors, so footprint is a selection criterion. Compact cells fit where a traditional robotic cell would be difficult to install.
What is palletized at the end of a distribution flow?
Most units at the end of a distribution flow are boxes and sealed cases, followed by bags, bundles and trays. Repack areas add case packing before the pallet.

. These are typical ranges for commercial screening:
TRF
confirms the final rate for your case mix, tooling, pallet pattern and reach.
What Is the Difference Between a Dedicated Palletizing Cell and a Centralized Multi-Line System?
A dedicated cell palletizes one flow at one point. A centralized system brings several flows to one palletizing area through conveying, merging and accumulation.
TRF
Speed
scales from a dedicated cell to a centralized multi-line system, and
TRF
Intraflow
routes cases to the palletizing points. The choice depends on combined rate, distances and dock layout.
How Do Conveyors Connect to Warehouse Systems?
Through the controls layer of the conveying system.
TRF
Intraflow
includes sensors, PLC and HMI, barcode and vision interfaces, machine communication, palletizer interfaces and warehouse system interfaces where applicable.
TRF
defines the scope of each interface during project engineering, so the conveying system exchanges the signals and data your site's systems expect.
Do Distribution Centers Need Case Packing?
Only if the site groups products into shipping cases. Repack areas and club packs are the usual reasons.
TRF
case packers are all top load, and the fit is confirmed per application:
TRF
Onepack
erects, packs and seals in one machine,
TRF
Rapidpack
handles robotic packing, and
TRF
Droppack
loads repetitive flexible packages by controlled gravity.
How Is Payback Calculated for Distribution Center Palletizing?
Payback depends on the labor hours the palletizer replaces, shifts per day, local labor cost and the installed cost of the system, including electricity and maintenance. Distribution operations should add peak overtime, temporary labor and training, plus the outbound flow recovered at the dock. The
payback calculator
gives a first estimate.

TRF
```

```
What slows down the end of a food processing line?

Many products and short runs.
Pouches in the morning, flow-wrapped bars after lunch, cartons at night.

and

Repetitive manual handling.

Sanitation boundaries.

Line balancing.

accumulation, metering and gapping buffer the line, so a changeover at one machine does not have to stop the machine before it.

How does automation change for prepared Foods and ready-to-eat lines?
Prepared and ready-to-eat lines run many formats and SKUs on the same line, and exposed product needs hygienic handling before it is packaged.

What Is the Difference Between a Sanitary Conveyor and a Standard Industrial Conveyor?
A sanitary conveyor carries exposed food and is built to be cleaned:
TRF
Cleanflow
uses open-frame construction, reduced horizontal surfaces and food-grade belting as design targets. A standard industrial conveyor such as
TRF
Intraflow
moves packaged units (cases, cartons, trays and totes) between packing, palletizing and the warehouse. Most food plants need both, joined where the sanitation zone ends.
How Do You Choose Between a Robotic, an Integrated and a Gravity Case Packer?
Start with product behavior: a robotic case packer such as
TRF
Rapidpack
fits many SKUs, variable product positioning and delicate items. An integrated case packer such as
TRF
Onepack
erects, packs and seals in one machine for smaller lines. A gravity case packer such as
TRF
Droppack
fits repetitive flexible bags that tolerate controlled gravity loading.
TRF
evaluates all three for each product.
What Is the Difference Between a Dedicated Palletizing Cell and a Centralized Multi-Line System?
A dedicated cell palletizes one line next to its packer. A centralized system receives cases from several lines and builds their pallets in one area.
TRF
Speed
scales from a dedicated cell to a centralized multi-line system, and
TRF
Intraflow
merges, routes and accumulates the cases so the palletizer receives an organized flow.
TRF
validates the combined rate for each plant.
How Is Payback Estimated for Food Processing Automation?
Payback depends on the labor hours the system replaces, shifts per day, local labor cost and the installed cost of the system. The estimate follows the method of the A3 (Association for Advancing Automation) robot ROI calculator: the system cost, including 20 years of maintenance at 5% per year, divided by the annual net savings, which is the labor cost of manual palletizing minus the electricity the robot uses. The
palletizing ROI calculator
applies this method to your location, your shifts and the people who palletize by hand today.
```

## /industries/household-and-cleaning-products/
==============================================================================
**title:** Household Products Packaging Automation | TRF
**description:** Household products packaging automation after filling: case packing, conveying and palletizing for cases, pails and cartons, engineered as one line.

```
Industries / Household and Cleaning Products
Household products packaging automation for heavy cases, high SKU
counts and stable pallets.
Handle heavy cases, high SKU counts and stable pallets in household products plants.
Household products packaging automation is the end-of-line equipment that packs finished cleaning and household products into cases, conveys them and builds stable shipping pallets after filling, capping and labeling are complete.
TRF
Industrial Automation engineers that end of line as one system for manufacturers in the United States.
Talk with our Sales Engineer
→

Turnkey integration
From concept to full production.
High reliability

Support across the United States.

TRF

.
End-of-line challenges in household and cleaning products
Dense cases, a long list of scents and sizes, and strict customer pallet requirements all land on the same few operators at the end of the line. Four challenges stand out.
Heavy, Liquid-Filled Cases
Cases of detergent jugs, bleach and surface cleaners are dense for their size. Operators lift, turn and place every one, which means repeated lifting, bending and twisting at the pallet all shift. Case weight is also what decides the palletizing platform.
SKU Proliferation and Promotional Packs
One brand can ship the same formula in several sizes, scents and pack counts, plus club packs, seasonal promotions and private label runs. Each variation can mean a different case, pallet pattern and label orientation. Manual lines absorb that variety with extra hands; automated lines absorb it with recipes.
Upstream Speed Versus Downstream Rhythm
Fillers want a steady outfeed, while palletizing works in cycles: a layer forms, the robot places it, the pallet is discharged and replaced. Without accumulation between those rhythms, every pallet change becomes a short stop at the filler.
Floor Space in Existing Plants
In existing household products plants, the space behind the case sealer is often limited. A robotic cell has to fit between existing columns, aisles and forklift paths.
What is packed and palletized at the end of a household products line?
Household and cleaning products leave primary packaging as sealed cases, pails, cartons, pouches, tubs and bundles. Household products case packing handles the cartons, pouches and tubs; cases, pails, bundles and boxes go straight to the palletizer.

How does a robotic palletizer handle SKU or pallet pattern changeovers?
A robotic palletizer handles changeovers through recipes: the operator selects a stored recipe that holds the case dimensions, pick logic and pallet pattern for the new SKU. When the new case fits the same end-of-arm tool, the change happens mostly in software.
TRF
Cobot
and
TRF
Compact
support multiple recipes, pallet patterns and SKU changes, and
TRF
Rapidpack
changes products and patterns through recipes with reduced mechanical adjustments. On the conveyors,
TRF
Intraflow
adds barcode and vision interfaces, sorting and reject stations, so the right case reaches the right pallet when several SKUs share a line.
The end-of-arm tool is the physical side of the changeover. A gripper that handles a case of jugs may not handle a shrink-wrapped multipack or a pail with a handle, so
TRF
validates the tool against the full product list, not only the fastest SKU.
Integrated end-of-line automation for household and cleaning products
Integrated end-of-line automation means the case packer, the conveyors and the palletizer are designed as one line with one controls concept, instead of three machines from three suppliers connected on site. Interfaces are the weak points of any end of line, so
TRF
plans machine communication, sensors, PLC and HMI together, plus interfaces with stretch wrapping, labeling and warehouse systems where the project includes them.
Case forming belongs inside the line.
TRF
Onepack
erects, packs and seals in one machine, and complete projects can include case forming and closing with
TRF
Rapidpack
. One partner stays responsible from concept and engineering to installation and commissioning, and the
robotic palletizing
,
case packing
and
conveyor systems
pages show each family in detail.
Questions
Frequently asked questions
What Payload Does a Palletizing Robot Need for My Product?
The robot must carry the case weight plus the end-of-arm tool, and the combined weight for multi-case picks. In household products palletizing, liquid-filled cases are dense, so weight often decides the platform:
TRF
Cobot
handles cases up to about 22 lb,
TRF
Compact
up to about 44 lb and
TRF
Speed
up to about 66 lb. Heavier units are evaluated as a custom palletizer.
How Does Zero-Pressure Accumulation Work on a Case Conveyor?
Zero-pressure accumulation divides the conveyor into zones that stop and start independently, so waiting cases do not push against each other. When the palletizer changes a pallet, cases queue without pressure.
TRF
Intraflow
includes zero-pressure accumulation, plus metering and gapping to release cases to the palletizer in a controlled stream.
What Is a Drop Packer and How Does Gravity Packing Work?
A drop packer collates flexible packages and loads them into the case through a controlled drop, without a robot picking each unit.
TRF
Droppack
works this way for pillow bags, pouches, flexible bags and similar packages that tolerate controlled gravity loading. It has fewer complex components and no robotic end-of-arm tool to maintain.
What Is the Difference Between a Case Packer and a Case Erector?
A case erector forms a flat corrugated blank into an open case; a case packer loads products into that case.
TRF
does not sell stand-alone case erectors:
TRF
Onepack
erects, packs and seals the case in one machine, and complete projects can include case forming and closing with
TRF
Rapidpack
, so one partner owns the flow from flat blank to sealed case.
What Automation Reduces Labor Dependency in a Household Products Plant?
Palletizing and case packing are usually the first steps to automate, because they are the most repetitive and physically demanding tasks after filling. A robotic palletizer takes over stacking heavy cases, a case packer takes over loading units, and accumulation reduces the stops that call people back to the line. Operators move to supervision, quality checks and changeovers.
How Is Payback Estimated for Household Products Packaging Automation?
Payback depends on the labor hours the equipment replaces, shifts per day, local labor cost and the installed cost of the system. The estimate follows the method of the A3 (Association for Advancing Automation) robot ROI calculator: the system cost, including 20 years of maintenance at 5% per year, divided by the annual net savings, which is the labor cost of manual palletizing minus the electricity the robot uses. Household plants should also count filler uptime recovered and shorter changeovers. The
payback calculator
gives a first estimate.
```

```
End-of-line challenges in paper and packaging plants
Finished paper goods change from one order to the next, and manual bundle stacking is heavy, repetitive work that is hard to staff. Two challenges stand out.
Order-Driven Production and Pallet Patterns
Converting plants run orders. Bundle counts, unit dimensions, pallet sizes and stacking instructions can change with each customer, so recipe-driven pallet patterns matter more than peak speed.
Several Converting Lines, One Shipping Area
Many plants run several converting lines that discharge toward the same shipping doors. Conveying then becomes a routing problem: merging, accumulating, diverting to the right palletizer and keeping each order together.
What is packed and palletized at the end of a paper or packaging line?
Most paper and packaging units go straight from converting to the pallet. Case packing enters when retail units have to be grouped into shipping cases.

What is the difference between a dedicated palletizing cell and a centralized multi-line system?
A dedicated palletizing cell sits at the end of one converting line and palletizes only that line. A centralized system brings several lines to one palletizing area through conveying, so one coordinated system builds all the pallets.
TRF
Speed
scales from a dedicated cell to a centralized multi-line system.
The conveying makes centralization work.
TRF
Intraflow
merges units from several lines, accumulates them through pallet changes and short stops, diverts each unit to the right palletizer or staging lane and keeps orders together with barcode and vision interfaces. Inclines, elevators and lowerators move units between levels where floor space is shared with forklift traffic.
The choice depends on the combined rate, the distance between lines, how often orders change and the layout of the shipping area. Compare the options on the
robotic palletizing
and
conveyor systems
pages.
Integrated end-of-line automation for paper and packaging

engineers case packing, conveying and palletizing as one system and takes responsibility from concept and engineering to manufacturing, controls, installation and commissioning. Interfaces with stretch wrapping, labeling, warehouse systems and the converting equipment already installed are defined in the same project.
Case forming follows the same logic: it is supplied inside
TRF
Onepack
or a complete line, never as a stand-alone case erector.
TRF
engineers conveyors as part of the system, because a conveyor bought as a loose component still leaves the integration work with the plant.

Can a Conveyor System Be Customized to My Exact Plant Layout?
Yes.

Intraflow
is custom-engineered around each plant's production flow, layout and operational requirements, using standard modules for straight conveying, curves and transfers, accumulation, merging, diverting, sorting and elevation.

What Is the Difference Between a Merge, a Divert and a Sorting Conveyor?
A merge combines units from several lines into one lane, a divert moves units from one lane to another, and a sorting conveyor sends each unit to its destination based on identification such as a barcode. In converting plants, these modules route each order to the right palletizer or staging lane.
TRF
Intraflow
includes all three.

Payback depends on the labor hours the palletizer replaces, shifts per day, local labor cost and the installed cost of the system. Converting plants should also count order changeover time.
```

```
End-of-line challenges in personal care and cosmetics plants
Cosmetics packaging automation has to absorb the variety that marketing creates upstream, usually with the same number of operators at the end of the line. Three challenges stand out.
A SKU List That Grows Faster Than the Line
One filling line can feed dozens of finished goods: shades, fragrances, sizes, single units and multipacks. Each variant can change the case count, the case size and the pallet pattern. Automation pays off here only when a change is a recipe on a screen instead of a mechanical setup.
Small, Decorated Packages That Mark Easily
Cosmetics packaging is decorated, so scuffs and dents matter. Handling at the end of the line has to be controlled, from gentle picking and guided placement into the case to conveying that does not press packages against each other.
Manual Packing, Manual Palletizing and Mixed Channels
In many plants the last two operations are still manual: grouping units into cases and stacking cases onto pallets. The same product can leave as a retail shipper, a club pack or an e-commerce case, each with its own pattern. These repetitive tasks absorb temporary labor during seasonal peaks, and they can be automated without touching the primary packaging equipment.
What is packed and palletized at the end of a personal care line?
Personal care products reach the end of the line as cartons, flow-wrapped units, pouches, cups and tubs, which are case packed, and as sealed cases, boxes and bundles, which go to the palletizer. Each format has its own detailed page.

Can a Robotic Case Packer Handle Pouches, Trays and Thermoformed Packages in the Same Line?

Does a Case Packer Need Vision-Guided Robotics for High-Mix Production?

Can a Conveyor System Integrate With My Palletizer and Case Packer?

What Is the Difference Between a Merge, a Divert and a Sorting Conveyor?
A merge combines cases from several lines into one lane, a divert moves cases from one lane to another, and a sorting conveyor sends each case to its destination based on identification such as a barcode. In personal care plants, these modules let retail, club and e-commerce cases follow their own lanes to the palletizer.
How Is ROI Estimated for Switching From Manual to Robotic Case Packing?
ROI depends on the labor hours case packing takes today across shifts, local labor cost and the installed cost of the system, plus the capacity the line gains when packing stops limiting it. Seasonal and promotional peaks should be counted in annual hours, not peak-week hours. A
```

```
What is a drop packer, and how does gravity packing work?
A drop packer groups bags into the pack pattern and lets them drop into the open case in a controlled way, so no robot picks each bag.
TRF
Droppack
works this way: bags arrive from the bagger, are collated into the count and arrangement the case needs and are loaded from the top. It packs pet food, VFFS packages and similar free-flowing packaged products.
Because the product moves vertically, the machine can keep a compact footprint, and without an end-of-arm tool there are fewer complex components to maintain. The condition is a repetitive pack pattern and a package that tolerates controlled gravity loading. When a pouch needs a specific orientation or the plant changes patterns several times a day, robotic case packing with
TRF
Rapidpack
is the closer match; when a smaller line also wants case forming and sealing,
TRF
Onepack
combines the three steps. For a pet food case packer project,
TRF
needs the bag dimensions and weight, the fill behavior, the target rate, bags per case, the case style and the SKU list.

What payload does a palletizing robot need for my product?
The robot must carry the bag or case plus the end-of-arm tool, and for multi-unit picks the combined weight.
TRF
screens its palletizers by load:
TRF
Cobot
up to about 22 lb,
TRF
Compact
up to about 44 lb and
TRF
Speed
up to about 66 lb, always subject to end-of-arm tool and reach validation. Bags beyond that range move the project to a custom palletizer.
Payload is only the first decision for a pet food palletizer. Slip sheets can be added between layers on
TRF
Speed
when the load requires it. The
bag palletizer
page covers grippers and patterns in detail.
Questions
Frequently asked questions
What Is Slip Sheet Palletizing and When Is It Needed?
Slip sheet palletizing places a thin sheet between pallet layers to separate them and help keep the load stable. Pet food plants use it when bags settle or shift, or when a customer requires it.
TRF
Speed
handles slip sheets together with automatic pallet handling and pallet discharge, so operators do not have to stop other work to insert sheets by hand.
What Is the Difference Between a Dedicated Palletizing Cell and a Centralized Multi-Line System?
A dedicated cell palletizes one line next to its bagger or case packer. A centralized system receives bags or cases from several lines and builds their pallets in one area, each line keeping its own pallet pattern.
TRF
Speed
scales from a dedicated cell to a centralized system, and
TRF
Intraflow
merges and accumulates units from each line.
TRF
validates the combined rate.
How Does a Robotic Palletizer Handle SKU or Pallet Pattern Changeovers?
Through stored recipes. A new bag size, pack pattern or pallet pattern becomes a selection on the operator screen rather than a mechanical rebuild.
TRF
Cobot
and
TRF
Compact
support multiple recipes and pallet patterns. Some changes still need parts, such as a different gripper for a new bag format, so the application study lists which SKUs share tooling before purchase.
Can One Automation Partner Supply Palletizing, Case Packing and Conveyors for a Pet Food Plant?
Yes.
TRF
supplies case packing, conveying and palletizing as one integrated end-of-line solution:
TRF
Droppack
,
TRF
Rapidpack
or
TRF
Onepack
pack the cases,
TRF
Intraflow
moves and routes them, and
TRF
Cobot
,
TRF
Compact
or
TRF
Speed
build the pallet. One partner is responsible from layout to startup, and
Parts and Service
supports
TRF
-supplied equipment afterward.
How Is Payback Estimated for Pet Food Packaging Automation?
Payback depends on the labor hours the system replaces, shifts per day, local labor cost and the installed cost of the system. The estimate follows the method of the A3 (Association for Advancing Automation) robot ROI calculator: the system cost, including 20 years of maintenance at 5% per year, divided by the annual net savings, which is the labor cost of manual palletizing minus the electricity the robot uses. The
palletizing payback calculator
applies this method to your location, your shifts and the people who palletize by hand today.
```

```
that change products often.

End-of-line challenges in pharmaceutical plants
The end of a pharmaceutical packaging line is shaped by product variety rather than by volume. Three challenges stand out.
Short Runs and Frequent Product Changes
A packaging suite can run several products, strengths and pack counts in one week, and each one can change the case size and the pallet pattern. Equipment that needs a mechanical rebuild for every change consumes time the schedule does not have, so recipe-based changeovers matter more than peak speed.
Tight Floor Space After Packaging
Packaging suites are built around the primary packaging equipment, and the space left at the end of the line is often a narrow corridor. A traditional robotic cell can be hard to install there, so footprint, operator access and pallet exchange have to be solved together.
Repetitive Lifting by Trained Staff
In many plants, the people stacking cases at the end of the line are the trained operators who also run the packaging equipment. Manual palletizing takes them away from the line and repeats the same lifting, bending and twisting all shift. Automating the pallet keeps qualified staff on supervision, quality checks and changeovers.
What is packed and palletized at the end of a pharmaceutical line?
Finished pharmaceutical products reach the end of the line as cartons, trays, pouches or sealed shipping cases. Cartons and smaller packages go through a pharmaceutical case packer and then to the palletizer; sealed cases go straight to the palletizer. Each format has its own detailed page.

Why does a cobot palletizer fit a high-mix pharmaceutical line?
In pharmaceutical palletizing, a cobot palletizer fits because demand at the end of the line is defined by variety and space, not by throughput.
TRF
Cobot
stores multiple recipes, pallet patterns and SKU changes, so a product change is a recipe selection instead of a mechanical rebuild.
Footprint is the second reason.
TRF
Cobot
is built for areas where a traditional robotic cell would be difficult to install, which describes many packaging suites. Its standardized architecture and intuitive operation also lower the barrier to a first automation project: operators learn one cell and one routine.
The third reason is the investment profile.
TRF
Cobot
is a lower-entry-cost path to robotic palletizing, particularly in one- to three-shift operations. When the rate grows beyond the collaborative range, the application moves to
TRF
Compact
; several suites feeding one palletizing area point to
TRF
Speed
. The
robotic palletizer cost
page explains what makes up the installed cost.
Integrated end-of-line automation for pharmaceutical plants
Integrated end-of-line automation means case packing, conveying and palletizing are engineered as one system by one supplier, instead of three machines bought separately and connected by the plant.
TRF
Rapidpack
,
TRF
Onepack
or
TRF
Droppack
pack the cases,
TRF
Intraflow
moves and routes them, and
TRF
Cobot
,
TRF
Compact
or
TRF
Speed
build the pallet.
For a pharmaceutical plant, fewer suppliers means fewer interfaces to define and one counterpart when something has to be adjusted.
TRF
Intraflow
includes barcode and vision interfaces, machine communication and reject stations, while systems that print or verify codes stay with their own suppliers.
TRF
engineering defines the signals exchanged with them for each project.
Case erecting and sealing are supplied inside
TRF
Onepack
or a complete line, never as stand-alone case erectors, and conveyors are engineered as part of a system.
Questions
Frequently asked questions
How Many Cases per Minute Can a Robotic Palletizer Handle?
It depends on the platform.
TRF
Cobot
covers about 1 to 10 cases per minute,
TRF
Compact
about 5 to 15 and
TRF
Speed
about 10 to 35. Lower-speed pharmaceutical lines fit the
TRF
Cobot
range. These are typical ranges for commercial screening:
TRF
confirms the final rate for your product, end-of-arm tool, pallet pattern and reach.
What Automation Reduces Labor Dependency in a Pharmaceutical Packaging Line?
Palletizing and case packing are usually the first steps to automate, because they are the most repetitive manual tasks after primary packaging. A cobot palletizer takes over case stacking, a case packer takes over grouping units into cases, and conveying removes manual carrying between them. Operators move to line supervision, quality checks and changeovers.
How Much Floor Space Does a Compact Palletizing Cell Require?
It depends on the pallet size, the number of pallet positions, how full pallets leave the cell and how operators reach it.
TRF
Cobot
and
TRF
Compact
are designed as compact cells for plants where space at the end of the line is limited.
TRF
draws a layout with the cell, the infeed conveyor and operator access during the application review.
What Is the Difference Between an Integrated Case Packer and Separate Erecting, Packing and Sealing Machines?
An integrated case packer erects the case, loads it and seals it in one coordinated machine.
TRF
Onepack
works this way, with one controls platform, fewer interfaces and a smaller footprint than three separate machines connected by conveyors. Separate machines make sense when each step must be sized or replaced independently.
Can a Palletizer Be Integrated With My Existing Conveyor and Wrapping Line?
Yes.
TRF
Intraflow
supplies the accumulation, machine communication and palletizer interfaces that connect a new cell to the conveyors already installed, and
TRF
Speed
projects can include interfaces to wrapping, labeling and warehouse systems. The scope of each interface is defined during project engineering, so one partner is responsible for how the new cell fits the line.
How Is Payback Estimated for a Robotic Palletizer?
Payback depends on the labor hours the palletizer replaces, shifts per day, local labor cost and the installed cost of the system. The estimate follows the method of the A3 (Association for Advancing Automation) robot ROI calculator: the system cost, including 20 years of maintenance at 5% per year, divided by the annual net savings, which is the labor cost of manual palletizing minus the electricity the robot uses. The
payback calculator
returns an initial estimate that a
TRF
Sales Engineer completes with your project costs.
```

```
What Is the Difference Between Preventive and Predictive Maintenance?
Preventive maintenance follows a schedule: inspections, adjustments and part replacements at planned intervals. Predictive maintenance plans the work from the condition of the equipment, so a part is changed when inspection shows wear, not only because the calendar says so.
```
