**title:** Privacy Policy | TRF Industrial Automation
**description:** How TRF Industrial Automation collects, uses, shares and protects information from trfautomation.com, and your privacy rights by region.

```
Privacy Policy
TRF
Industrial Automation
privacy policy.
Effective date: October 1, 2026. Version 1.0.
Talk with our Sales Engineer
→
Key facts about this policy
Topic
What applies
Who this policy covers
TRF
Industrial Automation, operating trfautomation.com from Lenexa, Kansas
Nature of the operation
Business to business only.
TRF
does not sell products or services to individual consumers through this website
What is collected
Information submitted through forms (for example, name, business email, phone, company and project details), technical and analytics data, and cookie identifiers
Sensitive personal information
Not collected. No form on this site asks for sensitive categories of personal information
Legal standard applied site wide
The strictest applicable US state standard, generally the California model, applied to every visitor regardless of location
Global Privacy Control
Honored automatically as an opt-out signal, as required in California and Colorado
Do Not Sell or Share
TRF
does not sell personal information. A "Do Not Sell or Share My Personal Information" link is available in the footer of every page
Regions covered by this policy
United States (including state-specific rights)
How to reach
TRF
about this policy
Contact
TRF
Industrial Automation
Policy status
Effective October 1, 2026, Version 1.0
Scope of this policy
This Privacy Policy applies to trfautomation.com, the website of
TRF
Industrial Automation, including every category, platform, product, industry, resource, tools and location page reachable from this domain. It covers information collected when a visitor browses the site, submits a form, uses the payback calculator, or otherwise interacts with a feature hosted on trfautomation.com.
This policy does not cover third-party websites linked from this site, such as a trade show landing page or an industry directory profile.
TRF
Industrial Automation is a U.S. operation, part of the Torfresma group, and this policy describes practices for the United States, Canada and Mexico. It does not describe the practices of Torfresma's operations outside the United States.
The operation described in this policy is entirely business to business.
TRF
designs, builds and supports robotic palletizing, case packing and conveyor systems for manufacturers, and this website is built for buyers evaluating that equipment on behalf of their employer, not for individual consumers purchasing goods for personal use. That distinction matters under US law, because most state privacy laws treat information tied to someone acting in a business capacity differently from information tied to a consumer, as explained in the state law section below.
Information we collect
TRF
Industrial Automation collects information in three ways: information a visitor submits directly, information generated automatically by technology, and information from cookies and similar tracking technologies.
Information Submitted Through Forms
Forms appear across the site wherever a visitor requests a specification, asks a question, or uses a calculator. Examples include the contact form on
Contact
TRF
Industrial Automation
, specification and consultation requests on platform and product pages across the
robotic palletizing
,
case packing
and
conveyor systems
pages, and inputs to the
palletizing ROI calculator
. Each form states, at the point of collection, what information is requested and what it will be used for.
The information requested is limited to what is needed to respond to the inquiry: typically a business name, a business email address, a phone number, a job function, a company name, and details about the project or the question being asked. A field is included only if
TRF
has a defined use for it.
TRF
does not ask visitors for personal details unrelated to a business inquiry, such as a home address, a government identification number, or financial account information.
Information Collected Automatically
When a visitor browses trfautomation.com, the site and its hosting and analytics infrastructure automatically record technical information about that visit. This can include the browser type and version, the device type, the operating system, the pages viewed, the time spent on each page, the referring website, an approximate location derived from the IP address, and similar diagnostic information. This information helps
TRF
understand which pages are useful to buyers, identify broken links or slow pages, and improve the site over time. It is analyzed in aggregate and is not used to build a profile of an individual visitor for advertising purposes.
Cookies and Similar Technologies
Cookies are small files a website stores in a browser to remember information between visits or during a single session.
TRF
Industrial Automation uses cookies for three general purposes: to make the site function correctly, to understand how the site is used, and, where enabled, to measure the performance of marketing activity. The full breakdown of cookie categories, and how to control them, is in the cookies section below.
How we use information
TRF
Industrial Automation uses the information described above for a defined set of business purposes, and for no purpose beyond what a visitor would reasonably expect from submitting a form on a manufacturer's website.
Purpose
What it looks like in practice
Responding to inquiries
Answering a specification request, scheduling a technical consultation, or sending requested information
Operating the site
Keeping forms, navigation, the payback calculator working correctly and securely
Measuring and improving the site
Understanding which pages, platforms and resources are useful to buyers, and finding technical issues
Communicating with prospects and customers
Sending information relevant to an inquiry already made, such as a follow up on a specification request
Supporting existing
TRF
equipment owners
Handling requests routed to the after-sales team once a visitor identifies as an existing customer
Meeting legal and security obligations
Maintaining records of consent, responding to lawful requests, and protecting the site and its data from misuse
TRF
Industrial Automation does not use information collected through this site to make automated decisions that produce a legal or similarly significant effect on a visitor, such as automatically approving or denying a commercial relationship. Every inquiry submitted through the site is reviewed by
TRF
's commercial or technical team before a response is sent.
Legal bases for processing
Where a legal basis for processing is required,
TRF
Industrial Automation relies on one or more of the following: performance of steps requested by the visitor before entering into a business relationship, such as responding to a specification request;
TRF
's legitimate interest in operating, securing and improving a business website, balanced against the visitor's privacy interests; consent, where consent is the basis required, such as for non-essential cookies or for specific marketing communications; and compliance with a legal obligation, such as maintaining records required by applicable law.
Because
TRF
's website serves a business to business audience, much of the information collected relates to a person acting on behalf of their employer rather than to that person in a personal or household capacity. Several US state privacy laws draw a distinction based on that capacity, and the practical effect of that distinction is described in the state law section below.
How we share information
TRF
Industrial Automation does not sell personal information, and this website has no mechanism for a visitor to sell or transfer personal information to
TRF
. Information collected through the site is shared only in the following circumstances.
Recipient category
Why information is shared
What is shared
Customer relationship management platform
To route, track and respond to inquiries submitted through forms
The information the visitor submitted, such as name, business email, phone and inquiry details
Website hosting provider
To operate, secure and deliver the website
Technical information needed to serve pages and store form submissions
Web analytics providers
To measure site usage and improve content
Automatically collected technical and usage information, subject to cookie consent choices
Email delivery service
To send a requested response, a resource, or a commercial email with an unsubscribe option
Business contact information needed to deliver the message
Parent company, Torfresma
Coordination between
TRF
and the manufacturer, for example on a project that involves manufacturing input
Business inquiry details relevant to the project, handled under this policy
Legal, regulatory or law enforcement authorities
Where required by law, or to protect the rights, property or safety of
TRF
, its visitors or the public
The minimum information necessary to respond to the request
A successor in a business transaction
If
TRF
is involved in a merger, acquisition or sale of assets
Information held at the time of the transaction, under confidentiality obligations
Each vendor that processes personal information on
TRF
's behalf, including the hosting and analytics providers above, is bound by a data processing agreement that limits how the information may be used. This policy does not name specific vendors, because the list of processors can change as the site evolves; the categories above describe every type of recipient that touches visitor information.
International data transfers
TRF
Industrial Automation is Torfresma in the United States. The group's manufacturing organization, Torfresma Industrial Ltda, is based in Brazil. Because
TRF
and its service providers operate across borders, information submitted through this website may be transferred to, stored in, or processed in a country other than the one where the visitor is located, including the United States, where
TRF
's hosting infrastructure is based.
Where information collected in the United States is shared with the Brazilian manufacturing organization for a specific project,
TRF
applies safeguards appropriate to that transfer, and the receiving organization is bound to protect the information consistently with this policy.
Data retention
TRF
Industrial Automation retains personal information only for as long as it is needed for the purpose it was collected, to meet legal, accounting or reporting requirements, or to resolve disputes. Inquiry and form data is generally retained for the length of an active business relationship or an active sales process, plus a defined period afterward to support follow up and recordkeeping; analytics data is generally retained in a form that supports trend analysis without long-term storage of individual-level detail; and cookie consent records are retained for as long as needed to demonstrate compliance with consent requirements. At the end of the applicable retention period, information is deleted or de-identified.
Security
TRF
Industrial Automation applies administrative, technical and physical safeguards designed to protect information collected through this website, including encryption of information in transit and, where applicable, at rest, access controls limiting who can view stored information to personnel who need it to do their job, and audit logging of access to systems that hold visitor information. No website or online system can guarantee complete security, and
TRF
maintains a documented incident response process to identify, contain and address a security incident, with defined notification steps where notification is required by law.
Your privacy rights under US state law
The United States does not have a single federal privacy law comparable to the general data protection frameworks used in some other countries. Instead, privacy regulation in the United States is set state by state, and the number of states with a comprehensive privacy law in effect has grown quickly: by 2026, twenty states had such a law in force, with Indiana, Kentucky and Rhode Island the most recent to take effect, each starting January 1 of that year. Most of these state laws follow a similar structure, commonly described as the Virginia model, which creates a shared core of rights and obligations across most states.
The Rights These Laws Generally Provide
Across the states with a comprehensive privacy law, an eligible individual generally has the right to: confirm whether a business is processing information about them; access the information a business holds about them; correct inaccurate information; delete information, subject to exceptions such as legal recordkeeping obligations; receive a portable copy of the information they provided; and opt out of specific uses, such as targeted advertising, the sale of personal information, or profiling that produces a legal or similarly significant effect. Most of these laws also give the individual the right to appeal a denied request.
The California Exception
California is the one state whose comprehensive privacy law, the California Consumer Privacy Act as amended by the California Privacy Rights Act (CCPA/CPRA), does not exempt business contact information from its scope. Every other state with a comprehensive privacy law provides an exemption for information tied to a person acting in an employment or business to business capacity, meaning the business contact details
TRF
collects through this site would often fall outside those other states' scope entirely. California does not draw that line, so a business contact in California retains rights under the CCPA/CPRA even when the interaction is entirely business to business.
Because
TRF
's website audience can come from any state, and because detecting a visitor's state reliably is not possible (state-specific detection depends on geolocation, which is defeated by a virtual private network and creates a real risk of applying the wrong state's rule to the wrong visitor),
TRF
Industrial Automation applies the California standard as the single floor for this entire website, for every visitor, rather than building separate rules state by state. In practice, this means every visitor to trfautomation.com, regardless of where they are located, receives the protections described in this policy, including the right to submit a rights request as described below, the Global Privacy Control honoring described below, and the "Do Not Sell or Share My Personal Information" link described below.
California residents specifically have the right to know what personal information is collected, used, disclosed or sold; the right to delete personal information, subject to exceptions; the right to correct inaccurate personal information; the right to opt out of the sale or sharing of personal information (
TRF
does not sell or share personal information for cross-context behavioral advertising, so there is nothing to opt out of, but the mechanism is provided regardless); the right to limit the use of sensitive personal information (
TRF
does not collect sensitive personal information through this site); and the right to non-discrimination for exercising any of these rights.
Other US States With Comprehensive Privacy Laws
States that had a comprehensive consumer privacy law in effect by 2026, alongside California, generally follow the Virginia model described above and typically exempt information tied to a business to business contact from the scope of the law. Because
TRF
's operation in the United States is entirely business to business, information collected through this site from a visitor acting on behalf of their employer is often outside the scope of these other states' laws by design. Where a state law does apply, for example because a visitor is interacting in a personal rather than business capacity,
TRF
Industrial Automation honors the rights described in the general framework above, applying the same request process described later in this policy regardless of which state's law technically applies.
Canada: PIPEDA and provincial privacy law
Visitors in Canada are protected by the Personal Information Protection and Electronic Documents Act (PIPEDA), Canada's federal private-sector privacy law, and by applicable provincial privacy legislation. PIPEDA is built around a set of fair information principles: accountability for the information a business holds, identifying the purpose of collection before or at the time of collection, obtaining meaningful consent, limiting collection to what is needed for the identified purpose, limiting use, disclosure and retention to that purpose, keeping information accurate, applying appropriate safeguards, being open about privacy practices, giving individuals access to their own information, and providing a way to challenge a business's compliance.
TRF
Industrial Automation applies these principles to visitors from Canada in the same way it applies the California standard described above: a Canadian visitor can request access to, correction of, or deletion of the information
TRF
holds about them, using the request process described later in this policy. Commercial email sent to a Canadian address also follows Canada's Anti-Spam Legislation (CASL), which requires consent for commercial electronic messages, clear sender identification, and a working unsubscribe mechanism, consistent with what this policy already describes for commercial email generally.
Mexico: Data protection for business contacts
Visitors in Mexico are protected under Mexico's federal framework for the protection of personal data held by private parties, which gives individuals rights generally described as access, rectification, cancellation and opposition, sometimes referred to together as ARCO rights.
TRF
Industrial Automation extends the same request process described later in this policy to visitors in Mexico, so a business contact in Mexico can ask
TRF
to confirm what information it holds, correct it, delete it (subject to legitimate business or legal reasons to retain it), or object to a specific use.
Cookies and tracking technologies
trfautomation.com uses cookies and similar technologies grouped into three categories.
Category
Purpose
Requires consent
Essential
Needed for the site to function: page navigation, form submission, security and load balancing
No, these cannot be disabled through the cookie banner because the site cannot function without them
Analytics
Measures how visitors use the site, such as which pages are viewed and how forms perform, to improve content
Yes, these only load after a visitor accepts this category
Marketing and performance
Measures the effectiveness of marketing activity that brings visitors to the site
Yes, these only load after a visitor accepts this category
On a first visit, trfautomation.com presents a cookie banner that allows a visitor to accept all categories, reject all non-essential categories, or choose category by category. Non-essential cookies do not load until the visitor makes an affirmative choice to accept them. A visitor's choice, along with the date, time, an associated IP address, and the version of this policy in effect at the time, is stored in an auditable consent record, so
TRF
can demonstrate what a given visitor agreed to and when.
A visitor can change cookie preferences at any time using the cookie settings link available on every page, or by adjusting browser settings to block or delete cookies, though blocking essential cookies may affect how parts of the site function, such as form submission.
Global Privacy Control
Global Privacy Control (GPC) is a signal a visitor's browser or browser extension can send automatically to every website it visits, indicating a preference to opt out of the sale or sharing of personal information. California and Colorado require covered businesses to honor this signal as a valid opt-out request.
TRF
Industrial Automation configures trfautomation.com to detect and honor the Global Privacy Control signal automatically, for every visitor who sends it, regardless of location, treating it the same way as a manually submitted opt-out request.
Do Not Sell or Share My Personal Information
TRF
Industrial Automation does not sell personal information to third parties, and does not share personal information for cross-context behavioral advertising as that practice is defined under California law. Even so, a "Do Not Sell or Share My Personal Information" link is available in the footer of every page on trfautomation.com, consistent with California requirements, so a visitor always has a clear, visible way to confirm and exercise this choice.
Children's privacy
trfautomation.com is a business to business website intended for adults evaluating industrial automation equipment on behalf of a manufacturer or organization. It is not directed to children, and
TRF
Industrial Automation does not knowingly collect personal information from children. If
TRF
learns that information has been submitted by a child,
TRF
will take steps to delete that information.
How to exercise your rights
A visitor who wants to access, correct, delete, or receive a portable copy of the information
TRF
holds about them, or who wants to opt out of a specific use described in this policy, can submit a request in either of two ways: through a dedicated request form linked from this policy, or by writing to
TRF
's privacy contact address, also linked from this policy and from the
Contact
TRF
Industrial Automation
page. Both channels reach the same review process.
TRF
Industrial Automation verifies each request using the information available from the original submission, such as matching the requester's email to the one on file, before acting on it, to protect against someone else requesting access to another person's information.
TRF
aims to respond within 45 days of receiving a verifiable request, which is the timeframe most US state privacy laws use as a standard, and this period can be extended once, with notice to the requester, where reasonably necessary. Every request received, along with the date it was received, the deadline for response, and the outcome, is logged so
TRF
can demonstrate compliance with this process.
There is no fee to submit a rights request under this policy, and
TRF
Industrial Automation does not discriminate against a visitor for exercising a right described in this policy, whether by denying a service, charging a different price, or providing a different level of support, beyond what is objectively related to the value of the information involved.
Accessibility
TRF
Industrial Automation built trfautomation.com to the Web Content Accessibility Guidelines (WCAG) 2.1, level AA, covering areas such as adequate color contrast, keyboard navigation, alternative text for images, and a semantic page structure that works with assistive technology. Accessibility is part of the site's design standard rather than a separate feature, and it applies across every page reachable from this domain.
Changes to this policy
TRF
Industrial Automation may update this Privacy Policy as its practices, the site's features, or applicable law change. Each version of this policy is dated, and
TRF
maintains a version history so a visitor can identify what changed and when. A visitor's continued use of trfautomation.com after a revised policy is published constitutes acknowledgment of the update; where a change is material,
TRF
will provide a more prominent notice on the site.
Questions about this policy
TRF
Industrial Automation welcomes questions about this Privacy Policy, about a specific request, or about how a particular part of the site handles information. The fastest way to reach
TRF
about this policy is through
Contact
TRF
Industrial Automation
, or through the dedicated privacy request channel linked above.
TRF
Industrial Automation is based at 14840 W 107th St, Lenexa, KS 66215, and serves manufacturers across the
regions
TRF
supports in the United States
from that base. A customer who already operates
TRF
-supplied equipment and has a question that touches both privacy and an active
TRF
Care
relationship can reach
TRF
through either channel; both are reviewed by the same team.
Contact
TRF
Industrial Automation

Questions
Frequently asked questions
What Is a Palletizer?
A palletizer is a machine that stacks packed units, such as cases, bags or trays, onto a pallet in a defined pattern at the end of a production line. A robotic palletizer does it with a robot arm and an end-of-arm tool, building each layer automatically so operators no longer lift every case by hand. The
robotic palletizing page
compares the
TRF
platforms.
What Is Palletization?
Palletization is the process of building a stable pallet load from cases, bags or other packed units. It covers the pallet pattern, the number of layers, slip sheets between layers when the load needs them, and the discharge of the finished pallet to wrapping and shipping. A palletizer automates that process at the end of the line, layer by layer.
What Is the Typical Payback Period for a Robotic Palletizer?
It depends on your line. Payback is driven by the labor hours the palletizer replaces, shifts per day, local labor cost and the installed cost of the system. The
palletizing ROI calculator
applies this method to your own line data.

**description:** How much does a robotic palletizer cost? See TRF reference list prices, the factors that move the investment and how to estimate payback for your line.

```

cost drivers and payback.

TRF
Cobot
Dual to US$ 350,000 for the

How much does a robotic palletizer cost?
A robotic palletizer costs what its scope requires, and the clearest reference is a standardized platform with a published list price. Three
TRF
platforms have one, all EX Works Lenexa, Kansas, installation not included.
Platform
Reference list price
Typical speed
Case weight
Typical fit
TRF
Cobot
Dual
US$ 120,000
About 1 to 10 cases per minute
Up to about 22 lb
Lower-speed, high-mix lines with limited floor space
TRF
Compact
Dual
US$ 180,000
About 5 to 15 cases per minute
Up to about 44 lb
Industrial duty cycle in a compact, standardized cell
TRF
Speed
100
US$ 350,000
About 10 to 35 cases per minute
Up to about 66 lb
High volume, continuous or multi-line palletizing
TRF
Subzero
Quoted per application

Palletizing in frozen and refrigerated areas below 32°F
Custom palletizer
Quoted per project

Engineered per project
Products, speeds, weights or layouts outside the standard platforms
Typical ranges.
TRF
engineering confirms final capacity for each application (product, end-of-arm tool, pallet pattern, reach and process).
A list price is a starting point, not a project total. Installation, line integration, the end-of-arm tool for your case, conveyors and options are quoted with your application, because two plants that order "a robotic palletizer" rarely need the same scope. Part of the investment also happens inside your own plant, such as electrical work, floor preparation and production time lost during installation.
What are you actually buying?
A robotic palletizing cell is a system, and each part of it carries cost. Use this list as the skeleton of your budget and as the checklist for every proposal you receive.
Robot and cell structure.
Payload and reach set the robot class, which drives a large share of the investment.
End-of-arm tool.
Vacuum, clamp, fork and multipick tools have different costs, and the tool is what makes the cell work with your product.
Infeed and conveying.
Infeed, accumulation and metering deliver cases in the right orientation, spacing and rate.
Pallet handling.
Empty pallet supply, slip sheets and discharge of full loads. Manual pallet handling lowers the investment and keeps an operator at the cell.
Safety concept.
Guarding and sensing defined for the complete cell.
Controls and integration.
PLC, HMI and interfaces with upstream packaging equipment, wrapping and labeling.
Recipes and pallet patterns.
The number of SKUs and patterns affects engineering hours and changeovers.
Installation, startup and training.
Installation, commissioning, production trials and training for operators and maintenance.
Two quotes that look far apart often differ in what each supplier assumed you would provide. Writing this list into your request for quotation keeps proposals comparable.
Which factors move the investment up or down?
Factor
Effect on the investment
Line speed
Faster and multi-line systems cost more than an entry-level cell
Case weight
Heavier cases require a larger robot class and a stronger end-of-arm tool
Product type
Cases, bags, pails, trays and bundles each need a different gripper and handling logic
Number of infeeds
Each line added to a central palletizing cell adds conveying, controls and pattern logic
Pallet handling
Automatic pallet dispensing, slip sheets and load discharge add scope and reduce operator work
SKU and pattern variety
More recipes mean more engineering, validation and training
Operating environment
Areas below 32°F, washdown areas and dusty areas add engineering requirements
Two factors are often underestimated. Palletizing in a freezer is a different engineering problem, which is why
TRF
offers
TRF
Subzero
for areas below 32°F. And in a brownfield plant, getting the cell in, connected and commissioned without stopping production is a significant part of the work.
Size the system for the throughput you will actually run, including the growth you can defend, not for the maximum the plant might one day reach.
What is total cost of ownership for a palletizer?
Total cost of ownership is what the system costs to buy, install and run over its useful life. A capital request built only on the purchase order tends to be challenged by finance, while one built on total cost of ownership is easier to defend.
Category
What it includes
Acquisition
Equipment, end-of-arm tool, controls and options, from the quote or list price
Project and installation
Electrical, air, civil work, rigging, removal of old equipment, internal engineering hours
Production impact
Reduced output during installation and startup
Operation
Electricity, compressed air, consumables such as slip sheets
Maintenance and spare parts
Preventive routines, wear parts, recommended spares
Changeovers
Time lost when switching SKUs and pallet patterns
Two items are easy to forget: production impact during installation and changeover time. Both are measurable in your own records.
What is the typical payback period for a robotic palletizer?
There is no typical figure that fits every plant: payback depends on the labor hours the palletizer replaces, shifts per day, local labor cost and the cost of the system. The estimate follows the method of the A3 (Association for Advancing Automation) robot ROI calculator: the system cost, including 20 years of maintenance at 5% per year, divided by the annual net savings, which is the labor cost of manual palletizing minus the electricity the robot uses.
Break-even = (system price + 20 years of maintenance at 5% per year) ÷ (manual labor per year minus the robot's electricity per year)
Build the estimate in four steps:
Count the labor.
People dedicated to palletizing per shift, hours per shift, number of shifts and days per week, including the overtime that palletizing generates today.
Apply a labor rate.
Use your own rate when you have it. The calculator fills an average production wage for your state from public U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics data, and you can replace it.
Build the system cost.
The system price plus maintenance at 5% per year over the 20-year life. Electricity (a 20 kW reference draw at your state's rate) is subtracted from the yearly labor savings.
Divide.
The result is the break-even in years. Multiply by 12 for months. The 20-year labor savings is the manual labor over 20 years minus the robotic system over the same period.
Run it twice: once with today's shift pattern and once with the pattern you expect as volume grows. The equipment is paid once, while the labor it replaces repeats on every shift.
The
palletizing ROI calculator
applies this method. Choose a platform to load its reference list price, or enter the investment you want to test, then add your state, people per shift, hours per shift, shifts per day and days per week. The result is an initial estimate that leaves out several costs, such as installation and integration, and a
TRF
Sales Engineer completes the calculation with the costs of your project.
What Does Payback Not Capture?
Payback is a screening tool, not the whole business case.
Ergonomics.
Repetitive lifting, bending and twisting at the end of the line is the work automation takes off your team.
Labor availability.
Some plants automate palletizing because the position is hard to fill and keep, not only because of the hourly rate.
Consistency.
A robot builds each pallet to the stored pattern, which supports load stability and the customers who receive your pallets.
Operator reassignment.
People move from repetitive lifting to supervision, quality checks and changeovers.
What should you ask a robotic palletizer supplier before buying?
Ask questions that make every supplier price the same scope.
Which platform do you recommend for our speed, case weight and product, and why?
How will you validate the end-of-arm tool with our actual product, including the hardest case?
How many recipes and pallet patterns are included, and what does adding more cost later?
Are infeed conveyors, pallet dispensing, slip sheets and discharge conveyors in the quote, or assumed to be ours?
Who owns the interfaces with our packaging equipment, labeling and wrapping?
What do installation, startup and training include, and how much production time should we plan to lose?
How do remote support, spare parts and field service work after startup?
A higher quote often includes work the lower one leaves to your team. For
TRF
-supplied equipment,
Parts and Service
explains how support and spare parts are organized.
Questions
Frequently asked questions
What Is a Palletizer?
A palletizer is a machine that stacks cases, bags, trays or other packed units onto a pallet in a defined pattern at the end of a production line. A robotic palletizer does this with a robot arm and an end-of-arm tool, which makes it flexible across products, SKUs and pallet patterns.
TRF
Industrial Automation supplies collaborative, compact industrial and high-speed robotic palletizing platforms.
What Is Palletization?
Palletization is the process of arranging packed units into a stable load on a pallet so it can be wrapped, stored, moved by forklift and shipped. It covers the pattern of each layer, the number of layers, the use of slip sheets and how the finished load leaves the line. Robotic palletizing automates that process at the end of the line.
How Do I Choose a Palletizing System?
Choose by line speed, case weight and floor space first, then by product type, number of lines and operating environment. Those variables point to a platform, and the end-of-arm tool and pallet pattern are validated per application. Send your line data, and a
TRF
Sales Engineer confirms which platform fits and what the scope of the quote should include.
How Many Cases per Minute Can a Robotic Palletizer Handle?
It depends on the platform.
TRF
Cobot
covers about 1 to 10 cases per minute,
TRF
Compact
about 5 to 15 and
TRF
Speed
about 10 to 35. These are typical ranges for commercial screening, and
TRF
confirms the final rate for your product, end-of-arm tool, pallet pattern and reach. Applications outside these ranges are engineered as a custom palletizer.
What Payload Does a Palletizing Robot Need for My Product?
The robot must carry the case weight plus the end-of-arm tool, and for multi-case picks, the combined weight of the cases.
TRF
Cobot
handles cases up to about 22 lb,
TRF
Compact
up to about 44 lb and
TRF
Speed
up to about 66 lb. Payload also sets the robot class, which is one of the main cost factors of the cell.
What Is the Difference Between a Cobot Palletizer and a Traditional Robotic Palletizer?
A cobot palletizer uses a collaborative robot, designed for shared workspaces and moderate speeds, in a compact standardized cell with a lower entry cost. A traditional robotic palletizer uses an industrial robot that moves faster, carries heavier loads and runs a higher duty cycle. That is why the
TRF
Cobot
Dual has the lowest of the three
TRF
reference list prices.
What Maintenance Does a Robotic Palletizer Require to Avoid Downtime?
A robotic palletizer needs periodic inspection of the gripper, sensors and the robot itself, following the manufacturer's schedule, and that yearly cost belongs in your payback estimate. Keep recommended spare parts for wear items on hand. For
TRF
-supplied equipment, Parts and Service covers technical support, spare parts and the
TRF
Care
preventive maintenance program.
Turn the cost question into a specification
The useful version of "how much does a robotic palletizer cost" is "what does my palletizing project include, and what does it return". Start from the list price of the platform that fits, build the scope list and run the payback estimate with your own shifts and labor cost. At that point the budget number stops being a guess and becomes a document your finance team can approve.
For the commercial view of each cost factor, see the
robotic palletizer cost and ROI page

==============================================================================
**title:** Avon Conveyor System Case Study | TRF Industrial Automation
**description:** Conveyor system case study: how TRF Industrial Automation engineered conveying, case handling and vertical transport as one material flow for Avon.

```
Resources / Case Studies / Avon
Avon conveyor system case study: Conveying, case handling and
vertical transport as one flow.
One material flow from conveying through case handling to vertical transport.
This conveyor system case study documents how
TRF
Industrial Automation designed and installed a completely new automatic material flow for Avon, with conveying, case handling and vertical transport engineered as one system.
Talk with our Sales Engineer
→
Custom engineering
Solutions designed around your line.
Turnkey integration
From concept to full production.
High reliability
Built for continuous operation.
U.S. market focus
Support across the United States.
Lifecycle support
Backed by
TRF

.
Key facts
Item
Details
Customer
Avon
Sector
Beauty and body care products
Facilities
A production center and a distribution center of the customer
Application
Internal material flow: conveying, case handling and vertical transport
Supplied by
TRF
, which designed and installed the solution
Scope
A completely new automatic solution, with a modular conveyor platform and roller systems for two facilities and 24 lifts from a vertical transport specialist in the production line
Figures reported by the customer
Distribution center capacity of 16,000 boxes per day, around 1,200 boxes per hour; around 9% productivity increase in the first three months
The challenge: Accuracy in a high-mix material flow
The problem was accuracy in the internal flow of materials. In a fully harmonized production and logistics operation, the right product has to reach the right stage at the right moment, and that accuracy supports the performance of the whole operation.
The solution: A new automatic material flow
TRF
was responsible for designing and installing a completely new automatic solution, and the customer credits it with a significant improvement in productivity.
TRF
worked in partnership with a specialist in vertical transport, whose lifts serve the production line, and used a modular conveyor platform and roller systems for both facilities.
The modular platform let the customer create solutions for the needs of its products by shape, size and weight. The flow runs from the product separation and packaging area, through the production and assembly line, forward to shipping to the end consumer.
What the customer reports
The customer's distribution center management reports a capacity of 16,000 boxes per day, around 1,200 boxes per hour. With a focused project, the customer reported an increase of around 9% in productivity in the first three months, which allowed orders to be processed faster and reduced processing time for reseller orders inside the distribution center.
The same account explains the choice of supplier: a solution aimed mainly at the safety of employees, which the customer describes as a company value, and a partnership already established at another of its plants.
Which conveyor systems fit a flow like this?
TRF
Intraflow
fits packaged goods moving between machines and levels, the kind of flow described in this case.
TRF
supplies conveyors as engineered systems integrated into the line, not as loose components.
Need
TRF
system
Where to read more
Case conveying, accumulation, routing, diverting and elevation
TRF
Intraflow
Case conveyor and intralogistics
Whole path from packaging discharge to shipping, as one scope
End-of-line integration
End-of-line integration
TRF
Intraflow
has no single speed figure, because throughput depends on the system design, the product and the layout rather than on a single module. For
personal care and cosmetics
plants, the conveying decision often comes with a case packing decision:
TRF
Rapidpack
for high-mix, delicate or irregular products,
TRF
Onepack
to erect, pack and seal the case in one machine, and
TRF
Droppack
for controlled gravity loading of flexible packages. At the end of the line,
robotic palletizing
closes the flow.
Questions
Frequently asked questions
Can a Conveyor System Be Customized to My Exact Plant Layout?
Yes. An engineered conveyor system is laid out around your building, your machines and your product mix, using standard modules for conveying, accumulation, diverting, routing, elevation and integration. In the Avon project, a modular conveyor platform let the customer create solutions for products of different shapes, sizes and weights across two facilities, with lifts between levels.
Can a Conveyor System Integrate With My Palletizer and Case Packer?
Yes. Integration is simplest when conveying, case packing and palletizing are engineered together, with accumulation placed to decouple the machines and controls that coordinate the flow. When the whole path from packaging discharge to shipping is one scope, it falls under end-of-line integration, with one partner responsible for how the machines work together.
Can a Single Supplier Engineer, Build and Install My Entire Conveyor System?
Yes.
TRF
Industrial Automation engineers, builds and installs conveyor systems as part of the line, with one partner responsible for engineering, manufacturing, controls, installation and commissioning. The same scope can include case packing and palletizing. In this case,
TRF
designed and installed the complete automatic flow, working with a vertical transport specialist for the lifts.
How Is Vertical Transport Handled in an End-of-Line System?
Lifts and elevators move product between levels without manual handling, and they are specified by load, cycle rate and footprint. In an integrated system, they are controlled as part of the same flow as the conveyors, so accumulation and routing account for the time a load spends changing levels. In this case, lifts connect levels inside the production line.
Next step: Map your own material flow
If your plant handles a wide product mix, moves product between levels or loses output at transfers rather than inside machines, this conveyor system case study describes a familiar problem. A
TRF
Sales Engineer starts with your product families and your layout, then designs the flow around them, with case packing and palletizing in the same scope where it makes sense.
Talk with our Sales Engineer

The challenge: Why palletizing below freezing is hard
The case lists four challenges the plant faced before automation:
Productivity declines over time.
Manual output falls as exposure to the cold accumulates across a shift.
Physical limitations of manual operations.
Repetitive lifting is harder to sustain in sub-zero conditions than at ambient temperature.
High ergonomic risk.
Lifting, twisting and reaching case after case in the cold puts strain on operators.
Difficulty maintaining production throughput.
When the palletizing station cannot hold rate, the whole line slows down.
The solution: A
TRF
Subzero
system engineered for cold storage
TRF
implemented a complete robotic palletizing solution for cold storage. The case describes three elements:
A

What changed at the end of the line
The customer reports three outcomes at Custom Foods:
A significant increase in end-of-line throughput.
A standardized and stable operation regardless of temperature.
Improved employee safety and ergonomics.
The repetitive lifting in sub-zero conditions moves from people to the robot.
Can a robotic palletizer operate below freezing?
Yes, when the cell is engineered for it, as at Custom Foods: a robot designed for sub-zero operation, with components and engineering developed for frozen environments.

How do you build the business case for freezer automation?
Build it like any palletizing project, with extra attention to the labor inputs of a cold area. The estimate follows the method of the A3 (Association for Advancing Automation) robot ROI calculator: the system cost, including 20 years of maintenance at 5% per year, divided by the annual net savings, which is the labor cost of manual palletizing minus the electricity the robot uses.
In a freezer, productivity that declines across a shift and throughput lost when the station cannot hold rate belong next to that number. Run your own shifts and labor cost in the
palletizing ROI calculator
, and read
how much a robotic palletizer costs
for the investment side.

plants with refrigerated or frozen finished goods.

What Payload Does a Palletizing Robot Need for My Product?
The robot must carry the case weight plus the end-of-arm tool, and for multi-case picks, the combined weight of the cases. How a case behaves when cold also affects gripping.
TRF
validates the final payload with your product, end-of-arm tool, pallet pattern and reach, and the table above shows the typical case weight for each platform.
How Does a Robotic Palletizer Handle SKU or Pallet Pattern Changeovers?
A changeover usually means selecting a stored recipe that holds the case dimensions, pick logic and pallet pattern for the new SKU. When the new case fits the same end-of-arm tool, the change happens mostly in software.
TRF
Cobot
and
TRF
Compact
store multiple recipes and pallet patterns, so a SKU change does not mean rebuilding the process.

```

End-of-line automation case study: Ergonomic case packing and robotic
palletizing at Lodge Cast Iron.
Ergonomic case packing and robotic palletizing delivered in a single project.
This end-of-line automation case study documents how
TRF

Key facts
Item

Lodge Cast Iron
Founded
1896, in Tennessee
Sector
Cast iron cookware manufacturing
Application
End of line: case packing and palletizing
System supplied
Tailor-made ergonomic manual case packing system and fully automatic robotic palletizing system

Productivity, safety, ergonomics and product quality
Outcomes reported

Supplier
TRF
Industrial Automation
The challenge: Packing lines that backed up
Before automation, the packing lines were very labor intensive and easily backed up with the volume of product coming down the line, according to the customer. The company wanted a solution that made the job easier, put less stress on employees, worked more efficiently and reduced the workload of the people doing it.
Two problems sit inside that description, and they are common to any plant packing heavy product by hand:
Rate is capped by people.
A manual station has a sustainable pace, and when the process upstream runs faster, the line backs up.
The physical load accumulates.
Taping cases and lifting heavy boxes onto pallets by hand, shift after shift, is the work behind shoulder and lower back complaints.
The solution: Fewer touches, from packing station to pallet
The customer's design brief was clear: the employee should touch the product as few times as possible. That required automated palletizing along with automated packaging, and a partner able to provide a full end-of-line solution rather than one machine.
TRF
delivered a tailor-made ergonomic manual case packing system together with a fully automatic robotic palletizing system. The operator places the product in the box at an ergonomic station, and from there the robot takes the case away. As the customer describes, the team no longer struggles with tape machines or lifts heavy boxes onto the pallet.
Which
TRF
systems fit a project like this?
Payload decides first: the robot carries the case weight plus the end-of-arm tool, then rate and floor space narrow the choice. The
robotic palletizing page
compares the
TRF
platforms, and a
custom palletizer
covers products, patterns or layouts outside the standard ranges. On the packing side, automatic case packing fits when format and rate justify it:
TRF
Rapidpack
,
TRF
Onepack
and
TRF
Droppack
.
What changed on the floor
The results are about people first. The customer reports less stress on employees and lines that can be operated with fewer people. It also reports fewer complaints of shoulder and lower back strain: once the product is in the box, the robot takes it away, and operators hold the weight for less time.
The customer also describes people who are not as worn out at the end of the day.
For a project like this, case conveying, accumulation and routing between packing and palletizing use
TRF
Intraflow
modules, and when packing, conveying and palletizing are scoped as one project, the scope is
end-of-line integration
.
Questions
Frequently asked questions
Can a Plant Automate Palletizing and Keep Manual Case Packing?
Yes, and it can be the right scope. An ergonomic manual packing station keeps the flexibility of hand packing, while the automatic palletizing cell removes the heaviest, most repetitive lifting. That is the combination installed at Lodge Cast Iron, where the robot takes each case away once the product is in the box.
Can Case Packing Equipment Integrate With My Existing Palletizer and Conveyors?
Yes, when packing, conveying and palletizing are engineered together. Case conveying, accumulation and routing connect the packing area to the palletizing cell, and the controls coordinate the flow. When the whole path from packing to pallet is one scope, it falls under end-of-line integration, with one partner responsible for how the machines work together.
Does End-of-Line Automation Reduce Headcount?
The customer says the lines can be operated with fewer people. The same case stresses the physical side: operators hold heavy cases for less time, and the most strenuous positions no longer depend on lifting. That makes automation a labor availability argument as much as a labor cost argument.
Who Makes Robotic Case Palletizers With Local Support in the US?
TRF
Industrial Automation designs, builds, installs and supports robotic case palletizers for manufacturers across the United States, with three standard platforms plus custom engineering. Installations include this Lodge Cast Iron project and the
Custom Foods
cold storage system. How support and spare parts work for
TRF
equipment is explained on the
Parts and Service
page.
Next step: Scope your own end of line

TRF
your case dimensions and weights, your line rate, your shift pattern and a layout of the packing area, and a Sales Engineer will come back with a concept covering packing, conveying and palletizing together.

What Is EOAT and How Does It Affect Palletizer Payload?
EOAT is the end-of-arm tool: the gripper, clamp, fork or vacuum head at the end of the robot arm that holds the case. Its own weight counts against the robot's payload, together with the case weight and, for multi-case picks, the combined weight of the cases.
TRF
validates the final payload with your product, gripper, pallet pattern and reach.

TRF
supplies conveyors: as engineered systems integrated into the line, never as loose components.

modules handle conveying, accumulation, routing and elevation between machines, and

How do you plan a first end-of-line automation project?
Define the problem first, collect line data second and choose equipment third.
Define the problem, not the machine.
Repetitive manual lifting, operators tied to the end of the line, a bottleneck that limits packaging or a growth plan the current line cannot absorb lead to different systems.
Collect line data.
Products and packages, case dimensions and weights, rate in cases or products per minute, pallet patterns, shifts, floor space, temperature of the area and the equipment upstream and downstream.
Shortlist by line setup and speed.
The
robotic palletizing page
shows which platform fits each speed and load; the
end-of-line integration page
covers projects that join packing, conveying and palletizing.
Estimate payback.
The
palletizing ROI calculator
gives an initial estimate from your shifts, labor and the investment you want to test.
Ask for an application review.
A
TRF
Sales Engineer validates product, end-of-arm tool, pallet pattern, reach and process before quoting.
Questions
Frequently asked questions
What Is the Difference Between a Cobot Palletizer and a Traditional Robotic Palletizer?
A cobot palletizer uses a collaborative robot, an arm designed for shared workspaces and moderate speeds, in a compact cell with a lower entry cost. A traditional robotic palletizer uses an industrial robot that moves faster, carries heavier loads and runs higher duty cycles.
TRF
Cobot
covers lower-speed, high-mix lines;
TRF
Compact
and
TRF
Speed
cover higher rates and payloads.
Can a Compact Palletizer Be Installed in a Brownfield Plant With Limited Space?
Yes.
TRF
Compact
puts an industrial robot in a compact standardized cell designed for brownfield plants and lines where production space has high value, at about 5 to 15 cases per minute and cases up to about 44 lb.
TRF
validates floor space, reach, pallet positions and operator access with your layout before quoting.
Turn a project idea into a plan

How should you prepare for a meeting with an automation supplier at a trade show?
Write the problem in one sentence, collect the data that describes your line and agree with your team on what a good answer looks like. Send the data in advance through
TRF
's PACK EXPO page or the
contact form
, mentioning PACK EXPO International 2026, so the meeting becomes a working session:
Product and package type, dimensions in inches and weight in lb, including the heaviest SKU
Current and target throughput, in cases per minute or products per minute
Number of lines feeding the end of line, and shifts per day
Pallet type, pattern and load height, with photos of a finished pallet
Available floor space in feet and a sketch of the current end of line
Environment: areas below 32°F, dry or washdown zones
What sits upstream and downstream: fillers, baggers, checkweighers, labelers, wrappers
For palletizing, bring the exact case dimensions and weight, plus photos of the floor space where the cell would sit. For case packing, bring samples when you can, because the behavior of a flexible package is hard to describe on paper. For conveying, mark where products wait, where operators intervene and where the line stops most often.
Schedule a Meeting at PACK EXPO International 2026
→

How Do I Schedule a Meeting at PACK EXPO International 2026?

Can a Robotic Palletizer Operate Below Freezing (32°F)?

Can a Palletizer Be Integrated With My Existing Conveyor and Wrapping Line?
Yes. The infeed, accumulation, pallet handling and discharge to wrapping are engineered with the palletizer, and the controls interface with the equipment that stays in place. Questions
Frequently asked questions
How Does a Robotic Palletizer Handle SKU or Pallet Pattern Changeovers?
A changeover usually means selecting a stored recipe that holds the case dimensions, pick logic and pallet pattern for the new SKU. When the new case fits the same end-of-arm tool, the change happens mostly in software.
TRF
Cobot
supports multiple recipes and pallet patterns, which is why it fits high-mix lines with frequent changeovers.
What Floor Space and Reach Do I Need to Validate Before Buying a Cobot Palletizer?
Validate the area for the robot, the infeed pick point, one or more pallet positions and operator access to remove full pallets. Then confirm reach: the robot must place the farthest case on the top layer of your tallest pallet. Pallet height and case size change the answer, so
TRF
validates reach, end-of-arm tool and pallet pattern for each application.

Questions
Frequently asked questions
What Is the Difference Between a Dedicated Palletizing Cell and a Centralized Multi-Line System?
A dedicated cell palletizes the output of one production line, so each line runs on its own. A centralized multi-line system brings several lines into one palletizing area, which changes the conveying, accumulation and pallet handling around it.
TRF
Speed
is configurable and modular, so it scales from a dedicated cell to a centralized system serving multiple lines.
What Is Slip Sheet Palletizing and When Is It Needed?
Slip sheet palletizing places a thin sheet between layers or under the load. Plants use slip sheets when a pallet load needs more stability or protection, or when their own customers require them. Slip sheet handling is one of the
TRF
Speed
options, alongside multipick, automatic pallet handling and pallet discharge.

Robotic Palletizing
Robotic palletizing systems engineered for food, beverage
and CPG production lines.
From a cobot for short runs to high-speed multi-line systems, the right palletizer follows your line setup and speed.
Robotic palletizing is the use of an industrial or collaborative robot to stack cases or boxes onto pallets automatically, replacing manual pallet building on food, beverage and CPG production lines.

What is a robotic palletizer?
A robotic palletizer is a cell in which a robot picks finished units from a conveyor and stacks them on a pallet in a programmed pattern, layer by layer, until the load is complete. Buyers also call it automated palletizing, a palletizing robot or a robotic pallet stacker.
The cell has a few standard parts. An infeed conveyor presents units in a known position, and the robot, collaborative or industrial, carries an end-of-arm tool selected for the product, such as vacuum, clamp or fork tooling. A pallet station holds the pallet being built, and options include pallet dispensing, slip sheets between layers and automatic pallet discharge.
Each pallet pattern is stored as a recipe, so an SKU change becomes a selection instead of a mechanical adjustment. What changes from one platform to the next is the rate per minute, the unit weight, the floor space and the number of lines a single cell can serve.
Why do manufacturers automate palletizing?
Manufacturers automate palletizing to take repetitive lifting off the end of the line and keep pallet output steady on every shift. Four problems usually start the project:
Labor.
Palletizing is repetitive work that is hard to staff. Automation lets you reassign operators to line supervision, quality checks and changeovers.
Ergonomics.
Manual pallet building means lifting, bending and twisting, case after case. A robotic palletizer takes that motion off your team.
Throughput and line stops.
When pallet building cannot keep up, cases back up and the packaging line slows. A palletizer sized for your peak rate makes pallet output independent of how fast people can lift.
Floor space and changeovers.
Compact cells fit tight end-of-line areas, and stored recipes handle frequent SKU and pallet pattern changes.
TRF
robotic palletizing platforms
The
TRF
palletizing family moves from accessible collaborative automation to compact industrial performance and high-throughput integrated palletizing.
TRF
Subzero
covers palletizing areas below freezing, and a custom palletizer covers what falls outside the standard ranges.
TRF
Cobot
: Collaborative Palletizing for Lower-Speed, High-Mix Lines
TRF
Cobot
is the entry point into robotic palletizing, for plants that still palletize by hand or run short, high-mix production. It makes automation viable where a traditional industrial cell would be oversized or hard to justify, with a small footprint and simple, recipe-based operation. Typical users are small and mid-sized food manufacturers, pharmaceutical and personal care lines, co-packers, and e-commerce and distribution operations.
TRF
Compact
: Industrial Performance in a Compact Cell
TRF
Compact
brings the speed, payload and duty cycle of an industrial robot into a compact, standardized cell. It fits brownfield plants where floor space is the main constraint, secondary lines inside larger plants, and co-packers that need robustness without losing SKU and pallet pattern flexibility.
TRF
Speed
: High-Throughput and Multi-Line Palletizing
TRF
Speed
is the high-performance platform for high-volume, continuous production. Its modular architecture scales from a dedicated palletizing cell to a centralized system serving several lines, with multipick, automatic pallet handling, slip sheets, pallet discharge and interfaces to wrapping, labeling and warehouse systems.
TRF
Subzero
: Robotic Palletizing Below 32°F
TRF
Subzero
is the
TRF
palletizer for areas operating below 32°F. It brings industrial reliability, modularity and integration to frozen food lines, ice cream plants, frozen prepared foods and meat processing in below-freezing areas (below 32°F). If your palletizing area runs below freezing, start the evaluation with
TRF
Subzero
.
Custom Palletizer: Engineered When a Standard Platform Is Not Enough
A custom palletizer is the answer when speed, load, product type, pallet handling or layout goes beyond the standard ranges.
TRF
engineers the cell around your products, pallet patterns, layout and production flow, and delivers it as one project.

Line data to prepare before sizing a palletizer
A palletizing proposal starts from data, not from a catalog. Gather the inputs below before your first conversation with a
TRF
Sales Engineer:
Product and packaging:
dimensions, unit weight in lb, packaging type and how rigid or flexible the unit is when picked.
Rate:
cases per minute per line, peak versus average, and how many lines feed the palletizing area.
Pallet:
pallet type and size, the pattern for each SKU, load height and whether slip sheets are used.
Layout:
distance from infeed to pallet stations, available floor space, ceiling height and forklift access.
Operation:
shifts per day, changeover frequency and the operating temperature of the area.
Interfaces:
the upstream case packer or conveyor and the downstream wrapper, labeler and warehouse systems.
Those inputs define payload, cycle rate, reach and layout, and they show whether the project fits a standard platform or a
custom palletizer
.

Questions
Frequently asked questions
What Is the Difference Between a Cobot Palletizer and a Traditional Robotic Palletizer?
A cobot palletizer uses a collaborative robot, an arm designed for shared workspaces and moderate speeds, in a compact standardized cell. A traditional robotic palletizer uses an industrial robot with higher speed, payload and duty cycle.
TRF
Cobot
is the collaborative option for lower-speed, high-mix lines, while
TRF
Compact
and
TRF
Speed
use industrial robots for higher rates and heavier cases.
How Many Cases per Minute Can a Robotic Palletizer Handle?
It depends on the platform.
TRF
Cobot
covers about 1 to 10 cases per minute,
TRF
Compact
about 5 to 15 and
TRF
Speed
about 10 to 35. These are typical ranges for commercial screening:
TRF
confirms the final rate for your product, end-of-arm tool, pallet pattern and reach, and applications above that range move to a custom palletizer.
What Payload Does a Palletizing Robot Need for My Product?
The robot must carry the heaviest unit plus the end-of-arm tool, and for multi-case picks, the combined weight of the cases.
TRF
Cobot
handles cases up to about 22 lb,
TRF
Compact
up to about 44 lb and
TRF
Speed
up to about 66 lb.
TRF
validates the final payload with your product, gripper, pallet pattern and reach.
Can a Robotic Palletizer Operate Below Freezing (32°F)?
Yes, with equipment built for that environment.
TRF
Subzero
is the
TRF
palletizer for areas below 32°F, used in frozen food, ice cream, frozen prepared foods and meat processing in below-freezing areas. The
Custom Foods case study
shows palletizing below freezing in a published installation.
How Does a Robotic Palletizer Handle SKU or Pallet Pattern Changeovers?
Pallet patterns are stored as recipes, so an SKU change usually means selecting the recipe that holds the case data, pick logic and pattern for the new product. When the new unit fits the same end-of-arm tool, the change happens mostly in software; otherwise the cell needs a tool change.
TRF
reviews your full SKU and pattern list before recommending a platform.
Can a Palletizer Be Integrated With My Existing Conveyor and Wrapping Line?
Yes. The palletizer needs cases in the right orientation, at a steady rhythm and with enough accumulation upstream to absorb a pallet change.
TRF
Intraflow
provides conveying, accumulation and routing modules with palletizer interfaces.
End-of-line integration
puts case packing, conveying, palletizing and the wrapping, labeling and warehouse interfaces under one engineering scope, and interfaces with equipment you already own are defined during project engineering.
How Much Does a Robotic Palletizer Cost?
Reference list prices are US$ 120,000 for the
TRF
Cobot
Dual, US$ 180,000 for the
TRF
Compact
Dual and US$ 350,000 for the
TRF
Speed
100, all EX Works Lenexa, Kansas, installation not included. Installation, line integration and options are quoted with your application. The
robotic palletizer cost guide
explains each cost driver.
What Is the Typical Payback Period for a Robotic Palletizer?
Payback depends on the labor hours the palletizer replaces, shifts per day, local labor cost and the cost of the system. The estimate takes the total system cost, including maintenance, and divides it by the annual labor cost of manual palletizing minus the electricity the robot uses, using reference production wages by state. The
palletizing ROI calculator
returns an initial estimate that a
TRF
Sales Engineer completes with your project costs.

What is a bag palletizer?
A bag palletizer takes over the lifting, turning and stacking of filled bags that operators otherwise do by hand after the bagging machine. The robot picks each bag from the infeed conveyor, places it where the pallet pattern defines and repeats the cycle layer by layer until the pallet reaches its target height. The finished pallet then moves on to wrapping, labeling and shipping.
Because the pattern lives in software, the cell follows product changes without a mechanical rebuild. Operators move from lifting bags to supervising the cell, checking quality and handling changeovers.
Bags are palletized in
pet food
,
chemicals
and
food processing
plants that pack grains and ingredients, and on shared lines at
co-packers
. In every one of them, the business case starts with the same two drivers: less dependence on repetitive manual palletizing, and no more repeated lifting, bending and twisting at the end of the line.
Why Bags Are Harder to Palletize Than Cases
No rigid shape.
A bag takes its shape from how it was filled and how the product settles on the way to the palletizer.
The contents move.
When a bag is lifted, the product flows toward the low points, so the bag sags between the points where it is held.
Uneven layers.
A layer of bags forms crowns and valleys, and the next layer has to sit on that surface without sliding.
Stability comes from interlocking.
A column of cases stands on its own walls. A stack of bags relies on friction and on bags bridging the joints of the layer below.
How do you keep a pallet of bags stable?
A stable pallet of bags comes from three decisions made together: an end-of-arm tool that supports the bag without deforming it, a pattern that interlocks the layers, and layer sheets where the bag surface needs friction. Get one of them wrong and the pallet still leans.
The end-of-arm tool.
The tool is selected for your bag type, size, weight and surface. A soft, heavy bag generally needs support across its base so the contents do not pull it out of shape, and the tool weight counts against the robot payload.
TRF
validates the tool with your actual bags, filled the way your line fills them.
The pallet pattern.
Like bricks in a wall, each layer is rotated or mirrored so bags bridge the joints of the layer below, which ties the load together. Column stacking, where every layer repeats the same footprint, is rarely stable for bags. The pattern also keeps bags inside the pallet edge, because overhang scuffs bags and pulls the load outward.
Sheets and the pallet.
Slip sheets or tier sheets between layers add friction between slippery bag surfaces and spread the weight above, and a sheet on the pallet deck protects the first layer from gaps and broken boards. On
TRF
Speed
projects, slip sheet handling is part of the integration scope; on smaller cells, the approach is defined with the application.
Where the application needs it, flattening the bag upstream gives the robot a consistent height and footprint, and consistent bags build level layers. The result is a square, tight load that wraps and ships predictably.
What is a sack palletizer?
A sack palletizer is a bag palletizer applied to heavier industrial sacks, such as the sacks used for grains, ingredients and chemicals. In most plants "sack" and "bag" describe the same machine, but sacks tend to bring more weight, more dust and more variation in surface and shape.
Weight is usually the first question for sacks. When the sack is heavier than the published payload of the standard platforms, or the rate or layout goes beyond them,
TRF
evaluates a
custom palletizer project
. For every sack, the end-of-arm tool, the pattern and the reach are validated with real filled sacks.
Sack construction changes how the tool grips and how the layers hold together, so the pattern and the use of slip sheets are chosen for each sack. In dusty areas, the project also defines sensor selection, cleaning access and how the tooling sheds residue in your environment.
Can a bag palletizer be integrated with your existing conveyor and wrapping line?
Yes.
TRF
engineers the infeed, the palletizer and the pallet outfeed as one system instead of placing a robot at the end of a conveyor. The conveyor between the bagger and the robot absorbs rhythm differences and delivers bags spaced and oriented for the pick.
TRF
Intraflow
provides the modules: conveying, curves and transfers, zero-pressure accumulation, metering, gapping, merges, diverts, inclines and elevators, with sensors, PLC, HMI and palletizer interfaces.
Accumulation matters on bag lines. A buffer ahead of the palletizer lets it finish a pallet exchange without stopping the bagger, and lets the bagger recover from a short stop without starving the robot. Downstream, the pallet moves to wrapping, labeling and the warehouse. When the whole end of the line is in scope,
end-of-line integration
puts case packing, conveying and palletizing under one partner, from layout to startup.
Smaller retail bags, such as pet food or rice bags, are often packed into cases before palletizing. That flow uses a case packer such as
TRF
Droppack
, covered on the
bag case packer page
, and a
case palletizer
for the finished cases.
How do you choose a bag palletizing system?
Choose by line setup and speed first: how many bags per minute the line produces, how many lines feed the palletizer and how much floor space the cell can use. Bag weight, end-of-arm tool and pallet pattern then confirm the choice.

What is a box palletizer?
A box palletizer is the automated end of a packing or fulfillment line. Boxes arrive on a conveyor, a robot picks them one or several at a time, and the pallet grows layer by layer in a pattern stored as a recipe. When the pallet is complete, it leaves the cell for wrapping, labeling and the dock or the warehouse.
On a robotic palletizer the pattern is software, so adding a box size or changing how a customer wants a pallet built is a recipe change rather than new mechanical guides. That is why robotic palletizing fits operations with many box sizes and frequent changes, such as
e-commerce and distribution
,
CPG
and
co-packers
. The business case starts with labor: fewer people dedicated to repetitive stacking, and no more repeated lifting, bending and twisting at the end of the line.
Box, Case or Carton: Which Term Fits Your Product?
The three words overlap in everyday use, but they point to different operations, and
TRF
has a page for each:
Term
What it usually means
Typical source on the line
TRF
page
Box
A corrugated shipper, often in many sizes, used in distribution, fulfillment, co-packing and CPG
Pack stations, case sealers, fulfillment lines
This page
Case
The sealed shipping case that leaves a case packer, usually one size per SKU at a steady rate
A case packer at the end of a production line
Case palletizer
Carton
A folding carton or light paperboard pack that ships as the palletized unit
Cartoning equipment
Carton palletizer
What gripper does a box palletizing robot use?
A box palletizing robot uses an end-of-arm tool selected for your box type, size, weight and surface. A sealed box with a flat, closed top is often picked from above with vacuum. Heavier boxes, or boxes whose tops cannot carry the load, may need clamping on the sides or support from below.
TRF
validates the tool with your real boxes before the design is fixed.
Box quality matters as much as the tool. Warped boxes, raised flaps, loose tape and boxes that bulge because they are overfilled or underfilled reduce the grip area and change how the next layer sits. Part of every box palletizing project is agreeing on what a good box looks like when it reaches the robot.
Should boxes be column stacked or interlocked?
It depends on box strength, product weight, storage time and how the pallet travels. Corrugated boxes carry load mainly through their corners and vertical walls, so the way layers line up decides how much weight the bottom layer can support over days in a warehouse or a trailer.
Column stacking
places every layer exactly on the one below, so corners and walls line up all the way down. It keeps most of the box strength working, but the columns are tied together only by wrap and friction.
Interlocked stacking
rotates or shifts each layer, like bricks, so boxes bridge the joints below. The pallet is more stable in transport, but corners no longer sit on corners.
Hybrid patterns
column-stack the lower layers and interlock the top ones, or add a tier sheet every few layers to tie the columns together.
Whatever the pattern, boxes stay inside the pallet edge, since overhang crushes and tears boxes in handling, and the layer count respects racking, trailer height and your customer's receiving rules. On a robotic box palletizer, each approach is simply a different recipe.
Can a box palletizer connect to your conveyors, sorter and warehouse system?
Yes. A box palletizer is only as fast as the flow of boxes it receives, so
TRF
engineers the conveying and the palletizer together.
TRF
Intraflow
, the
TRF
platform for moving cases, cartons, trays and totes between packing, palletizing and warehouse operations, provides the modules a box operation needs:

A pail palletizer, also called a bucket palletizer, is a robotic system that picks filled, lidded pails from the end of a filling line and stacks them onto pallets in a stable, repeatable pattern.
TRF

Cobot
,

Pail palletizer key facts
Topic
What applies to pails and buckets
What it does
Picks closed pails from the infeed conveyor and stacks them on a pallet in a stored pattern
TRF
models
TRF
Cobot
,

and

TRF
