```
palletizer for below-freezing areas (below 32°F)
What is a pail palletizer?
A pail palletizer is the last automated station of a pail filling line. It takes each closed pail from an infeed conveyor, sets it in a planned position on the pallet and repeats the cycle layer after layer until the load is complete. "Pail" and "bucket" describe the same container, so this page covers both.
Pails are demanding to palletize by hand. They are round or square, often filled with dense liquids, pastes or powders, and many carry a bail handle that swings freely. An operator lifts every pail from conveyor height, turns and sets it down near the floor for the first layer and at chest height for the top layer. A robotic pail palletizer takes that repetitive lifting, bending and twisting off your team.
Factor
Manual pail palletizing
Robotic pail palletizing
Lifting and ergonomics
Every pail is lifted, turned and placed by a person
The robot handles each pail
Pattern consistency
Depends on the operator, the shift and fatigue
The same stored recipe on every layer
Staffing
People dedicated to the pallet on every shift
Operators reassigned to supervision and quality checks
Size and pattern changes
The operator adapts the pattern from memory
A stored recipe for each pail size and pattern
Pails are palletized in
chemicals
,
household and cleaning products
and
food processing
plants that ship ingredients in pails, and at
co-packers
that fill pails for several brands.
How does a robot grip pails with lids and bail handles?
The end-of-arm tool is selected for your pail, its lid and its handle, and validated with filled pails as they leave your line. A tool that holds the pail by its lid needs a flat, fully seated and dry lid. A tool that holds the pail body needs clearance around each pail, which the pallet pattern has to allow.
Bail handles are the detail most often underestimated. A handle that stands up can catch on the tool or on a neighboring pail, so the application review defines how the handle reaches the robot: folded flat, turned to a set position or held by the tool. Lids with tamper bands or spouts define where the tool can touch, and labels usually face outward on the finished pallet, so the recipe also controls the rotation of each pail.
At higher rates, a tool that picks more than one pail per cycle reduces the number of robot moves per pallet. Multipick is part of the
TRF
Speed
scope, and the combined weight of the pick is what engineering checks against the robot and the reach.
How are pails arranged on a pallet?
Pails go on the pallet in a square grid or an offset pattern, chosen for the pail shape and for how the load must rest. Round pails leave gaps between them however they are arranged. A square grid keeps each pail directly on top of the one below, which helps when pails are designed to stack lid to base. An offset pattern, where alternate rows shift by half a pail, can fit more pails per layer, but it changes how the load rests on the layer below. Square pails fill the pallet more completely and usually run in a block pattern.
Filled pails have small contact areas, so stability comes from three things working together: a pattern with no overhang at the pallet edge, a flat and consistent pallet, and, when needed, a slip sheet between layers that spreads the weight of the upper layer and keeps pails level. After palletizing, the load is stretch wrapped, and wrap settings are part of the stability plan. Every pattern is stored as a recipe, so it repeats identically on every pallet.
Can a pail palletizer be integrated with your filler, conveyors and wrapper?
Yes. Upstream, a pail line usually runs a filler, a lid placer and press, weight verification and coding or labeling. The palletizer needs pails one at a time, correctly spaced, with the lid seated and the handle in a known position. Accumulation between the filler and the robot absorbs short stops, so a pallet exchange does not stop filling and a brief filler stop does not starve the robot.
TRF
designs these connections as engineered systems, not as loose conveyor sections.
TRF
Intraflow
provides modules for conveying, accumulation, diverting, routing, elevation and integration, and
end-of-line integration
brings the filling-line outfeed, palletizing, wrapping and labeling under one project with one partner.
TRF
Speed
adds pallet handling, slip sheets and warehouse interfaces when the scope calls for them.
How do you choose a pail palletizer?
Choose by line setup and speed: how many pails per minute the filling line produces, how many lines feed the palletizer and how much floor space the cell can use. The filled pail weight, the end-of-arm tool and the pattern then confirm the choice.

Can One Palletizer Handle Different Pail Sizes and Pallet Patterns?
Yes, within the validated application.
TRF
palletizing platforms run multiple recipes and pallet patterns, so the same pail palletizer can handle more than one pail size or pattern. Can a Pail Palletizer Fit a Plant With Limited Floor Space?

TRF

has a small footprint for areas where a traditional robotic cell is hard to install, and
```

```
Bundle palletizer key facts
Topic
What applies to bundles
What it does
Picks bundles from the infeed conveyor and stacks them on a pallet in an interlocked, stored pattern
TRF
models
TRF
Cobot
,
TRF
Compact
and
TRF

What decides the model

What is validated

What is a bundle palletizer?
A bundle palletizer turns a stream of bundles into a shippable pallet load. It picks each bundle from the infeed conveyor, turns it when the pattern calls for it, places it on the pallet and repeats the cycle until the load is complete, following a stored recipe for bundle size, pattern, layer count and tier sheets.
On a production line, a bundle is a group of products held together as one unit without a closed case around it:
Film-only shrink bundles
, such as bottles or cans grouped and wrapped in shrink film.
Pad or tray and shrink bundles
, where products stand on a pad or shallow tray inside the film, which gives a firmer base.
Strapped or banded bundles
of flat goods, such as paper products, paperboard or blanks.
If your product ships in a closed case, see the
case palletizer
. If trays of product are stacked without film, see the
tray palletizer
.
Bundles are the shipping unit in
beverage
,
paper and packaging
,
household and cleaning products
and
co-packers
. The business case starts with labor and ergonomics: stacking heavy multipacks by hand means repeated lifting, bending and twisting at the end of the line, shift after shift.
Why Bundles Are Harder to Palletize Than Cases
A closed case has flat, rigid walls on every side; a bundle often does not. A film-only bundle has an uneven top of caps and shoulders, a bottom that flexes when lifted from the ends and film that slips against the film of the next layer. A strapped bundle is rigid but heavy, and its edges dent if a tool presses in the wrong place. These traits change how the bundle is gripped, how it is turned and which pattern holds together.
How does a robot pick shrink-wrapped bundles without damaging them?
With an end-of-arm tool selected for your bundle and a well-prepared infeed. A film-only bundle generally needs support across its whole base, so the bottom does not sag and the film is not stressed. Heavier multipacks need a tool that holds them firmly while the robot turns them.
TRF
validates the tool with production bundles.
The infeed matters as much as the tool. Bundles leave the shrink tunnel with warm film that has not fully set, so a section of conveyor between the tunnel and the palletizer lets the film firm up before the pick. On paper and packaging lines, bundles come from a stacker and a strapping station, and the palletizer needs straps tensioned and bundles square. A tool that supports the bundle from below also needs space to withdraw after placement, which is part of the pattern design from the start.
How do you keep a pallet of bundles stable?
A stable bundle pallet comes from the pattern, the sheet plan and the handoff to wrapping, designed together, because film-wrapped layers slide more easily than case layers.
Stability factor
Why it matters for bundles
How it is handled
Pattern
Film layers slide more than case layers
Interlocking patterns stored as recipes, with bundles turned as the pattern requires
Tier sheets
Film-on-film contact and heavy layers
Sheets between layers, placed manually or automatically
Bundle support in the tool
Soft bottoms sag when lifted from the ends
Tools that support the base of the bundle
Pallet quality
A warped deck tilts the first layer
Defined pallet supply and inspection
Wrapping
Holds the load through transport
Wrap tension suited to bundles, tight enough to hold slippery layers without deforming the edges
The recipe also controls which face points outward, so labels and the open ends of the shrink film sit where the plant wants them on the finished pallet. Automatic slip sheet handling is part of the
TRF
Speed
scope; on lower-speed cells, the sheet approach is defined with the application.
How do you choose a bundle palletizer?
Choose by line setup and speed: how many bundles per minute the palletizer must sustain, including peaks after a stop, and how many lines will feed it now and in the coming years. Then confirm with the heaviest bundle, its film, pad or straps, and the floor space.
```

```
What is a carton palletizer?
A carton palletizer takes cartons from the end of a packaging line and builds them into a pallet load that can be wrapped, labeled and shipped without being touched by hand. The robot picks each carton, or a group of cartons, from the infeed conveyor and places it according to a recipe that defines the layer pattern, the orientation of every carton, the number of layers and the use of slip sheets.
Cartons behave differently from heavy shipping cases. They are often lighter, they arrive in larger numbers per minute, their surfaces carry retail graphics or lot codes, and paperboard loses strength quickly when corners are misaligned or the load overhangs the pallet edge. A carton palletizer is therefore engineered for gentle, precise placement as much as for rate, with the gripper, the pattern and the line connections designed together.
Carton lines are common in
personal care and cosmetics
,
pharmaceutical
,
confectionery
and
dairy and cheese
plants. The business case starts with labor and ergonomics: fewer people dedicated to repetitive pallet building, and consistent patterns on every shift.
Carton, Box or Case: Which Page Fits Your Product?
Term
What it usually means
Where to read more
Carton
Paperboard or light corrugated container that leaves the line as the palletized unit
This page
Box
Corrugated box in distribution, e-commerce and co-packing flows
Box palletizer
Case
Closed shipping case that leaves a case packer
Case palletizer
If your cartons are first packed into shipping cases, the
carton case packer
and the case palletizer are the pages for that flow.
Can a robot palletize several cartons at once?
Yes, when the application supports it. Light cartons often arrive faster than one robot move per carton allows, so engineering looks at two numbers: the carton rate from your line and the pick rate the robot needs. When the tool picks two or more cartons per cycle, the pick rate drops while the carton rate stays the same.
Multipick is part of the
TRF
Speed
scope, together with automatic pallet handling and slip sheets. A grouping or row-forming section in front of the robot prepares the cartons, and the pick count per cycle is set during validation with your production cartons, since it also changes the tool weight and the pattern.
How does a carton palletizer avoid crushing cartons?
It combines a gentle end-of-arm tool, a pattern suited to carton strength and good load practice. The robot sets cartons down rather than dropping them, keeps them inside the pallet edge and follows a pattern chosen for the carton and the load height.
The tool.
The end-of-arm tool is selected for your carton type, size, weight and surface. Coatings, porous board and glossy print affect a vacuum grip, and side pressure can dent panels or scuff graphics, so the tool is tested with production cartons.
The pattern.
Carton patterns trade compression strength against load stability:
Pattern
Strength of the stack
Stability in transit
Typical use for cartons
Column
Higher, walls aligned
Lower without help from sheets and wrap
Tall loads, lighter cartons, long storage
Interlocking
Lower, walls offset
Higher, layers tie together
Shorter loads, stronger cartons, rough handling
Column with interlocked top layers or tier sheets
Balanced
Balanced
Loads that need both strength and stability
Print and labels.
Labels, barcodes and lot codes usually face outward so the load can be read without breaking it down, and the recipe controls the rotation of each carton. A slip sheet on the pallet deck protects cartons from gaps between boards, and wrap settings suited to cartons complete the protection.
How does a robotic palletizer handle carton changeovers?
A changeover becomes a recipe selection, plus any tool change the application requires. Each recipe stores the carton size, pattern, orientation, layer count and sheet plan, so operators do not have to rebuild a pattern from memory on every shift. Three questions shape the changeover strategy:
How many carton sizes run on the line, and how different are they?
A narrow range may run on one tool; a wide range may need an adjustable or interchangeable tool.
How often does the SKU change?
Frequent SKU and pattern changes are part of the
TRF
Cobot
and
TRF
Compact
profiles.
Do customers specify their own pallet patterns?
Co-packers often build to customer rules, so the number of stored patterns grows over time.
How do you choose a carton palletizer?
Choose by line setup and speed: the carton rate the palletizer must sustain, how many cartoning lines feed it and how much floor space it can use. Carton weight, picks per cycle and pattern then confirm the choice.
```

**title:** Case Palletizer for Sealed Shipping Cases | TRF
**description:** Case palletizer systems from TRF Industrial Automation stack sealed cases from your case packer: TRFCobot, TRFCompact, TRFSpeed or custom.

```
Robotic Palletizing / Case Palletizer
Case palletizer sized to your
case packer output.
Keep pace with your case packer and take manual stacking off your crew, from a single line to a centralized multi-line system.
A case palletizer is an automated system that receives sealed shipping cases from the case packer and stacks them onto pallets in a programmed pattern, ready for stretch wrapping and shipping.
TRF
Industrial Automation builds case palletizers on
TRF
Cobot
,
TRF
Compact
and
TRF
Speed
, or as a custom palletizer, and the right model is defined by your line setup and speed.

Case palletizer key facts
Item
What to know
What it does
Receives sealed cases from the case packer or case sealer and stacks them on pallets in a programmed pattern
TRF
models
TRF
Cobot
,
TRF
Compact
and
TRF
Speed
, plus a custom palletizer beyond their ranges
What decides the model
Peak cases per minute, number of lines and floor space, then case weight and pallet handling
Multi-line capability
TRF
Speed
can consolidate the flow of several production lines into one coordinated palletizing system
Integration scope
Conveyors, accumulation, multipick, automatic pallet handling, slip sheets, pallet discharge, wrapping, labeling and warehouse interfaces
Palletizing below 32°F
TRF
Subzero
, the
TRF
palletizer for below-freezing areas (below 32°F)
What is a case palletizer?
A case palletizer builds pallet loads from the sealed shipping cases that leave a packaging line. Cases arrive on a conveyor at the rate the case packer sets. The palletizer picks one case or a group of cases, places them according to the pattern recipe for that SKU, adds layer sheets where required, and releases the finished pallet for wrapping and shipping.
Two things make case palletizing more predictable than most palletized products: the case is rigid and dimensionally consistent, and one SKU usually means one case size and one pattern. That is also why
TRF
publishes its palletizing ranges in cases per minute and pounds per case. The business case is the same in every plant: fewer people dedicated to repetitive stacking, no more repeated lifting at the end of the line, and a palletizer that keeps pace with the packing line.
Case palletizing runs at the end of most packaged-goods lines, in
food processing
,
dairy and cheese
,
bakery and snacks
,
CPG
and
co-packers
. Film-wrapped multipacks without a case go on the
bundle palletizer
page.
Why the Case Packer Defines the Case Palletizer
A case palletizer is sized backward from the packing line, because the case packer decides how many cases reach the palletizer and how consistent they are.
Rate.
TRF
case packing platforms are positioned by application range:
TRF
Rapidpack
for applications of about 100 to 200 products per minute,
TRF
Onepack
for applications of about 20 to 50 products per minute and
TRF
Droppack
for applications of about 20 to 500 products per minute. Those are product rates, so the cases per minute at the palletizer depend on how many products go into each case.
Case quality.
A squarely erected, properly sealed case presents a flat top for the gripper. Loose flaps, excess tape or bulging cases change how the robot picks and how the layers sit.
Case identification.
When several SKUs run on one line, the palletizer needs to know which pattern applies, from line control or from a barcode or vision interface on the conveyor.
How fast does a case palletizer need to be?
Size it for the peak rate of the case packer, not the shift average. A palletizer sized for the average stops the line whenever the packing line runs at its peak or catches up after a stop. Four numbers matter:
Peak cases per minute
, the highest sustained rate the case packer produces.
Cases per layer and layers per pallet
, since every pallet change takes time out of the cycle.
Recovery rate
after a short upstream stop, when the line runs faster to catch up.
Time that is not case picking
, such as pallet exchange, layer sheet replenishment and SKU changes.
Between the case packer and the palletizer, accumulation decides how often a palletizer stop becomes a line stop. Zero-pressure accumulation holds cases without pressure between them and releases them in a controlled flow, which gives the palletizer time to change a pallet.
TRF
Intraflow
provides accumulation, metering and gapping modules, together with merges, diverts, sorting, reject stations and elevation.
What is the difference between a dedicated palletizing cell and a centralized multi-line system?
A dedicated cell palletizes the output of one line. A centralized system brings several packing lines into one palletizing area, which plants choose when several lines need palletizing but none of them justifies its own cell.
TRF
Speed
covers both, scaling from a dedicated palletizing point to a centralized system serving multiple lines.
A centralized system adds these elements:
Merging and routing.
Case conveyors from each line merge into the palletizing area, with diverts and sorting sending cases to the correct pallet position.
Case identification.
Barcode or vision interfaces identify each case so the system applies the right pattern and the right pallet.
Several pallet positions.
One cell can build more than one pallet at a time, within the layout and reach the project validates.
Multipick and pallet handling.
Picking more than one case per cycle, automatic pallet handling, slip sheets and pallet discharge keep the cell running with less manual work.
Because the architecture is modular, a plant can start with one line and add others as production grows.
How are sealed cases gripped and stacked?
The end-of-arm tool is selected for your case type, size, weight and surface. A sealed case with closed flaps offers a flat top that is often picked with vacuum, while heavy, tall or weak-top cases may need side clamping or support from below. Every option is validated with your actual cases, sealed the way your line seals them, because tape, glue and board grade all affect the grip.
Case pallet patterns are usually defined per SKU and often by the customer receiving the pallet. A robotic case palletizer stores each one as a recipe, so a retailer that wants a specific layer count or labels facing out does not require a mechanical change. The pattern chooses interlocking or column stacking for case strength and storage time, keeps cases inside the pallet edge and respects racking and trailer height. Slip sheets between layers add friction and spread load, and the palletizer hands a square load to stretch wrapping and pallet labeling.
How do you choose a case palletizer?
Choose by line setup and speed: a single line at a modest rate, a compact cell on a busy secondary line, or a high-throughput system serving one or several lines in continuous production. Case weight, floor space and future lines then confirm the choice.

Can One Case Palletizer Handle Different Case Sizes and Pallet Patterns?
Yes, within the validated application. Each case size and pallet pattern is stored as a recipe, so the same palletizer can serve several SKUs, and the operator selects the pattern when the line changes over. The limits are the size range of the end-of-arm tool, the reach of the robot and how many pallet positions the layout allows.
What Is the Typical Payback Period for a Robotic Palletizer?

Yes, with
TRF
Subzero

Custom Foods case study
shows a
TRF

What Is Slip Sheet Palletizing and When Is It Needed?
A slip sheet is a sheet placed on the pallet deck or between layers. It adds friction between smooth case surfaces, spreads the load of the layers above and helps column patterns stay square. It is needed when the case surface, the pattern or the customer's rules call for it. On
TRF
Speed
, slip sheet handling is part of the integration scope.
```

```
Why do manufacturers choose collaborative robot palletizing?
Manufacturers choose collaborative robot palletizing to take repetitive manual lifting off the end of the line at a lower entry cost than a full industrial cell. These are the six reasons plants buy
TRF
Cobot
.
Labor
TRF
Cobot
reduces dependence on repetitive manual palletizing and lets you reassign operators to line supervision, quality checks or upstream changeovers.
Ergonomics
Manual palletizing means lifting, bending and twisting, case after case, shift after shift. A palletizing cobot takes that motion off your team.
Payback
TRF
Cobot
is a lower-entry-cost path to robotic palletizing, particularly in one- to three-shift operations. Payback depends on your labor hours, shifts and installed cost.
Flexibility
Multiple recipes, pallet patterns and SKU changes are supported without the complexity of a large custom system, so the robot runs every day, including during short runs.
Simplicity
A standardized architecture and intuitive operation lower the barrier to a first automation project. Operators learn one cell and one routine, and maintenance supports one standardized platform.
Small Footprint
TRF
Cobot
fits applications where a traditional robotic cell would be difficult to install: automate the repetitive work without over-automating the process.
What is a cobot palletizer, and how is it different from a traditional robotic palletizer?
A cobot palletizer is a palletizing cell built around a collaborative robot, an arm designed for shared workspaces and moderate speeds, that picks cases at the end of a production line and stacks them on a pallet in a programmed pattern. It replaces manual pallet building with a compact automated cell.
A traditional robotic palletizer uses an industrial robot. Industrial robots move faster, carry heavier loads and run higher duty cycles, usually as a dedicated cell with its own conveyors, pallet handling and controls. That architecture makes sense at higher rates or when several lines feed one palletizing point. For a lower-volume line, it can be more machine, more floor space and more project than the application needs.
Collaborative robot palletizing sits below that level on purpose: a smaller robot, a simpler layout and a lower entry cost. The trade-off is speed and payload, so a cobot palletizer is sized for lower rates and lighter cases.
Which applications fit cobot palletizing?
Cobot palletizing fits lines where manual palletizing still takes one or more operators and the rate stays at about 1 to 10 cases per minute. Five customer profiles fit
TRF
Cobot
:
Small and mid-sized food manufacturers that still palletize by hand.
Many
food processing
plants run lower-speed lines where a full industrial cell is hard to justify, but lifting every case by hand is no longer sustainable.
Pharmaceutical, personal care and similar industries
with lower-speed production and frequent product changes. See
pharmaceutical packaging automation
and
personal care and cosmetics lines
.
Co-packers and contract manufacturers
where flexibility, SKU changes and short production runs are critical. See
CPG
and
co-packers
.
E-commerce and distribution operations
with repetitive case handling. See
palletizing for e-commerce and distribution
.
Plants with limited floor space
that prefer standardized, fast-to-deploy automation over a custom project.
Each recipe stores what the robot needs to know about one product: case dimensions, pick logic and pallet pattern. On a high-mix line, operators switch recipes when the SKU changes instead of rebuilding the process. The infeed, the pallet positions and the pallet exchange are laid out for your floor and the way your operators work today.
Is your line ready for a cobot palletizer?
Your line is a strong candidate for
TRF
Cobot
when most of these statements are true:
Manual palletizing runs at about 1 to 10 cases per minute.
Cases weigh up to about 22 lb, subject to end-of-arm tool and reach validation.
Floor space at the end of the line is limited.
The line has a high product mix or frequent SKU and pallet pattern changes.
One or more operators are dedicated to repetitive palletizing on each shift.
Your priority is simple operation, flexibility and a lower entry cost rather than maximum throughput.
Before quoting,
TRF
validates five factors with your line data: the product (case dimensions, weight, material and how it behaves when gripped), the end-of-arm tool, the pallet pattern, the reach (the robot must place the farthest case on the top layer of your tallest pallet) and the process (infeed rate, accumulation ahead of the cell and pallet exchange). Floor space is checked at the same time, including operator access to remove full pallets.
```

```
Why do manufacturers choose a compact robotic palletizer?
Plants choose
TRF
Compact
when a collaborative cell is too limited for the line and a large custom cell is more than the line needs. These are the five reasons.
Industrial Performance
An industrial robot brings higher production capability and a robust duty cycle to lines where a cobot would limit the rate.
Small Footprint
TRF
Compact
is designed for brownfield plants and for lines where production space has high value, so the cell fits where a large system cannot.

Standardization
A standardized, plug-and-play architecture means less engineering and fewer open questions on scope than a fully custom cell.
What is a robotic case palletizer, and how is it different from a cobot palletizer?
A robotic case palletizer is an automated cell in which a robot picks shipping cases from the end of a packaging line and stacks them on pallets in a programmed pattern, replacing manual or semi-automated pallet building. The same equipment is often called an automatic case palletizer or a robotic palletizing cell.
Every robotic case palletizer has the same basic parts: an infeed conveyor that presents the cases, a robot that moves them, an end-of-arm tool (EOAT) that grips them, one or more pallet positions and a control system that stores recipes and pallet patterns.
What changes from one cell to another is the robot and the scale. A cobot palletizer uses a collaborative robot for lower rates and lighter cases. A compact palletizer uses an industrial robot, with the speed, payload and duty cycle that come with it, in a cell designed to occupy as little floor as possible. High-speed systems go further, serving several lines at once and handling pallets, slip sheets and discharge automatically.
TRF
Compact
holds the middle position. Compared with a large robotic cell, the difference is the footprint and the project: the cell is standardized and compact, so it is easier to deploy, configure and scale, and the project work concentrates on your products, your layout and your pallet flow.
Which applications fit a compact palletizer?
A compact palletizer fits manufacturers that need industrial performance in a constrained area, at rates where a cobot falls short and a large custom cell is hard to justify. Five customer profiles fit
TRF
Compact
:
Small and mid-sized food, CPG and chemical manufacturers
that need industrial performance in a constrained area. See
food processing
,
chemical packaging automation
and
household and cleaning products
.
Larger manufacturers with decentralized or secondary production lines
, where a full central palletizing system would be excessive. A compact cell automates that line where it is, without rebuilding the plant layout.
Co-packers and contract manufacturers
that need a robust system while keeping SKU and pallet pattern flexibility. See
CPG
and
co-packers
.
E-commerce and distribution operations
where palletizing can benefit from an industrial robot platform. See
e-commerce and distribution palletizing
.
Brownfield plants
where available floor space is a major limitation: a traditional large cell may not fit, and a collaborative cell may fit but fall short on speed or duty cycle.
The platform is selected by cases per minute and case weight, so the rate, weight and pack format of your load decide the fit. The pages by palletized product, such as the
case palletizer
and
carton palletizer
pages, explain the selection for each load.
Is your line ready for a robotic case palletizer?
Your line is a strong candidate for
TRF
Compact
when most of these statements are true:
Palletizing is manual or semi-automated, at up to about 15 cases per minute.
Cases weigh up to about 44 lb, subject to technical validation.
You need industrial reliability and a higher duty cycle than a typical collaborative application.
Floor space is limited, or a traditional large robotic cell is hard to install.
The line has a high product mix or frequent SKU and pallet pattern changes.
You want a standardized solution, but you expect more speed and robustness than a cobot.
Before quoting,
TRF
validates five factors with your line data: the product (case dimensions, weight and how it behaves when gripped), the end-of-arm tool, the pallet pattern, the reach and the process (infeed rate, accumulation ahead of the cell and pallet exchange). In a brownfield plant, the cell is also laid out on your drawing, against existing conveyors, aisles and pallet routes, before the scope is fixed.

Cobot
,
TRF
Compact
and

Frequently asked questions
How Much Does a Compact Robotic Palletizer Cost?
The
TRF
Compact
Dual has a reference list price of US$ 180,000, EX Works Lenexa, Kansas, installation not included. Installation, line integration, gripper and conveyor interface are quoted with your application, because line speed, case weight, pallet pattern and layout change the scope. Use the payback calculator on this page to estimate what manual palletizing costs you today.
How Much Floor Space Does a Compact Palletizing Cell Require?
It depends on the robot reach, the number of pallet positions, the infeed layout and the access your operators and forklifts need.
TRF
Compact
is designed for brownfield environments and lines where production space has high value, so the cell is laid out on your plant drawing during the application review.
TRF
confirms the footprint for your pallet pattern and pallet height before the quote.
Can a Compact Palletizer Be Installed in a Brownfield Plant With Limited Space?
Yes. Brownfield plants where floor space is a major limitation are one of the core applications of
TRF
Compact
. The platform combines an industrial robot with a compact, standardized cell, so it can fit next to an existing line without the footprint of a traditional large robotic cell.
TRF
validates layout, reach and pallet flow against your drawing and your existing conveyors.
What Payload Does a Palletizing Robot Need for My Product?
The robot must carry the case weight plus the end-of-arm tool, and the combined weight when it picks more than one case at a time.
TRF
Compact
handles cases up to about 44 lb,
TRF
Cobot
up to about 22 lb and
TRF
Speed
up to about 66 lb. These are typical ranges:
TRF
validates the final payload with your product, gripper, pallet pattern and reach.
How Does a Robotic Palletizer Handle SKU or Pallet Pattern Changeovers?
A changeover usually means selecting a stored recipe that holds the case dimensions, pick logic and pallet pattern for the new SKU. When the new case fits the same end-of-arm tool, the change happens mostly in software.
TRF
Compact
keeps multiple SKU recipes and pallet patterns on an industrial platform, so a high-mix line changes products without a new project.
Can a Palletizer Be Integrated With My Existing Conveyor and Wrapping Line?
Yes, when the interfaces are defined in the application study: case infeed rate and orientation, spacing, signals between machines and how full pallets travel to the stretch wrapper.
TRF
engineers these interfaces as part of the project and can supply
TRF
Intraflow
conveyors where the line needs accumulation, routing or elevation. See
end-of-line integration
for the full-line approach.
What Is the Typical Payback Period for a Robotic Palletizer?
Payback depends on the labor hours the palletizer replaces, shifts per day, local labor cost and the installed cost of the system. The estimate follows the method of the A3 (Association for Advancing Automation) robot ROI calculator: the system cost, including 20 years of maintenance at 5% per year, divided by the annual net savings, which is the labor cost of manual palletizing minus the electricity the robot uses. The calculator on this page applies this method to your location, your shifts and the people who palletize by hand today, and returns an initial estimate that a
TRF
Sales Engineer completes with the costs of your project.
What Maintenance Does a Robotic Palletizer Require to Avoid Downtime?
A robotic palletizer needs periodic inspection of the gripper, sensors and the robot itself, following the manufacturer's schedule, plus replacement of wear parts before they fail. For
TRF
-supplied equipment,
Parts and Service
covers technical support, spare parts kept in the United States and the
TRF
Care
preventive maintenance program.
Who Makes Robotic Case Palletizers With Local Support in the US?
TRF
designs, builds, installs and supports robotic case palletizers, including
TRF
Compact
```

```
When do you need a Custom palletizer?
You need a custom palletizer when the application falls outside what a standard platform was designed to do. In practice, one of the four situations below is usually present, and often more than one at the same time.
The Product or Load Is Outside the Standard Range
The standard platforms are screened by cases per minute and by the weight of each unit. Many products that are not regular shipping cases still fit a standard platform once the end-of-arm tool and the pallet pattern are validated for them. When the unit weight, size, shape or fragility goes beyond what a standard platform and its tooling can handle, the project moves to a custom palletizer.
The pages by palletized product, such as the
drum palletizer
and
bag palletizer
pages, explain how the platform is selected for each load.
The Line Speed or the Number of Lines Goes Beyond One Configured Cell
TRF
Speed
can scale from a dedicated palletizing cell to a centralized system that serves several production lines. When the combined flow, the number of infeed lines or the production logic goes beyond what a
TRF
Speed
configuration covers, the application is evaluated as a custom project, for example when each consolidated line must keep its own pallet pattern and product sequence.
Pallet Handling Has to Happen Inside the System
Multipick, automatic pallet handling, slip sheets between layers, pallet discharge and interfaces with stretch wrapping, labeling or warehouse systems all change the cell.
TRF
Speed
already covers many of these functions. When their combination or sequence is specific to one plant, the pallet handling is engineered into a custom palletizing system instead of added around a standard cell.
The Layout Does Not Fit a Standard Cell
Building columns, existing conveyors, forklift aisles, pallet staging areas and ceiling height all constrain where a palletizer can go.
TRF
Cobot
and
TRF
Compact
were designed for tight footprints, and they solve many of these cases. When the only viable position requires a special frame, an unusual infeed direction or a split between picking and pallet building, the cell is laid out as a custom project.
What is a Custom palletizing system, and how is it different from a standard platform?
A custom palletizing system is engineered as a project around one application: layout, robot selection, tooling, controls and pallet handling are defined for one plant. A standard platform starts from a known architecture, so the project concentrates on what is specific to your line. Standardization reduces engineering complexity and commercial uncertainty compared with a fully custom cell.
That is why the standard options are checked first, even when a buyer asks for a custom palletizer. Several requirements that sound custom are already covered by a standard platform:
Multiple recipes and pallet patterns.
The standard platforms run several SKU recipes and pallet patterns, so frequent product changes do not require a special machine.
Compact footprints.
TRF
Cobot
fits lines where a traditional robotic cell is difficult to install, and
TRF
Compact
brings an industrial robot into a compact, standardized cell for brownfield plants.
Customer-specific layouts at higher speed.
TRF
Speed
is a configurable platform that uses modular engineering to reduce project risk while keeping the customization each production flow requires.
A custom palletizer is the right answer when the application still does not fit after these options are considered. You pay for engineering where the application needs it, and you keep standard components where they already work.
End-of-Arm Tool and Pallet Pattern
In a custom project, the end-of-arm tool and the pallet pattern are engineered together with the layout, not added at the end. The tool has to pick the unit securely at the required rate, and the pattern has to produce a stable load for wrapping, forklift handling and shipping. Changeovers follow the same principle as the standard platforms: operators select a recipe, and the system changes the pattern and the related settings.
Which products can a robotic palletizer handle?
TRF
palletizes
bags
and
sacks
,
boxes
,
cases
,
trays
and
crates
,
buckets and pails
,
cartons
,
bundles
and
drums
.
Start from the product you ship. Each product page explains which platform fits it by rate and unit weight, and when the application moves to a custom palletizer. For the industries behind these products, the
industries overview
shows which
TRF
platforms fit each one, from
chemicals
to
frozen food and ice cream
.
What information do you need to evaluate a Custom palletizing system?
You need product, line, pallet and plant data, because payload, cycle rate and reach are validated against the real application. The more complete the first data package, the faster engineering can tell you whether a standard platform or a custom project fits.

Interfaces

Goals
Labor to be reassigned, ergonomic issues to remove, capacity targets and planned expansion
If you do not have every item, start with what you have. Product weight, rate per line and a photo or sketch of the available area are usually enough for a first screening conversation with a Sales Engineer.
How do you choose between a standard platform and a Custom palletizer?
Match the operational problem to the platform, and move to a custom palletizer only when no standard platform fits. The
TRF
palletizing family is the reference point for that decision.

Can a Custom Palletizer Serve More Than One Production Line?

What Is EOAT and How Does It Affect Palletizer Payload?
EOAT stands for end-of-arm tool, the gripper at the end of the robot that picks each unit. The robot has to carry the unit weight plus the tool, and the combined weight when it picks more than one unit at a time. That is why the platform ranges are always subject to EOAT and reach validation with your real product.

A custom palletizer has no list price, because it is quoted from each project's data: robot and tooling, pallet handling, conveyors, controls, integration, installation and training. The

What Maintenance Does a Robotic Palletizer Require to Avoid Downtime?
A robotic palletizer needs periodic inspection of the gripper, sensors, conveyors and the robot itself, following the manufacturer's schedule, plus replacement of wear parts before they fail. For
```

```
Robotic Palletizing / Drum Palletizer
Drum palletizer evaluated by drum weight
and line rate.
Drum palletizing evaluated per application: drum weight first, then line rate and layout.
A drum palletizer is a robotic system that lifts closed drums from a filling line and sets them upright on pallets in a fixed, repeatable arrangement, with tooling engineered for the weight and shape of the drum.
TRF
Industrial Automation evaluates each drum application individually: on
TRF
Cobot
,
TRF
Compact
or
TRF
Speed
when the filled drum falls within their published case weights, or as a custom palletizer project when it does not.

Drum palletizer key facts
Topic
What applies to drums
What it does
Lifts closed drums from the filling line and places them upright on a pallet in a stored arrangement
TRF
models
TRF
Cobot
,
TRF
Compact
and
TRF
Speed
within their published case weights, plus a custom palletizer
First screening question
The weight of the filled drum, then the line rate, the line setup and the floor space
Above the published case weights
Evaluated per application as a custom palletizer project
Drum-specific engineering
End-of-arm tool, bung and label orientation, drum pallet, single or double layer
What is validated
The actual drum, the end-of-arm tool, the pallet arrangement, the robot reach and your process
What is a drum palletizer?
A drum palletizer is the end-of-line station that takes closed drums from a filling and closing line and places them on a pallet in a defined arrangement, so a forklift moves one stable load instead of drums handled one at a time. The robot follows a recipe that defines the drum size, the number of drums per pallet, the position and rotation of each drum, and whether a second layer goes on top.
It replaces manual drum handling with drum trucks, lift assists or forklift attachments, where every extra handling step adds time and strain. A robotic drum palletizer builds the same arrangement on every pallet, which matters because a badly arranged drum load is a transport problem, not just an appearance problem.
Drums are palletized in
chemicals
and
food processing
plants that ship liquids, concentrates and ingredients to industrial customers, and in
household and cleaning products
and
pharmaceutical
operations. Plants that fill pails or bags as well can compare the
pail palletizer
and the
bag palletizer
.
How does a robot grip and orient drums?
Drums are handled by their geometry: a cylinder with a rim, a top chime and, on closed-head drums, closures on the lid. The end-of-arm tool is selected for your drum's construction, rim profile, closures and handles, and it has to hold the drum securely at full weight with no risk of slipping during the move.
TRF
validates the tool with your filled drums.
Orientation is part of the recipe on a drum line. Closed-head drums are often placed with the bungs turned to a defined position, so the plant or the customer can reach them without rotating a heavy drum on the pallet. Labels and lot codes are usually turned outward so they can be read while the load is wrapped, and drums with molded handles may need a set rotation so handles do not interfere with the neighboring drum.
Surface condition counts too. Drums leaving a filling station can carry product residue, condensation or cleaning water, and some are warm from the process, so
TRF
reviews the real condition of drums at the pick point instead of assuming a clean, dry container.
Can drums be stacked in more than one layer on a pallet?
It depends on the drum, the contents and your shipping rules. A single layer is the simpler load. When double stacking is used, a slip sheet or a stacking frame spreads the load and keeps the upper drums centered, and the robot must place a heavy drum at height, which raises the reach and payload the system needs. That is why the layer plan is declared at the start of the project, not added later.
Load decision
What it affects
Where it is defined
Drums per pallet and arrangement
Pallet use, overhang, stability
Pattern recipe
Single or double layer

Bung and label rotation

Point loads, deck strength, forklift handling
Pallet supply plan
Sheets, frames and wrap
Stability in transport
Load stability plan
After palletizing, drum loads are usually stretch wrapped, banded or both. Wrapping a round load is different from wrapping a square case load, so the handoff from the palletizer to wrapping and labeling is defined during integration design.
How do you choose a drum palletizer?
Check the weight of the filled drum against the published case weights first, then choose by line setup and speed. Within those weights, line setup and speed define the model; outside them, the application is evaluated as a custom palletizer project.

Questions
Frequently asked questions
Can a Robotic Palletizer Handle My Drums?
That is evaluated per application. Published case weights are up to about 22 lb for
TRF
Cobot
, 44 lb for
TRF

and 66 lb for
TRF

, so a drum whose filled weight falls within that range can be validated on a standard platform with your drum, tool and pattern. A drum above that range is not a standard-platform application;
TRF

Can One Palletizer Handle Different Drum Sizes and Pallet Arrangements?
Yes, within the validated application.
TRF
palletizing systems store multiple recipes with the drum size, the number of drums per pallet, the rotation of each drum and the layer plan, so one system can serve more than one format. A wide range of drum diameters may need an adjustable or interchangeable end-of-arm tool, which is defined during project engineering.
Can a Drum Palletizer Be Integrated With My Filling Line, Conveyors and Wrapper?
Yes. The palletizer needs drums upright, closed, oriented or able to be oriented, and stopped at a known pick position.
TRF
engineers the drum conveying, pallet supply and discharge, wrapping and labeling interfaces as one system, and
end-of-line integration
delivers the whole flow, from filling-line outfeed to wrapped pallet, as one project with one partner.
How Much Does a Drum Palletizer Cost?
Cost on a drum project starts with the payload: the drum weight, the end-of-arm tool, the reach and height of the arrangement, the pallet handling and the conveyor work. Standard platforms have list prices on their own pages; custom drum systems are quoted after the application review. The
robotic palletizer cost page
explains each cost factor.
What Is the Typical Payback Period for a Robotic Palletizer?
Payback depends on the labor hours the system replaces, shifts per day, local labor cost and the installed cost. The
palletizing ROI calculator
applies this method to your location, your shifts and the people who palletize by hand today. On drum lines, also record what it does not price directly: less strain for operators who handle heavy units, and fewer manual handling steps.
What Maintenance Does a Robotic Palletizer Require to Avoid Downtime?
A robotic palletizer needs periodic inspection of the end-of-arm tool, the sensors and the robot itself, following the manufacturer's schedule. On a drum line, check the tool's grip surfaces and the drum conveyor stop position as well. For
TRF
-supplied equipment,
Parts and Service
covers technical support, spare parts and preventive maintenance.
What Information Should I Prepare to Size a Drum Palletizer?
Bring the heaviest filled drum weight, the drum type and dimensions, the line rate, the number of drums per pallet and whether you plan single or double layers, your pallet type, and a photo or drawing of the filling area. A
TRF
Sales Engineer uses this data to screen the standard platforms or a custom palletizer and to validate the fit.
```

```
Why do manufacturers choose a high-speed palletizing system?
High-capacity plants choose
TRF
Speed
when the end of the line has to keep up with production. These are the six reasons.
High Throughput
TRF
Speed
is designed for production environments where the palletizer cannot become the bottleneck.
24/7 Reliability
An industrial architecture built for continuous operation, repeatability and production uptime.
Multi-Line Capability
The flow of several production lines can be consolidated into one coordinated palletizing system, instead of one palletizer per line.
Modularity
A project can start as a single palletizing point and grow into a broader, centralized system as your plant expands.
Integration
TRF
Speed
fits conveyors, accumulation, pallet handling, slip sheets, wrapping, labeling and warehouse interfaces as one end of line.
Customization With Control
A repeatable platform keeps enough engineering flexibility to match your layout and production logic, and its modular engineering reduces project risk.
What is a high-speed palletizing system, and how is it different from a compact palletizing cell?
A high-speed palletizing system is an automated cell, or a group of connected cells, that builds pallets at the rate of a continuous packaging operation, usually with an industrial robot, automatic pallet handling and conveyors that feed it from one or more production lines. It is the end-of-line station that has to keep up with everything upstream.
Compared with a compact cell, a high-speed system adds the functions that keep it moving at higher rates. Multipick, picking more than one case per cycle, raises effective throughput. Automatic pallet handling keeps empty pallets flowing in and full pallets flowing out. Slip sheets, when the load needs them, are placed in the same sequence. These functions decide whether the typical range holds on your line, so they are validated with each application.
TRF
Speed
is built for that reality without forcing every customer into a full custom project. It is a configurable platform: modular engineering reduces project risk, while the configuration keeps the flexibility each production flow requires. A system that starts as a dedicated cell can be extended later, when a new line comes online.
What Is the Difference Between a Dedicated Palletizing Cell and a Centralized Multi-Line System?
A dedicated cell palletizes one line at its own rate. A centralized multi-line system receives cases from several lines, routes them and builds a pallet for each SKU in one palletizing area.
Question
Dedicated palletizing cell
Centralized multi-line system
Lines served
One line
Two or more lines consolidated in one area
Conveying
Short run from the case packer to the cell
Routing, merging and accumulation from each line to the palletizing area
Floor space
Space at the end of each line
One palletizing area, freeing space along the lines
Growth
Add a cell per new line
Add a line to the existing system where capacity allows
Typical driver
A single line is the bottleneck
Several lines, capacity expansion or labor concentrated at the end of line
The choice depends on how many lines you run, how far apart they are, the product mix and how much you expect to grow.
TRF
reviews these factors in the application study and sizes the system so the palletizer does not become the bottleneck of the plant.

and
co-packers
.
Customers planning capacity expansion
and concerned about end-of-line bottlenecks.
Operations that need deeper end-of-line integration
, pallet handling or system customization.

How Much Does a High-Speed Palletizing System Cost?
The

What Is Slip Sheet Palletizing and When Is It Needed?
Slip sheet palletizing places a thin sheet between layers, under the load or between the products and the pallet. It is used when layers need separation or extra stability, or when the receiving operation requires it. On
TRF
Speed
, slip sheet placement is one of the pallet handling functions configured for the project, along with automatic pallet feeding and pallet discharge.
Can a Palletizer Be Integrated With My Existing Conveyor and Wrapping Line?
Yes, when the interfaces are defined in the application study: infeed rates and orientation, spacing, signals exchanged between machines and how full pallets travel to the stretch wrapper or the warehouse.
TRF
engineers these interfaces as part of the project and can supply
TRF
Intraflow
conveyors where the existing line needs accumulation, routing or elevation, so the end of line works as one system.
Can a High-Speed Palletizer Keep the End of My Line From Becoming the Bottleneck?
Yes, when it is sized for your peak rate.
TRF
Speed
is designed for production environments where the palletizer cannot become the bottleneck: multipick and automatic pallet handling keep the cell moving, and accumulation ahead of the cell absorbs short variations between the lines and the palletizer.
TRF
engineering sizes the system from the rate of each line, the product mix and the growth you expect.
How Does a Robotic Palletizer Handle SKU or Pallet Pattern Changeovers?
A changeover means selecting the stored recipe for the new SKU, which holds case dimensions, pick logic and pallet pattern. When the new case fits the same end-of-arm tool, the change is mostly in software. On a multi-line
TRF
Speed
system, recipes also define how cases from different lines are routed and which pallet each SKU builds, so several products can run at the same time.
What Is the Typical Payback Period for Switching From Manual to Robotic Palletizing?
Payback depends on the labor hours the palletizer replaces, shifts per day, local labor cost and the installed cost of the system. The estimate follows the method of the A3 (Association for Advancing Automation) robot ROI calculator: the system cost, including 20 years of maintenance at 5% per year, divided by the annual net savings, which is the labor cost of manual palletizing minus the electricity the robot uses. The calculator on this page applies this method to your location, your shifts and the people who palletize by hand today, and a
TRF
Sales Engineer completes it with the costs of your project.
What Maintenance Does a Robotic Palletizer Require to Avoid Downtime?
A robotic palletizer needs periodic inspection of the robot, end-of-arm tool, sensors, conveyors and pallet handling, following the manufacturer's schedule, plus replacement of wear parts before they fail. For
TRF
-supplied equipment,
Parts and Service
covers technical support, spare parts kept in the United States and the
TRF
Care
preventive maintenance program.

TRF

and
TRF
```

```
What is a robotic palletizer?
A robotic palletizer takes finished cases from a conveyor and stacks them on a pallet in a programmed pattern, layer by layer, until the load is complete. The robot, collaborative or industrial, carries an end-of-arm tool selected for the case type, size, weight and surface.
Each pallet pattern is stored as a recipe with the case dimensions, the pick logic and the layer arrangement. A plant running several SKUs switches recipes when the product changes instead of rebuilding the process, and operators move from lifting cases to supervising the line.
Automation for Kansas food and consumer goods plants

TRF

Food processing

TRF
Subzero
.

and
CPG
:
short runs, frequent SKU changes and pallet pattern flexibility.

:

Can a palletizer be integrated with your existing conveyor and wrapping line?
Yes. A palletizer rarely stands alone: cases arrive from a case packer at a controlled rhythm, with accumulation upstream to absorb a pallet change without stopping the line behind it.
TRF
Speed
in particular is built to integrate with conveyors, accumulation, pallet handling, slip sheets, wrapping, labeling and warehouse interfaces.
TRF
Intraflow
provides the conveying, accumulation and palletizer interfaces, and
case packing
platforms deliver the case the palletizer stacks. When a Kansas project buys all three as one scope,
end-of-line integration
puts them under one engineering plan and one responsible partner.

What Is the Difference Between a Cobot Palletizer and a Traditional Robotic Palletizer?
A cobot palletizer uses a collaborative robot, designed for shared workspaces at moderate speeds and lighter payloads. A traditional robotic palletizer uses an industrial robot with higher speed, payload and duty cycle, usually as a dedicated cell.

Cobot
is the cobot option, while

Compact
and

Can a Robotic Palletizer Operate Below Freezing (32°F)?

What Is the Typical Payback Period for a Robotic Palletizer?
Payback depends on the labor hours the palletizer replaces, shifts per day, local labor cost and the installed cost of the system. The
palletizing ROI calculator
applies this method to your shifts, your state and the people who palletize by hand today, and returns an initial estimate. The
robotic palletizer cost guide

How Does a Robotic Palletizer Handle SKU or Pallet Pattern Changeovers?
Pallet patterns are stored as recipes, so changing SKU usually means selecting the stored recipe instead of making mechanical adjustments. The limit is usually the end-of-arm tool: one tool has to handle every case in the mix, or the cell needs a tool change.
TRF
reviews the full SKU and pattern list during the application review.
```

```
What drives robotic palletizer cost?
Robotic palletizer cost is driven by the scope your application requires. These factors explain the difference between two quotes for the same line:
Cost factor
How it affects the investment
Platform tier and payload
A heavier unit or a higher rate requires a robot and end-of-arm tool with more capacity
End-of-arm tool
Vacuum, clamp, fork and multipick tools differ in complexity; a tool designed for your unit, or one that handles several SKUs or places slip sheets, costs more to design and validate
Pallet patterns and recipes
More patterns, mixed loads and slip sheets add tooling, programming and validation
Conveyors
Infeed, accumulation, orientation and routing from each line are part of the system scope
Pallet handling
Pallet dispensing, slip sheets and pallet discharge add equipment and controls
Integration with the existing line
Adapting an existing plant can cost more than a new installation
Environment
Palletizing below 32°F with
TRF
Subzero
is quoted per application
Options and training
Quoted with your application
Pallet Patterns, Conveyors and Pallet Handling
A line with one product and one pattern needs less programming than a co-packer running many SKUs with frequent changes. The palletizer also depends on how products arrive: at
TRF
, conveyors are engineered as part of the line with
TRF
Intraflow
case conveying
, never sold as loose components. Automatic pallet dispensing and discharge remove manual work and add equipment to the scope.
Controls, Installation and Training
Controls define recipes, the operator interface and the signals exchanged with case packers, conveyors, wrapping, labeling and warehouse systems. Installation covers mechanical and electrical work, commissioning and ramp-up, and operator training is part of the project scope.
Palletizer cost by platform tier
Palletizer cost rises with capacity, but each tier solves a different operational problem, so compare cost against the problem you need to solve. Sizing the platform for your real rate and unit weight avoids paying for capacity you will not use, and avoids a cell that becomes the bottleneck when volume grows.
Platform
Role
Where the investment goes
TRF
Cobot
Entry automation for lower-speed, high-mix lines
Collaborative robot, standardized architecture, small footprint
TRF
Compact
Compact industrial palletizing
Industrial robot in a standardized, space-efficient cell
TRF
Speed
High-performance, continuous and multi-line palletizing
Throughput, pallet handling, slip sheets and broader end-of-line integration
When more than one platform could work, choose the one that matches your throughput, production intensity and growth plans, not simply the lowest initial cost.
What goes into a palletizing system quote?
A palletizing system quote lists the base system and every scope item around it, and two quotes are only comparable when they cover the same scope. Read each one line by line:
Base system:
robot, structure and standard controls for the selected platform.
End-of-arm tool:
tool type, SKUs covered and any tool change.
Pallet patterns:
recipes programmed and validated at startup.
Infeed and conveyors:
conveying, accumulation, orientation and routing from each line.
Pallet handling:
dispensing, slip sheets, discharge and interfaces with wrapping and labeling.
Controls integration:
the signals exchanged with upstream, downstream and warehouse systems.
Installation, startup and training:
installation, commissioning, ramp-up support and operator training.
Options and support:
anything priced separately, including the support plan for the installed system.
Total cost of ownership after the purchase order
The installed price is paid once; the costs below are paid, or avoided, every year the system runs.
Cost element
What to ask
Spare parts and wear items
Which parts wear and where parts are stocked
Planned maintenance
Which preventive tasks the system needs and who performs them
Changeovers and new patterns
How long a pattern change takes and who can create a new recipe
Energy and utilities
Electrical and, where used, compressed air consumption
Operator time
Tasks that remain manual, such as supplying pallets
Expansion
Whether the platform can grow with added lines or higher rates
How do you calculate payback on a robotic palletizer?
Payback depends on the labor hours the palletizer replaces, shifts per day, local labor cost and the cost of the system. The estimate follows the method of the A3 (Association for Advancing Automation) robot ROI calculator: the system cost, including 20 years of maintenance at 5% per year, divided by the annual net savings, which is the labor cost of manual palletizing minus the electricity the robot uses.
Manual labor per year:
people palletizing per shift × hours per shift × number of shifts × days per week × 52 weeks × hourly labor rate.
Total system cost:
the system price plus maintenance at 5% per year for 20 years. For larger crews the calculator scales the system price up, as the A3 method does: 1x for 1 to 2 people, 1.5x for 3 to 4, 1.95x for 5 to 6, 2.535x for 7 to 9 and 3.2955x for 10 or more, because replacing more people takes more equipment.
Break-even, in years:
total system cost ÷ (manual labor per year minus the robot's electricity per year).
The
palletizing ROI calculator
applies this method with average production wages by state from public U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics data and electricity at public U.S. Energy Information Administration state rates with a 20 kW reference draw, and also shows the 20-year labor savings. The result is an initial planning estimate that a
TRF
Sales Engineer completes with the installation and integration costs of your project.
Labor and Shifts
Count the operators dedicated to palletizing on each shift and use a loaded labor rate that includes benefits and payroll costs, because the state reference in the calculator is a base wage. The same palletizer saves more on a line that runs more shifts, because the investment is paid once while the savings repeat on every shift.
Throughput and Ergonomics
If manual palletizing limits the line rate, the added output belongs in the business case next to labor. Manual palletizing also means repeated lifting, bending and twisting; that benefit is harder to price, so present it beside the payback figure.

This calculator compares your current manual labor costs against the total cost of owning and operating a robotic system over its 20-year lifespan. The analysis includes the system purchase price, maintenance (5% annually), electricity costs, and the scale of automation needed based on your workforce size, following the method of the A3 (Association for Advancing Automation) Robot System Value Calculator. Labor data from the U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics; electric data from the U.S. Energy Information Administration (2024 to 2025). Figures are undiscounted and exclude inflation, financing and taxes.
Manual vs robotic palletizing: Comparing the real costs
Manual palletizing has almost no capital cost but carries a labor, ergonomic and capacity cost on every shift. A robotic palletizer converts much of that recurring cost into a one-time investment plus an operating cost.
Cost item
Manual palletizing
Robotic palletizing
Capital investment
Minimal
Installed cost of the system
Labor per shift
One or more operators dedicated to repetitive lifting
Remaining tasks such as pallet supply and supervision
Ergonomic exposure
Repeated lifting, bending and twisting
Repetitive lifting removed from operators
Rate consistency
Depends on the operator and the shift
Set by the system and the recipe
Additional shifts
Add operators for each shift
Same system runs the added shifts
Pattern changes
Operator follows a new pattern
Operator selects a recipe
How does a brownfield plant change palletizer cost?
Adapting an existing plant can cost more than installing a palletizer in a new building, because space, existing equipment and production schedules constrain the project. Look for these cost drivers early:
Floor space:
a cell that does not fit may require relocating equipment or choosing a compact platform.
Existing conveyors:
reusing, modifying or replacing them changes the scope.
Pallet and forklift flow:
new pallet positions can change aisles and staging areas.
Installation windows:
work often has to fit planned shutdowns.
Utilities:
electrical supply and, where used, compressed air must reach the new position.
Questions to ask a robotic palletizer supplier before you buy
Ask every supplier the same list, so the quotes become comparable:
Which platform fits my rate, unit weight and floor space, and why not the next tier up or down?
Has the end-of-arm tool been validated with my products and pallet patterns?
What does the quote include for conveyors, pallet handling, controls, installation and training, and what is excluded?
How long does a pattern change take, and can my team create new recipes?
Who is responsible if the palletizer and the upstream equipment do not work together?
How are support requests and spare parts handled after startup, and can the system grow with added lines?
Questions
Frequently asked questions

What Is the Difference in Cost Between a Cobot Palletizer and a Full Industrial Robotic Cell?
A cobot palletizer is the lower-entry-cost path, designed for lower speed, lighter cases and high-mix lines. An industrial robot cell costs more and delivers more speed, payload and duty cycle. At

How Much Does a Freezer Palletizer Cost?
A palletizer for areas below 32°F is engineered for the cold environment, so it is quoted per application.
TRF

, the

What Is the Typical Payback Period for a Robotic Palletizer?
There is no single typical figure. Payback depends on the labor hours the palletizer replaces, shifts per day, local labor cost and the installed cost of the system, so a line with more shifts and more operators on palletizing recovers the investment sooner. Run the
palletizing ROI calculator
with your own data for an initial estimate.
Can a Compact Palletizer Be Installed in a Brownfield Plant With Limited Space?
Yes.
TRF
Compact
was designed for brownfield plants where floor space is a major limitation, with an industrial robot in a compact, standardized cell.
TRF
Cobot
fits small footprints where a traditional robotic cell is hard to install. Layout, reach and pallet flow are confirmed for each installation, and adapting an existing plant can add integration cost.
What Maintenance Does a Robotic Palletizer Require to Avoid Downtime?
A robotic palletizer needs periodic inspection of the gripper, sensors and robot, following the manufacturer's schedule, plus spare wear parts on hand. For
TRF
-supplied equipment,
Parts and Service
covers technical support, spare parts kept in the United States and the

Care
preventive maintenance program, which belong in your total cost of ownership.
```

```
Why do manufacturers choose a palletizer built for freezers?
Frozen food, ice cream and frozen meat plants choose

Built for the Cold

What is a subzero palletizer, and how is it different from a standard robotic palletizer?
A subzero palletizer is a robotic palletizing system specified for areas below 32°F, so cases are stacked inside or next to the cold area without operators building pallets by hand. A standard robotic palletizer is specified for ambient conditions, and its design starts from the rate.
Below freezing, the application is defined by the environment as much as by the rate. The first questions are concrete: where the palletizer sits, what temperature the area holds during production, and how cases enter and leave the cold area.
Which applications fit subzero palletizing?
The rule of thumb is simple: if the palletizing area operates below 32°F, start with
TRF
Subzero
. Three cold-chain applications fit it:
Frozen food production lines
, including frozen prepared foods and other packaged food operations that palletize below 32°F. See
frozen food and ice cream
.
Ice cream manufacturing
and its end-of-line palletizing.
Meat processing in below-freezing areas (below 32°F).
See
protein processing
, where
TRF
also supplies
sanitary conveyors
and
case conveying
for the same end of line.
Custom Foods, in De Soto, Kansas, runs robotic palletizing below freezing. The
Custom Foods case study
shows the application and the equipment installed.
Is your line ready for a subzero palletizer?
Your line is a strong candidate for
TRF
Subzero
when most of these statements are true:
The palletizing area operates below 32°F, or pallets are built inside or next to a freezer.
Operators build pallets by hand in the cold today.
Rate and case weight can be validated for your application with the end-of-arm tool.
You want fewer operator hours in the coldest part of the plant.
The palletizer has to connect with conveyors and pallet handling that cross the temperature boundary.
To size the application, a Sales Engineer needs the data that defines the cold:
Temperature:
what the area holds during production and during a stop.
Location and boundary:
where the palletizer sits relative to the cold area, and every point where cases or people cross between the cold area and a warmer one.
Product and rate:
case sizes and weights, cases per minute per line and shifts per day.
Pallets:
pallet size, the patterns your customers expect and the number of pallet positions at the cell.
People:
who enters the cell today, how often and for which tasks, such as pallet changes and clearing a stop.
Layout:
a drawing of the cold zone with pallet routes and access.
With these facts,
TRF
validates the product, end-of-arm tool, pallet pattern, reach and process, and confirms the construction, the layout and the scope. A rough drawing and an honest description of the cold area are enough to start.

Can a Robotic Palletizer Operate Below Freezing (32°F)?

Can Palletizing Equipment Operate Below Freezing for Frozen Meat?
Meat processing in below-freezing areas (below 32°F) is one of the target applications of
TRF

How Does a Freezer Palletizing System Work?
A freezer palletizing system takes cases from the packaging line, builds pallets with a robot in a stored pattern and sends finished pallets out of the cold area. The layout and the entries into the cell are defined for the temperature of your area, so operators spend less time in the cold.
Who Manufactures Robotic Palletizers for Frozen or Cold-Chain Production?
TRF
offers

Can a Palletizer Be Integrated With My Existing Conveyor and Wrapping Line?
Yes, when the interfaces are defined in the application study: case infeed rate and orientation, spacing, signals between machines and how full pallets leave the cold area toward wrapping or storage. In a cold-chain plant, the entry and exit points of the cold area are planned with the layout. See
end-of-line integration
for the full-line approach.
What Is the Typical Payback Period for a Robotic Palletizer?
Payback depends on the labor hours the palletizer replaces, shifts per day, local labor cost and the installed cost of the system. In a freezer, manual pallet building is demanding work to staff, and the labor it removes is part of the calculation. A
TRF
Sales Engineer can walk through the estimate with your location, your shifts and the people who palletize by hand today.
What Maintenance Does a Robotic Palletizer Require to Avoid Downtime?
A robotic palletizer needs periodic inspection of the robot, gripper, sensors and conveyors, following the manufacturer's schedule, plus replacement of wear parts before they fail. For
TRF

Parts and Service
covers technical support, spare parts kept in the United States and the
```

```
Tooling
Selected for the tray format, since an open tray offers no packaging surface on top
Crates
Reusable crates are covered in the
crate palletizing section
of this page
Palletizing below 32°F
TRF
Subzero
, the
TRF
palletizer for below-freezing areas (below 32°F)
What is a tray palletizer?
A tray palletizer is the robotic cell that takes loaded trays from a conveyor and builds them into pallet loads. The trays may hold cups, tubs or cans, carry film-wrapped packaged product, or be plastic trays of bakery, dairy or meat items. The robot places each tray where the recipe defines, layer after layer, and the finished load moves to wrapping or into cold storage.
Tray stacking by hand is hard work: wide, shallow units, sometimes wet, often cold, lifted over and over on every shift. That is why a tray palletizing project usually starts as an ergonomics and staffing conversation, and only then becomes a throughput conversation. Trays are palletized in
bakery and snacks
,
dairy and cheese
,
protein processing
and
frozen food and ice cream
plants.
What Makes Trays Hard to Palletize?
No top surface.
An open tray exposes product, not packaging, so the robot has to work from the sides or from underneath.
The product carries the load.
In a stack of open trays, the weight above rests on the tray walls, the corners or the product itself, and poor alignment crushes what is inside.
Shallow units, many layers.
Small placement errors accumulate up the stack.
Slippery, cold and wet.
Film-wrapped trays slide on each other, and dairy, protein and frozen lines add condensation and cleaning routines.
How does a robot grip an open tray without touching the product?
By working from the base or the sides. Tooling that slides under the tray carries it from below, while clamp or rim tooling holds the tray walls or lip. A film-wrapped tray with a continuous film surface can sometimes be handled from above. The tooling is selected for your tray format and validated with your trays, loaded as they run and at the temperature they reach the palletizer.
Tooling that supports or clamps trays can weigh more than a simple pick head, and it counts against the robot payload together with the loaded tray. A tray that flexes when full also behaves differently from the empty sample that usually arrives first, which is why validation uses production trays.
Keeping the Tray Stack Stable
Align the load path.
Trays stack most securely when walls and corners line up, so the weight travels down the structure rather than through the product.
Use tier sheets where the stack needs a floor.
A sheet over a layer of open trays spreads load and gives the next layer a flat surface.
Fight the slide.
Layer sheets, pattern choice and wrap tension keep film-wrapped trays square.
Respect the pallet height.
Cooler and freezer racking, truck height and how trays behave under load set the number of layers.
What is a crate palletizer?
A crate palletizer stacks rigid reusable crates, the open containers that bakery, dairy and protein operations use to move product between plants, distribution centers and stores. Crates and trays often appear in the same plant, and even on the same line, which is why crate palletizing is covered on this page.
Crates change the problem in three ways:
They are designed to stack.
Feet, rims or interlocking features seat one crate into the one below, so column stacking is the natural pattern and placement accuracy matters more than pattern complexity.
They are rigid.
A crate carries the load of the crates above it, which protects the product inside.
They give the robot something to hold.
Rims, handles and strong sidewalls suit tooling that clamps the walls or hooks the rim.
Crate applications are screened the same way as trays, by rate, loaded crate weight, number of formats, floor space and the room the cell will work in, and validated per application. Where a crate application needs handling the standard platforms do not cover, it is evaluated as a
custom palletizing project
.
How do you choose a tray palletizer?
Choose by line setup and speed: trays per minute at peak, including bursts after an oven, a cooler or a packing machine, how many lines feed the palletizer and the room it will work in. The loaded weight of the heaviest format, plus the tooling, then confirms the choice.
Platform

Case weight
Best fit
TRF

About 1 to 10 cases per minute

TRF
Compact

TRF

Custom palletizer

Defined by the project
```
